Laboratory Preparation of Oxygen 3. Electrolysis of Water. If an electric current of low voltage is passed through water which has been made slightly acid with sulphuric acid, bubbles of oxygen are given off at the anode (positive electrode where the current enters the solution) and hydrogen is given off at the cathode (negative electrode where the current leaves the solution). When an appreciable amount of the gases collects, it will be seen that the volume of the gases will be in the ratio of 2 volumes of hydrogen to 1 of oxygen. The sulphuric acid acts as a catalytic agent. The chemical change taking place is: 2H2O 

2 vols. water 

2H2 + O2 

2 vols. hydrogen + 1 vol. oxygen

Commercial Preparation. For commercial use, oxygen is prepared by the electrolysis of water and by the liquefaction of air. 

In the latter process, the nitrogen in the liquid air boils off first, leaving the oxygen.

Physical Properties. Oxygen, as you can detect from its presence in the air, is a colorless, odorless gas. It is a little heavier than air and, since it can be collected by displacement of water, is but slightly soluble in water (ratio of solubility is 1 volume of oxygen to 25 volumes of water).

HYDROGEN 

CATHODE ANODE BATTERY 

Electrolysis of Water

Chemical Properties. Oxygen is very active in its combination with other substances. A splinter which glows in air burns with intense action in pure oxygen. A piece of iron left in moist air may become covered with rust, but in a bottle of oxygen an iron wire will burn with great rapidity. Likewise, sulphur burns in oxygen with a blue flame, forming a suffocating gas, sulphur dioxide. 

Phosphorus gives a brilliant yellow flame, forming a white solid, phosphorus pentoxide.

OXYGEN AND OZONE 

9 Uses of Oxygen. Oxygen is necessary to sustain life. Human beings and animals need the oxygen in the air for respiration. 

Sometimes pure oxygen is used for artificial respiration, as in the following cases: treatment of pneumonia, asphyxiation from gas, and drowning; oxygen helmets for aviators; oxygen tanks in submarines. Oxygen is also useful for the purification of water by aëration and in the decay of waste matter. Commercially, oxygen is used to create extremely high temperatures, as in the case of oxy-acetylene and oxy-hydrogen blow-torches (see page 17). 

OXIDES AND OXIDATION 

Oxides. An oxide is a compound composed of oxygen and another element. Many oxides are formed when substances burn in oxygen: C+ O2

Oxides of non-metallic elements dissolve in water to form acids. 

Oxidation. A chemical change in which oxygen unites with a substance is called oxidation. Rusting of iron is due to slow oxidation without perceptible heat. Rapid oxidation generates heat and light, as in the burning of a candle.

Combustion. Combustion is a chemical action in which heat and light are given off. Most cases of combustion take place in the form of rapid oxidation. Hydrogen, however, will burn in a bottle of chlorine gas to form hydrogen chloride. To produce ordinary combustion there must be a substance to burn or be combustible, an agent to furnish the oxygen, and lastly the substance must be heated to a point where combustion will begin. The temperature at which combustion begins is called the kindling temperature. When a match is rubbed on a rough surface, the heat created by friction will kindle the head of the match. In turn, this heat raises the wood of the stick to its kindling point.

Bunsen Burner. In the laboratory, heat is needed to produce and control many chemical reactions. The Bunsen burner consists of a cast iron base, through which a tube for the gas runs. This tube terminates in a small aperture called the spud. The spud opens into the barrel, which has two openings just above the spud. The size of these openings is regulated by a sleeve around the barrel. Illuminating gas, the fuel used in a Bunsen burner, contains two combustible elements, carbon and hydrogen. If the holes in the base of the burner are closed, the gas will burn with a

Nature of the Bunsen Flame. A flame is a burning gas. If we study the Bunsen flame (see diagram), we note that there are three separate zones. At the bottom, near the mouth of the burner, is unburned gas which has reached its kindling temperature. To

test this, we insert one end of a glass tube into this inner cone and ignite the gas issuing from the other end of the tube. The middle cone is the part where the gas starts to burn. The heat generated reaches its peak at the top and just above this cone. Because of the need for oxygen at this point, this part of the flame is called the reducing part-it takes oxygen from substances. The luminous top portion of the flame is the oxidizing part-the excess oxygen at this point combines with other substances. 

Spontaneous Combustion. If oily rags are left in a poorly ventilated place, the oxidation taking place will generate enough heat to raise the rags to the kindling point, so that they will begin to smoulder. This is called spontaneous combustion. 

Explosive Mixtures. Explosions are usually caused by the chemical combination of combustible gases or dust with air. When the mixture is ignited, an instantaneous flame is produced throughout the mass as a result of the exposure of large surfaces of the combustible material to oxygen. As a result of the intense heat, the gases that are formed undergo a tremendous expansion and thus produce a high pressure. If this high pressure is confined, an explosion results. Dust in coal mines and flour mills may be exploded by a spark from machines, causing destruction of property. 

Gasoline vapor and air in the cylinders of automobiles explode to furnish the power to the pistons of the engines. 

Oxygen and Living Things. Oxidation of the tissues in all plants and animals produces heat and energy for growth and locomotion. In warm-blooded animals, the oxygen is taken from the air by the lungs and carried by the blood to the tissues of the body, where oxidation takes place. The water vapor and carbon dioxide produced is in turn expelled by the lungs. Plants absorb carbon dioxide from the air. The carbon dioxide, when combined with water, is converted into starch in the green leaves (photosynthesis). Animals in turn are dependent on plants for starchy foods. This constitutes the carbon dioxide-oxygen cycle (see page 139).

OXYGEN AND OZONE 

13 

OZONE 

Allotropism. When the same element exists in two or more forms, it is said to be allotropic. Allotropic forms have different chemical and physical properties and energy contents. Ozone is an allotropic form of oxygen. 

Preparation. Ozone is prepared by discharging electric sparks of high voltage through oxygen. 302 →203 

3 vols. Oxygen 2 vols. Ozone

Properties and Uses. Ozone is a light-blue gas with a pungent odor. This odor can be noticed in the vicinity of high voltage electrical devices. Ozone is very unstable, changing back to oxygen readily. When this change takes place, energy is released. 

Ozone is much more active than oxygen, speeding up oxidation considerably. As a result, it is used as a bleaching agent and deodorant. It is interesting to note, in connection with the bleaching properties of ozone, that the air near the seashore, which contains traces of ozone, bleaches linens more rapidly.

CHAPTER HYDROGEN 

Distribution. Hydrogen is not found free in air as the abundant oxygen is found; it is so light that it will escape into the upper layers of the atmosphere. In compounds, it makes up 11% of water by weight and, united with carbon, forms many hydrocarbons such as the important fuels, gasoline and kerosene. Water gas contains free hydrogen. Hydrogen is also found in natural gas as part of the compound, methane. Many of our foods, such as starches, sugars and fats, contain hydrogen.

Hydrogen was named "water-former" by Lavoisier because it burns in air to form water.

Preparation 

1. Replacement of Hydrogen of an Acid with a Metal. Hydrogen was first produced by Cavendish (1766) by this method. The materials generally used are granulated zinc with dilute hydrochloric acid. The acid is poured through the thistle tube upon the 15

zinc. Hydrogen is generated and drives out the air. The gas should first be tested for purity before it is collected (a mixture explodes, while the pure gas burns quietly). The pure gas is collected in bottles over water and, since the gas is lighter than air, the filled bottles are kept inverted. Zn + 2HC1 -

ZnCl2 + H2 ↑ 

Zinc Chloride + Hydrogen

The presence of the zinc chloride is shown by evaporating the liquid left in the generator, leaving white zinc chloride crystals. 

2. Decomposition of Water by Metals. Certain metals, such as potassium and sodium (calcium and iron to a lesser degree), will combine directly with water, liberating part of the hydrogen in the water. If a test tube filled with water is inverted in a shallow dish of water and a small pellet of potassium is inserted under the tube, the potassium will react with the water, forming potassium hydroxide and hydrogen. 2H2O + 2K 

Water Potassium 2KOH + H2 ↑ 

→Potassium Hydroxide + Hydrogen 

The hydrogen will collect at the top of the test tube and will give its characteristic test (a sharp "bark" or explosion) when brought near a flame. The soapy feeling of the liquid in the dish and its action on red litmus paper proves the presence of a hydroxide. The action is much slower with metallic calcium; and iron must be finely divided and strongly heated in an ignition tube while steam is passed through it in order to decompose the water. Physical Properties. Hydrogen is a colorless, odorless gas, only slightly soluble in water. Its most important property is its density, hydrogen being only 1/14 as heavy as air. A soap bubble filled with hydrogen will rise quickly to the ceiling. 

Chemical Properties. Hydrogen burns in air with an intensely hot flame to form water but a splint will not burn in the hydrogen itself (i.e., hydrogen burns but does not support combustion).

Hydrogen + Oxygen

Hydrogen has a strong tendency to unite with oxygen. This property is seen when hydrogen comes into contact with heated oxides. Oxygen is removed from the oxide, leaving the metal. If hydrogen (free from water vapor) is passed through a tube containing heated copper oxide, the black oxide will turn into metallic copper, and water vapor is condensed on the funnel at the outlet.

HYDROGEN AND COPPER OXIDE YIELDS COPPER AND WATER 

Reduction of Copper Oxide

When a substance, such as hydrogen, takes oxygen away from an oxide, that substance is called a reducing agent. Since the oxide furnished the oxygen, it is called an oxidizing agent. The processes of reduction and oxidation take place simultaneously. Copper oxide was reduced by the hydrogen, and the hydrogen was oxidized by the copper oxide.

Uses. When hydrogen is mixed in the right proportions with oxygen in the oxy-hydrogen blowpipe, the flame produced by the

19 burning mixture reaches a temperature of 2000° C. The oxyhydrogen blowpipe is used to melt platinum and other metals. 

OXYGEN HYDROGEN 

Oxy-hydrogen Blowtorch

Since hydrogen is much lighter than air, balloons inflated with hydrogen rise quickly in air. Small balloons thus inflated are used to test wind direction at high levels (a knowledge of wind direction is very useful in aviation). Hydrogen is also used to inflate and lift dirigibles or Zeppelins. Since this light gas is highly inflammable in air, however, it is a constant source of danger. Consequently, the inert gas, helium (found in natural gas wells), is used in dirigibles in the United States, in spite of the fact that its lifting power is only ninety per cent that of hydrogen. 

Hydrogen is now used in several synthetic chemical processes. 

Nitrogen and hydrogen can be combined to form synthetic ammonia. Liquid oils, such as cottonseed oil, are changed to solid fats by a combination of the liquid oil with hydrogen in the presence of a catalytic agent (finely divided nickel). Synthetic gasoline can be made by combining hydrogen with heavier oils to increase their hydrogen content and lighten their specific gravity. 

Note. See page 69 for a discussion of the recent discovery of "heavy" hydrogen.

CHAPTER WATER SOLUTIONS, PURIFICATION 

Distribution. Water is undoubtedly the most widely distributed substance on the earth's surface. Three-quarters of the earth's surface is covered by the oceans and lakes. All plant and animal matter contains water. Water is in the air and locked up in crystals of rocks. Water is an oxide of hydrogen. [་ 

BOILING POINT OF WATER MELTING POINT OF 

ICE 100°C (212°F) Oc (32°F) ICE 

Physical Constants of Water

Physical Properties of Water. Clear spring or lake water is generally free from harmful substances but contains dissolved mineral matter which gives it a distinct taste. Pure water is tasteless, odorless and colorless unless in thick layers. Cooled to 0° C, water becomes ice, and when raised to 100° C boils away into steam. 21.

States of Matter. Water is a good illustration of the three forms in which matter is known. Ice is a solid which is characterized by definite volume and shape. Melted ice becomes a liquid with definite volume which will assume the shape of the containing vessel. Steam is a gas which has no definite shape or volume. 

Solution. In the laboratory, water is used to make solutions of solids, liquids and gases. Sodium hydroxide solution contains the solid, sodium hydroxide, dissolved in water. Sulphuric acid solution contains a dissolved liquid, while hydrochloric acid solution contains a dissolved gas. 

When a lump of sugar is placed in water the crystals vanish and the entire mass of water tastes sweet. The particles of sugar are mixed uniformly with the water. More sugar may be added and the solution will be sweeter. A solution of sugar and water may have varying degrees of sweetness.

A solution is a uniform mixture which has no definite composition. 

The liquid used for dissolving is called the solvent. The substance dissolved by the solvent is called the solute. It is important to study solutions because they are the media enabling many substances to react with each other (see Chapter 8, Ionization Theory). 

Suspension. A suspension is a mixture of two or more substances which separate upon standing. Muddy water, dust and air, starch and water are examples of suspension. The difference between a suspension and a solution is made clear by the following table:

COMPARISON OF SOLUTION AND SUSPENSION 

Particles in Solution 

Particles in Suspension Fine 

Coarse (by comparison)

Uniformly distributed in solvent Usually not uniformly distributed in solvent 

Will not settle out 

Will settle out 

Can be filtered

Cloudy 

Cannot be filtered

Clear Miscible and Non-Miscible Liquids. Liquids which will mix with each other and not separate into layers are called miscible. 

Alcohol and water are miscible, but oil and water will separate into distinct layers, and consequently are called non-miscible.

WATER 

23 Dilute and Concentrated Solutions. Since solutions vary as to the amount of solvent and solute, the term dilute is used if the amount of solute is small in proportion to the solvent; the term concentrated is used if the amount of solute is large. If a pinch of table salt is placed in a beaker of water the solution is said to be dilute. However, if more salt is added the solution becomes concentrated until a point is reached where no more salt can be dissolved. 

Conditions Affecting Solubility. Three factors that affect solubility are: the nature of the solvent and the solute, the temperature, and the pressure. Iodine is quite insoluble in water but very soluble in alcohol, forming tincture of iodine. Oil and grease stains are removed by gasoline since they are soluble in it. 

Most solids are more soluble in liquids at higher temperatures. 

The extent of solubility may vary greatly. Calcium chloride is very soluble at higher temperatures, while sodium chloride is nearly as soluble in hot as in cold water. However, calcium hydroxide is more soluble in cold than in hot water. 

Gases are more soluble in water (1) as the temperature is decreased and (2) as the pressure is increased. Carbon dioxide gas bubbles out of a bottle of carbonated water as the cap is removed, releasing the pressure.

Saturation. A saturated solution is one in which no more solute can be dissolved under a fixed set of conditions of temperature and pressure. As long as more solute can be dissolved under the given set of conditions, the solution is not saturated. However, a solution saturated with respect to one solute may still dissolve another substance (see page 174 for application of this property to the purification of salt).

Supersaturation. In our definition of a saturated solution we mentioned "fixed set of conditions." Two of these conditions, temperature and pressure, as we know, affect definitely the solubility of substances. It is possible, by altering the conditions, to produce a supersaturated solution, or a solution containing more dissolved solute than it would ordinarily have at a fixed temperature and pressure. 

A saturated solution of "hypo," sodium thiosulphate, is made in boiling water and allowed to cool slowly. We know that an increase in temperature increases the solubility of "hypo," yet when

the temperature is thus reduced, the "hypo" fails to precipitate out of the solution. The solution is now supersaturated because more "hypo" is dissolved than would ordinarily be the case at the lower temperature. The instability of supersaturated solutions can be shown in this instance by adding a small crystal of "hypo" to the solution. All the excess "hypo" precipitates, liberating heat and leaving the solution saturated. The precipitate is in the form of crystals.

Crystallization is used to purify and recover many substances from solution. Crude salt from sea water is obtained by utilizing the heat of the sun to evaporate the liquid in shallow basins until the crystals of sodium chloride crystallize out (see page 174). 

Crystallization. Crystals are the regular geometrical forms which substances sometimes take when they become solids. The shape of the crystals of a substance is one of the physical properties of that substance which helps identify it. Crystals are formed from solutions by evaporating the solvent or by altering the conditions affecting solubility. They may also be formed by sublimation (see iodine, page 93) or by cooling from the liquid state (see sulphur, page 103).

Water of Crystallization. Water of crystallization is the water that combines chemically with a substance to give it crystalline form. A substance which contains water of crystallization is known as a hydrate. When hydrates are heated, water is given off and the crystalline form is destroyed. "Hypo" contains enough water of crystallization to dissolve a quantity of it. If a number of "hypo" crystals are placed in a test tube and heated, the water thus driven out of the hydrate will dissolve the remaining noncrystalline solid. Some crystals, however, such as common salt and sugar, contain no water of crystallization. These are called anhydrous (without water). 

Efflorescence. On exposure to the air some crystals, such as copper sulphate or sodium carbonate, lose their characteristic shape and crumble into a powder. Crystals which give up their water of crystallization upon exposure to the air are called efflorescent.

Deliquescence. Sodium hydroxide and calcium chloride absorb enough water from the air to become dissolved. This property of absorbing water is called deliquescence. Deliquescent substances

are sometimes called dehydrating agents ("take out" water). A hygroscopic substance is one which absorbs water from the air but does not dissolve. Wool is an example of this. 

Purification. Water from streams, lakes and reservoirs contains dissolved minerals, such as lime and magnesium bicarbonate, suspended matter, such as soil, which will tend to settle out, and waste organic matter, such as decayed vegetation, which may be a source of disease germs. Typhoid fever and intestinal disorders have their source in contaminated water. 

Water suitable for domestic use must be clear, reasonably soft (see page 183) and free from harmful bacteria. Water may be purified by aëration, filtration, chlorination, coagulation and distillation. Distillation is the only method of purification which will remove dissolved impurities. Boiling sterilizes the water by destroying bacteria, but this method is too expensive for largescale purification. Copper sulphate is sometimes added to water to kill the minute plants, algae, which give it a bitter taste. 

1. Aëration. This consists of spraying the water into the air or allowing it to run in thin sheets over an incline to bring it into contact with the oxygen in the air and with the sunlight. Oxygen destroys the organic matter in water. Sunlight includes ultraviolet, or short, waves which destroy bacteria. These ultra-violet rays may be produced artificially in the mercury vapor arc, which is sometimes used to purify the water of swimming pools. 

2. Filtration. In city systems, the water is allowed to pass through beds of sand which filter out the suspended matter in water and some of the bacteria. Small filters can be made in the home. These consist of earthenware flower pots filled with sand and charcoal. The charcoal removes objectionable gases. Home filters have the disadvantage of needing frequent cleaning to be effective. 

3. Chlorination. Most city water systems now depend upon liquid chlorine to destroy harmful bacteria. The amount of chlorine added to the water is regulated by taking a bacterial count every day. The water usually is first filtered and then chlorinated. 

4. Coagulation. Solid particles in water may be removed by adding aluminum sulphate and lime. A precipitate is formed which carries down with it most of the suspended matter.

WATER 27 Purifying Water by Distillation. Pure water from the faucet contains gases from the air and traces of minerals which the water has dissolved from the rocks or soils as it flowed over them. The 

4% or 5% of mineral matter in sea water came from the fresh waters of the rivers, and became concentrated through long evaporation. To separate water from mineral matter and dissolved gases by distillation it is necessary first to change the water to a vapor by heating and then to cool the vapor until it condenses into a liquid. For example, ordinary tap water will show traces of

Distillation of Water chlorides (it becomes cloudy when silver nitrate is added). When blue copper sulphate is dissolved in tap water and the solution distilled (see diagram) the distilled water shows no trace of copper sulphate or natural chlorides. The copper sulphate or chlorides do not vaporize but remain in the still. 

Fractional Distillation. A mixture of several liquids may be separated by distillation when the liquids have different boiling points. This is called fractional distillation and necessitates boiling the mixture carefully so that the liquid fraction with the lowest boiling point will boil off first, the fraction with the next lowest boiling point second, and so on. When alcohol and water are mixed in a flask and boiled, the alcohol, which vaporizes more readily than water, distills first. The distillate contains a high percentage of alcohol and some water. If the distillate is again distilled, a

higher concentration of alcohol is obtained. Additional distillations result in greater purity. 

Vacuum Distillation. In cases where a high temperature is not desirable, vacuum distillation is used. This consists in lowering the pressure over the solution thereby lowering its boiling point (the liquid molecules become vapor molecules more easily under decreased pressure).

WATER AS A COMPOUND 

Water is an oxide of hydrogen. As the most abundant chemical compound its properties are important. 

Chemical Properties of Water 

1. Stability. Water possesses great stability. Since hydrogen and oxygen liberate a great amount of heat when they combine, the same amount of energy must be expended to separate them. 

However, high temperatures will not decompose water to any extent. It may be easily decomposed by the process of electrolysis (see page 7). 

2. Action with Active Metals. The active metals, potassium, sodium, and calcium, decompose water to form a hydroxide and hydrogen.

Chemical action is promoted in water solution as the individual molecules of the dissolved substance come in contact with each other. Baking powder does not generate carbon dioxide until water is added. 

7. Water as a Catalyst. Water acts as a catalytic agent in many chemical reactions. Phosphorus does not burn in dry air, but burns readily in damp air.

Synthesis of Water by Volume 

Composition of Water. The composition of water is determined by the following two methods: 

1. Analysis. Analysis is the chemical process of separating a compound into its elements. When an electric current is passed through water containing dilute sulphuric acid the water is separated into two volumes of hydrogen and one volume of oxygen (see electrolysis of water, page 7). 

2. Synthesis. Synthesis is the chemical process of forming a compound by the direct union of its elements or simpler com-

pounds. To measure the composition of water or any compound by synthesis two methods of computation may be used: (a) Computation by Volume. The volumes of hydrogen and oxygen which will combine to form water may be measured in an apparatus called a eudiometer. This graduated tube has a pair of platinum wires near the closed end to form a spark gap. The tube is filled with mercury and inverted in a dish of mercury. If 20cc. of oxygen and 20cc. of hydrogen are introduced into the tube and the mixture exploded by an electric spark, 10cc. of oxygen and a film of water will remain. The 20cc. of hydrogen combined with 

10cc. of oxygen. The composition of water by volume is in the ratio of 2 volumes of hydrogen to 1 of oxygen.

Law of Definite Proportions. Just as the ratio of hydrogen to oxygen in water is always found to be constant, the synthesis of other compounds shows similar results. If fine copper wire is heated in a covered crucible with powdered sulphur, the two elements always combine to form copper sulphide in the ratio of weight of 2 parts of copper to 1 part sulphur.

The above results lead to the following general statement or law. The Law of Definite Proportions states that when elements combine to form compounds they do so in a fixed ratio by weight. 

HYDROGEN PEROXIDE 

Another familiar compound of hydrogen and oxygen is called hydrogen peroxide (commonly just "peroxide"), which contains twice as much oxygen as water does. 

Preparation. Hydrogen peroxide can be prepared by treating sodium peroxide with dilute sulphuric acid.

Na2O2 + H2SO4 

Sodium peroxide + sulphuric acid

Na2SO4 + H2O2 

Sodium sulphate + hydrogen peroxide 

Physical Properties. Pure hydrogen peroxide resembles a colorless, syrupy liquid. It is one and one-half times heavier than water. It is bitter to the taste. 

Chemical Properties. Hydrogen peroxide is very unstable; breaking up into water and oxygen at ordinary temperatures. In its pure state, its unstable nature makes it explosive. It is usually sold in a water solution containing about 3% hydrogen peroxide. 

Brown bottles are used to prevent decomposition by light. 

When hydrogen peroxide reacts with substances, it gives up its oxygen as an oxidizing agent and is itself reduced to water. This is another instance of how oxidation and reduction take place simultaneously. H2O2

WATER 

3333 Uses. When hydrogen peroxide decomposes, it releases nascent oxygen. Nascent oxygen, which is oxygen at the instant of its liberation from a compound, is very active. Thus, hydrogen peroxide is a very useful oxidizing and bleaching agent. It is a mild antiseptic and wash for wounds (oxygen destroys bacteria). 

Hair, wool and silk can be safely bleached by hydrogen peroxide because its final product is water. 

Law of Multiple Proportions. Hydrogen and oxygen unite to form several different compounds. The weight ratio of hydrogen to oxygen in water is approximately 1:8; in hydrogen peroxide it is 

1:16. Thus it will be noticed that while the weight of hydrogen is the same in each compound, the weight of the oxygen is doubled in the hydrogen peroxide. The Law of Multiple Proportions states that when two elements form more than one compound, if the weight of the first element is considered constant, or fixed, the weights of the second element are in the simple ratio of whole small numbers to each other.

CHAPTER  

Early Concepts of the Atom. As far back as Grecian times, and even earlier, scholars conceived of matter as composed of infinitesimally small particles, atoms. It is interesting to note that many of our latest discoveries were prophesied and guessed at by the ancients. In marvelling at their foresight, however, we should bear in mind that for every good guess they made, there were thousands of incorrect ones. Such is the difference between the scientific method of today, which bases its theories or "guesses" upon previous experiments and known facts, and the unscientific method of the ancients. 

Dalton's Atomic Theory. In 1803, John Dalton, a poor English school-teacher, propounded the following theory of the composition of matter. 

1. All matter consists of infinitesimally small, indivisible particles called atoms. 

2. Atoms of the same element are all exactly alike in every respect: in size, in shape, and in weight; but differ from atoms of every other element. 

3. Chemical change takes place between atoms, which act as individual units in the change.

Law of Definite Proportions. This law states (see page 31) that every compound has a definite composition by weight. 

Dalton's atomic theory explains why this is so. We know that each atom of the same element is exactly the same in weight as any other atom of that element. For example, each atom of mercury in a container weighs exactly the same as the next and similarly each atom of oxygen weighs exactly the same as the next. In a chemical change, one atom of mercury (atomic weight-200.6) will unite with one atom of oxygen (atomic weight-16) to form the compound, mercuric oxide (200.6 16 216.6). Since (1) atoms do not divide or break up in a chemical change, and (2) since atoms of the same element all have the same weight, and (3) 36

since matter cannot be destroyed, we see that a compound (which is a fixed combination of a definite number of atoms of two or more elements) must have a definite composition by weight. 

Law of Multiple Proportions. This law states that when two elements form more than one compound, if the weight of the first element is considered constant, or fixed, the weights of the second element are in the simple ratio of small whole numbers to each other. Water is a compound composed, as you know, of two atoms of hydrogen combined with one atom of oxygen. The hydrogen atom has an atomic weight of 1, and the oxygen atom an atomic weight of 16. The weight of one molecule of water is, therefore, hydrogen 2, oxygen 16, and the weight ratio of the two elements in the compound is 1:8.

Hydrogen peroxide is composed of two atoms of hydrogen and two atoms of oxygen. Its molecular weight consists of hydrogen 2, oxygen 32, and the weight ratio of the two elements is 1:16. If we apply the law of multiple proportions to water and hydrogen peroxide, we notice that the weights of oxygen in the two compounds are 16 and 32 and that the ratio between these two weights is expressed in small whole numbers: 1:2. Dalton, when he propounded the law of multiple proportions, explained it in the light of his atomic theory. If the weight of the first element is considered constant, weights of the second element are in the simple ratio of small whole numbers to each other because the differences in weight are due to the addition or subtraction of whole atoms of the second element. Since each whole atom of the second element weighs exactly the same as the next, and since these atoms do not break up or divide in a chemical change, the ratio of the different weights of the second element is the same as the ratio of the number of atoms of that element, or a ratio of small whole numbers. 

Definition of an Atom. An atom is the smallest unit of matter which acts in a chemical change. 

Symbols. A symbol is merely a short way to write the name of an element. There is an individual symbol for each of the ninetytwo elements. These symbols are generally abbreviations of the 

Latin names for the elements. Each symbol represents one atom of the element and the atomic weight of that atom. O stands for one atom of oxygen and its atomic weight, 16.

Atomic Weight. Atoms are much too small to be seen, much less weighed. Consequently, atomic weights are not expressed in absolute terms like pounds or grams, but are expressed in relative terms, with oxygen as the standard because of its great activity. 

The atomic weight given to oxygen is 16. All other atomic weights are computed with the weight of oxygen as the base. The atomic weights of the common elements are listed in the Appendix. ATOM

Definition of a Molecule. A molecule is the smallest particle of an element or a compound that still retains the properties of that element or compound. A molecule of an element may consist of one or more atoms of the element. (A helium molecule consists of one atom; a hydrogen molecule consists of two atoms). A molecule

of a compound always consists of two or more atoms (a carbon dioxide molecule is composed of one carbon and two oxygen atoms). 

Formulas. A formula consists of one or more symbols and is used to indicate the composition of molecules. By looking at a formula, we know: 

1. The elements in the molecule (represented by symbols: H2O). 

2. The number of atoms of each element (represented by subscript numbers: H2). 

3. The weight of the molecule (by adding up the atomic weights of each element).

Molecular Weight. The weight of a molecule of an element or compound is the sum of the weights of all the atoms in the molecule.

The molecule H2 is composed of two atoms of the element. hydrogen, each having an atomic weight of 1 (exact weight is 

1.008). Therefore the molecular weight of H2 is 1 plus 1, or 2 (exact weight is 2.016).

The molecule H2O is composed of two atoms of the element hydrogen, each with an atomic weight of 1; and one atom of oxygen, with an atomic weight of 16. The molecular weight of H2O is 

1 plus 1 plus 16, or a total of 18.

The gram-molecular weight of a substance is the number of grams equal to the molecular weight of that substance. For example, the gram-molecular weight of O2 is 32 grams and of H2O is 

18.016 grams. Valence 1. Definition. A knowledge of valence is essential to the writing of formulas. The valence of any element is a number equal to the number of hydrogen atoms with which one atom of that element can combine, or which it can replace. Hydrogen is used as a basis or standard because one atom of hydrogen never combines with more than one atom of any other element. Elements with a valence of 1 are monovalent; 2-divalent; 3-trivalent. 

2. Valence of Radicals. A radical is a group of atoms which act as a single unit in a chemical change. Radicals have a definite valence. For example, the valence of SO4 is 2. 

3. Elements with More than One Valence. Some elements have more than one valence. For example, iron has a valence of 2 and 

3, nitrogen a valence of 3 and 5, and sulphur a valence of 2, 4, and 6.

Valence and Formulas. The valences of all important elements and radicals should be memorized. In writing formulas use the following procedure as a guide: 

1. First write the metallic element of the formula and then the non-metallic element or radical, thus: in NaCl, Na (metallic), Cl (non-metallic), and in ZnSo4, Zn (metallic) and SO4 (non-metallic). 

2. Indicate the valence of the metallic element by a number of + signs equal to its valence. Thus monovalent H is written H+, divalent Ca is written Ca++, trivalent Fe is written Fe+++. Indicate the valence of the non-metallic element or radical by a number of signs equal to the valence. Thus, monovalent Cl is written 

Cl-, divalent O is written 0--, divalent SO4 is written SO4——. 

3. Since the metallic and non-metallic valences of a formula must equal each other, add a subscript to the metallic element equal to the valence of the non-metallic element or radical, and add a subscript to the non-metallic element or radical equal to the valence of the metallic element, thus Zn++SO4 (already equal, no subscripts necessary), Na2SO4, Fe2+++03. Note that where the subscript is 1, it can be omitted, as in Na+Cl-. 

Equations. A chemical equation is a statement expressed in formulas representing a chemical change that has actually taken place. It shows all the substances which took part in the changeon the left side of the equation the reacting substances and on the right side the yielded or resulting substances. The two sides of the equation are separated by an arrow which represents "yields" or "gives." 

CuO+H2 H2O + Cu 

Balancing Equations. Since the Law of Conservation of Mat ter (see page 4) states that matter cannot be created nor destroyed and since the atomic theory states that atoms act as in-. divisible units in a chemical change, we know that exactly the same number of atoms of each element involved in the change must appear on both sides of the equation. The process of making the number of atoms of each element exactly the same for both sides of the equation is known as balancing the equation and is accompplished by placing the correct figures (coefficients) before the symbols or formulas in the equation. Helps in balancing equations are as follows: 

1. Elements such as hydrogen, oxygen and chlorine in their

free state occur as molecules. When writing equations, these elements are written as molecules, thus: H2 + Cl2 → 2HC1 2H2 + O2 → 2H2O 2. Since radicals usually act as elements in a chemical change, they can be written without change on the right side of the equation. Thus, the SO4 radical is transferred without change to the right.

H2SO4 + Zn →→ ZnSO4 + H2 ↑ 3. Check the number of atoms of each element on the left side of the equation with the number of atoms of the same element on the right side and be sure that they equal each other. When adding coefficients before a formula to balance one element be careful of unbalancing other elements in the formula without making the necessary adjustments in the equation. Be sure, also, not to change the subscripts in any formula because that would change the composition of that substance. Unbalanced

CHAPTER  

Need for Exactness. Chemistry is an exact science in which the analytical chemist determines with extreme accuracy the weights and volumes of substances which enter into reactions. Through research, new compounds are discovered and new processes are developed as a result of which useful materials are produced for the benefit of all mankind. Every large manufacturing organization maintains a staff of chemists who are specialists in the particular field of their company's products. Upon them depend the reputation of their firm and its ability to establish and keep at recognized standards the particular goods which they market. 

Standards of Measurement. The analytical chemist who works with small samples finds the metric system best suited for his measurements. The units of measure commonly used in the laboratory are: Weight 10 milligrams 

10 centigrams 10 decigrams 

1 centigram 1 decigram 1 gram 1,000 grams = 1 kilogram = 2.2 lbs.

Volume 1 liter 

1,000 cubic centimeters

Measurement of Temperature. Temperature is measured by means of thermometers, of which there are three types: Fahrenheit, Centigrade, and Absolute. The Absolute temperature scale was devised to show relative changes in volumes of gases when there is a temperature change (see below, Charles' Law). Thermometer readings are converted from one scale to the other as follows. 

1. From Centigrade to Fahrenheit: Multiply Centigrade reading by 1.8 and add 32. 

2. From Fahrenheit to Centigrade: Subtract 32 from Fahrenheit reading and divide by 1.8. 45

995 

3. From Centigrade to Absolute: Add 273 degrees algebraically to Centigrade reading.

MEASUREMENT OF GAS VOLUMES 

Changes in Gas Volumes. Since gases are difficult to weigh directly, chemists are required to measure the volumes of gases evolved in reactions. Gas-measuring tubes are called eudiometers. 

Volumes of gases are affected by the pressure of the atmosphere and the temperature of the room. In order that all conditions may be alike, volumes of gases are reduced to standard conditions of pressure and temperature. Standard pressure = Barometer reading 760 mm. of mercury. Standard temperature = 0° Centigrade.

Boyle's Law. Robert Boyle, an Irish physicist, studied the behavior of gases under pressure and found that if the temperature is constant, as the pressure increased on a confined gas its volume decreased and as the pressure decreased the volume increased. If a gas has a volume of 50 cc. under a pressure of 15 lbs. to a square inch (atmospheric pressure), and the pressure is doubled to 30 lbs. per square inch with the temperature remaining constant, the volume becomes one half (25 cc.) If the pressure is tripled, the volume is reduced to one-third.

Boyle's Law states that if the temperature is constant the volume of a gas varies inversely as the pressure upon it. 

Type Problem: To Correct for a Change in Pressure. To find the new volume of a gas when there has been a change in pressure upon it, one of two methods is used. 

Example: Given the volume of a gas as 200 cc. at 750 mm. pressure; find the new volume (a) at 740 mm. pressure (b) at standare pressure (760 mm.). 

1. Make an inverse proportion between the volumes and the pressures.

V:V' = P' :P 

200:V' = 740:750 = 200 X 750 740 V' V' 

202.7 cc, ans. to (a)

2. Form a fraction of the pressures with the greater pressure as the numerator when the pressure is decreased, and the greater pressure as the denominator when the pressure is increased. Multiply the given volume by this fraction. 200 cc. X 750 760 

197.3 cc, ans. to (b)

Charles' Law. Gases expand when heated and contract when cooled. The increase of volume for 1° Centigrade is called the coefficient of cubical expansion. All gases have the same coefficient of expansion. Charles, a French chemist, found the amount of expansion to be 1/273 of the original volume at 0° C. If a gas is heated to 273° C. it would have twice the volume that it had at 0° C.; if cooled to -273° C. it would have no volume. Actually all gases liquefy before such a temperature is approached. 

If a gas has a definite volume at 0° C., at 1° Centigrade its new 273 +1 of its former volume; at -1° C. its volume volume is would be 273 273 1 273 of its previous volume. 

Charles' Law states that, when the pressure remains constant the volume of a gas varies directly as the absolute temperature. 

Type Problem: To Correct for a Change in Temperature. Two methods are used to find the change in volume which has taken place when the temperature has changed or to reduce a given volume of a gas to standard temperature conditions. 

Example: The volume of a gas is 1500 cc. at 30° C. Find the volume at (a) 20° C, (b) at standard temperature (0° C). 

1. Change the two centigrade readings to absolute temperatures and make a direct proportion for the volumes and new temperatures. 

1500:x = 273 +30:273 + 20 x = 1450.4 cc. Answer to (a) 

2. Form a proper or improper fraction of the two absolute temperatures according to whether the volume is to be decreased or increased and multiply the given volume by the fraction. 273 +0 1500 X 1500 X 273 + 30 273 303 

1351.4 cc. Answer to (b)

Type Problem: To Correct for a Change in both Temperature and Pressure. Since the effects of temperature and pressure are independent of each other, a volume of gas is corrected for temperature and pressure changes by multiplying the given volume by two fractions, one to correct for the change in pressure, the other to correct for the change in temperature. 

Example: 550 cc. of oxygen were measured in a eudiometer at 

770 mm. pressure and 15°C. What is the volume of the gas at standard temperature, standard pressure (S.T.S.P.)? 550 X 770 273 760 288 

529 cc. 

Gay-Lussac's Law. If 20 cc. of oxygen and 20 cc. of hydrogen are combined and the mixture exploded in a eudiometer, water and 10 cc. of oxygen remain, showing that 20 cc. of hydrogen combine with 10 cc. of oxygen (see page 29). If a steam jacket is put around the eudiometer and the water which was formed is changed to steam, the volume of the steam will be found to be 20 cc. 

20 cc. hydrogen + 10 cc. oxygen 

2 vols. hydrogen + 1 vol. oxygen 

20 cc. steam 

2 vols. steam

It will be found by the experiment that the following volume combinations also hold true. 

1 vol. hydrogen + 1 vol. chlorine 

2 vols. hydrogen chloride (gas) 

1 vol. nitrogen + 3 vols. hydrogen 2 vols. ammonia (gas)

On the basis of the above data, Gay-Lussac's Law states that when two gases combine to form a gaseous product, the ratio of the combining volumes to each other, and the ratio of either gas volume to that of the gaseous product, may be expressed by small whole numbers. For example in watervol. hydrogen: vol. oxygen = 2:1 vol. hydrogen: vol. steam = 2:2

directly as their absolute temperature (Charles' Law). From these similarities in the behavior of gases, Avogardo formulated the following theory, which is now considered a law. 

Equal volumes of gases under the same temperature and pressure contain the same number of molecules. 

While working on his theory, Avogadro proved that the molecules of most gases contain two atoms (inert gases, helium, argon, etc., have only one atom in a molecule). This can be shown with reference to hydrogen as follows: (Gay-Lussac's Law) 

1 vol. hydrogen + 1 vol. chlorine 

(Avogadro's Law) 

2 vols. hydrogen chloride gas 

1 molecule hydrogen + 1 molecule chlorine 

2 molecules hydrogen chloride

From the last statement we can see that 1 molecule of hydrogen must supply hydrogen for two molecules of hydrogen chloride, in which there are at least 2 atoms of hydrogen, and consequently the hydrogen molecule must be composed of at least 2 atoms. This can be seen also by examining the formula of the equation. 

1 vol. + 1 vol. 

H + C1 2 vols. 2HC1 This equation is chemically impossible (see page 41). 

The only way that one volume of hydrogen can furnish 2 atoms for the 2 volumes of hydrogen chloride formed is, according to Avogadro, for the hydrogen to contain 2 atoms within one molecule. The same holds true for the chlorine. Thus: 

1 vol. + 1 vol. 2 vols. 1 molecule + 1 molecule 

H2 + C12 2 molecules 2HC1 

CALCULATION OF MOLECULAR WEIGHTS 

Molecular weights are computed from two sources of data.

Type Problem: From the Formula of the Substance. When the formula is known, find the sum of the atomic weights.

Example:

Compute the molecular weight of sulphuric acid 

H2SO4. Atomic weights: H

04 1; S 32; O = 16 (2 × 1) +32 + (16 × 4) = 2 +32 + 64 98, molecular weight.

Type Problem: From the Weight of a Liter of a Gas 1. Vapor Density Method. Divide the weight of a liter of the gas by the weight of a liter of hydrogen (under same conditions of temperature and pressure). This number is the vapor density of the gas as referred to hydrogen. 

Since there are equal numbers of molecules in the equal volumes of gases, the vapor density of a gas is the ratio of its molecular weight to hydrogen. Molecular weight of hydrogen is 2. 

Hence the molecular weight of a gas is equal to twice its vapor density. 

Example: To find the molecular weight of oxygen, Wt. of liter of oxygen, 1.43 g. 

Wt. of liter of hydrogen, .09 02. 16 X 2 

32, mol. wt. of O2. vapor density 2. Specific Gravity Method. The specific gravity of a gas is its weight in air divided by the weight of an equal volume of air. 

Oxygen, 1.43 g. per liter Air, 1.29 g. per liter 

Air, 1.29 g. per liter 

Hydrogen, .09 g. per liter 

1.11, Sp. gr. of oxygen. 

14.4, vapor density of air

Hence, to find molecular weight from specific gravity, multiply specific gravity by 14.4 X 2. 

Example: Find the molecular weight of a gas having a specific gravity of 1.2. 1.2 X 14.4 X 2 

35, mol. wt. 

3. Gram-Molecular Weight Method. 2.008 grams of hydrogen or 

32 grams of oxygen occupy a volume of 22.4 liters, at S. T. S. P. 

In other words, the gram-molecular weights of all gases have the same volumes. Hence, the weight of a liter of a gas, times 22.4, equals the gram-molecular weight of a gas.

Example: Find the molecular weight of oxygen, a liter of which weighs 1.43 grams. 1.43 X 22.4 = 32, mol. wt. of oxygen

CHAPTER ELECTRON THEORY 

Historical Introduction. In the closing years of the nineteenth century, doubt was cast upon the previously held concept of the atom as the smallest possible particle of matter. The English physicist, J. J. Thomson, after exhaustive research, stated the belief that particles of negative electricity, or electrons, formed a constituent part of the atoms of all elements. Rutherford, continuing the study of atomic structure, discovered another constituent of the atom, a positively charged particle, or proton, which equalized the negative electron. This concept of the structure of the atom opened a new field of scientific investigation and made possible the answering of questions about properties of matter which had previously defied explanation. What is the difference between a metal and a non-metal? What determines the valence of an element? What governs its activity? These and other questions can now be answered more or less definitely through our knowledge of the electron theory of atomic structure. 

Nature of the Atom. According to the electron theory, atoms of all elements are composed of positively charged particles called protons, and negatively charged particles called electrons. 

1. Structure. The central part of the atom is the nucleus, which contains both protons and electrons. In the nucleus, the number of protons is always in excess of the number of electrons. The rest of the atom is made up of outer or planetary electrons (in sufficient number to balance the number of excess protons in the nucleus) arranged in orbits or rings around the nucleus (Bohr's Theory). 

The entire character of the element, as we shall see later, is determined by the number and arrangement of these planetary electrons. 

2. Electrically Balanced. Atoms are electrically neutral; that is, in any given atom there are exactly the same number of positive protons and negative electrons present. 62

ELECTRON THEORY 

63 

3. Atomic Numbers and Weights. The number of planetary electrons, according to a scale of atomic measurement devised by Moseley, is called the atomic number of the element (see Table in Appendix). Experiments have shown that the weight of an atom is the weight of its protons, the electrons being so light (of a hydrogen atom) as to be negligible. The atomic number of an element, therefore, is the number of planetary electrons and the atomic weight of an element is the weight of its protons. 

Composition of the

ATOM 

NUCLEUS EXCESS OF PROTONS RINGS PLANETARY ELECTRONS ELECTRICALLY 

NEUTRAL PROTONS ELECTRONS Number of Electrons in the Rings. The number of electrons needed to fill or complete each ring varies with the position of the ring. The first ring (nearest the nucleus) holds two electrons; the second and third rings, eight electrons each. Other rings require larger numbers of electrons to complete them. Starting with the lowest atomic number, let us see how the rings are completed. 

Elements with One Ring. Hydrogen, atomic number 1, has 1 excess proton in the nucleus. We shall represent the positively charged nucleus as a dot, marked + 1. We know that one planetary electron is required to make the atom electrically neutral, so

we place a minus charge-an electron-in the first ring. We have just made an electron diagram of the hydrogen atom. 

Helium, atomic number 2, requires two outer electrons to balance the excess nuclear protons. The first ring is now complete. 

Elements with Two Rings. Lithium, atomic number 3, requires a second ring to hold the third electron. For successive elements with two rings, electrons may be added up to atomic number 10. 

Neon, atomic number 10, completes the second ring.

THREE RINGS TWO RINGS ONE RING 

(+1 +2 +10 +n He

Ne Na Arrangement of Electrons in the Atom Elements with Three Rings. The third ring is filled similarly by the elements sodium (11) to argon (18). Potassium (19) and calcium (20) begin the fourth ring.

Borrowing and Lending Electrons. Having seen how the electrons are arranged, we are now ready to learn how this arrangement determines the properties of the element. In the following paragraphs, the student should keep in mind this important fact: in chemical action, atoms tend to acquire a complete outer ring in the simplest manner possible. For example, if the outer ring has less than half the number of electrons required to complete it, a complete ring may be had by discarding these outer electrons (practically, by lending them to or sharing them with another element). If the outer ring has more than half the number of electrons required, the ring may be more easily completed by obtaining (borrowing) electrons to fill the vacancies. 

Activity of Elements. As we glance over the properties of elements, these questions may arise: Why are some elements, such as

ELECTRON THEORY 

65 sodium and fluorine, intensely active; others, such as carbon, less active, and still others absolutely inactive?

Examining the atomic structure, it is noted that sodium has but one electron in the outer or third ring. A complete second ring may be had by lending this electron, which can be quickly accomplished. Sodium, as a result, is an active element. Similarly, fluorine completes the outer ring by borrowing one electron; it is also very active. Elements which must acquire or discard two, three or more electrons progressively decrease in activity. 

Amphoteric Elements. Boron and carbon have four electrons in the outer ring., Atoms of this type may either borrow four or lend four electrons to complete the ring; they are, in fact, "on the fence". These elements are known as amphoteric, or doubleacting elements.

Inert Elements. The rare gas elements, helium, neon, argon (also krypton and xenon), have outer rings which are complete. 

There is no need to borrow or lend electrons; these elements exhibit no chemical activity. They are known as the inert or inactive elements.

In general, the smaller the number of electrons which must be borrowed or lent to complete the outer ring, the greater is the activity of the element.

Metallic and Non-Metallic Elements. It has been shown that elements with less than half the required number of electrons in the outer ring tend to lend these electrons. What change results when electrons are lent? Let us again consider sodium. This element has eleven electrons outside the nucleus which are balanced by eleven excess protons in the nucleus. If one electron (in the outer ring) is lent, the sodium atom is no longer neutral, but has an excess positive charge (see Ionization, pages 72ff.). When fluorine borrows one electron, it acquires an extra negative electron and thus becomes negatively charged. 

Elements which become positively charged by lending electrons are metals; those which become negatively charged by borrowing electrons are non-metals.

Valence. See Valence table in Appendix. If we compare the valence of a given element with the number of electrons borrowed or lent when an element enters into chemical combination, it can

be seen that the valence number and the number of electrons exchanged are identical. The outer ring of an atom, then, determines the valence by governing the number of electrons borrowed or lent. Sodium and fluorine accordingly have valences of +1 and -1 respectively. (The plus sign indicates a positive or lending element; the minus sign a negative or borrowing element.) Magnesium, lending two electrons, has a valence of +2; aluminum, +3.

Oxygen, borrowing two electrons, has a valence of -2; nitrogen, -3. Amphoteric elements have both a positive and negative valence. Carbon, for example, has a valence of ±4. Inert elements have no valence, or valence 0. 

Electronic Definition of Oxidation and Reduction. When we studied oxygen and hydrogen, we learned that oxidation was the chemical union of oxygen with a substance and that reduction was the chemical removal of oxygen from a substance. According to the electron theory, these definitions are enlarged to read as follows: Oxidation is a chemical reaction involving a loss of electrons and an increase in positive valence; reduction is a chemical reaction involving a gain of electrons and a decrease in positive valence. This electronic definition is broad enough to include nearly all types of chemical reactions involving all elements and not just oxygen. 

Examine the following equations and see how this applies. (1) Oxidation (2) Reduction FeCl2 + C1 →FeCl3 FeCl3+H FeCl2 + HC1 In equation (1), Fe(ferrous) increased its positive valence of 2 to Fe(ferric), with a positive valence of 3-a gain in positive valence of 1. Also, Fe(ferrous) loaned 2 electrons to the chlorine in ferrous chloride while Fe (ferric) loaned 3 electrons to the chlorine in ferric chloride a net loss of electrons of 1 (the difference between 2 and 3). (Fe+++ 2C1-) + C10 (Fe+++ + 3C1-) 

Ferrous chloride

Ferric chloride In equation (2), Fe(ferric) decreased its positive valence of 3 to Fe(ferrous), with a positive valence of 2-a decrease in positive valence of 1. Also, Fe(ferric) loaned 3 electrons to the chlorine in ferric chloride while Fe (ferrous) loaned only 2 electrons to the chlorine in ferrous chloride a gain in electrons of 1. (Fe++++3C1-) + H⁰ Ferric chloride (Fe++ + 2C1−) + (HCl) 

Ferrous chloride

67 In equation (1), nascent chlorine is the oxidizing agent; in equation (2), nascent hydrogen is the reducing agent. 

How Are Chemical Compounds Formed? The combination of elements to form a compound is a direct result of the lending and borrowing (or better, in this case, sharing) of electrons. The term sharing is used because the electrons act as a bond between the elements united. To illustrate:

ELECTRON THEORY 

69 Polar and Non-Polar Compounds. Polar compounds are those formed when two elements share only one electron. Non-polar compounds are those formed when two elements share two or more electrons. Molecules of gases are examples of non-polar compounds.

Isotopes. The earlier belief that each element has a definite and unchanging atomic weight has been shattered by the discovery of isotopes of many elements. Recently, isotopes of hydrogen having atomic weights of two and three have been discovered. It is now believed that hydrogen (and other elements) is composed of isotopes in such proportions that their average weight is the atomic weight of the element. Similarly, oxygen has isotopes having atomic weights of 15, 16, and 17. An isotope of an element is one that has the same atomic number but a different number of protons in the nucleus. Since the protons determine the weight of an atom, the atomic weight of isotopes differs. Since the atomic number of isotopes, and therefore the number of planetary electrons, is the same, the chemical properties of isotopes are similar. 

If the heavier isotopes of hydrogen and oxygen combine, the resulting compound must necessarily have a molecular weight greater than 18, that of common water. Dr. H. C. Urey has isolated a compound of this type and aptly called it heavy water. A study of its properties shows that it differs in many respects from the ordinary compound of hydrogen and oxygen. 

Importance of Atomic Numbers. We have seen that the atomic number of an element, by determining the number of electrons outside the nucleus, makes possible the structural arrangement of atoms and an explanation of element properties. Moseley's discovery of the law of atomic numbers did more: it established, for the first time, a clear and definite relationship of the elements. 

From the earliest days of chemistry, scientists tried to account for the occurrence of element groups, the similarity of their properties, and relationship to atomic weight. Mendeleyeff, in 1869, noted that as the atomic weight of elements increased, their properties varied in regular order from metallic, through amphoteric, to non-metallic, which he set down in a periodic table. 

This arrangement of elements made possible the prediction of the discovery of then unknown elements, and suggested the correction of atomic weights of others. It also permitted the grouping of

elements into families by noting the periodic repetition of properties as the atomic numbers increase (e. g., the halogen family). 

Useful as this arrangement was, its weakness lay in the fact that hydrogen has no place and also that some elements-argon and potassium-do not group themselves properly when arranged by atomic weights. The law of atomic numbers establishes a perfect scale of elements, beginning with hydrogen, in which each element has a unit number. The discrepancies of the periodic table of 

Mendeleyeff are thus completely eliminated. 

Note. For recent developments (radioactivity, atomic energy, new constituents of the atom) see Chapter 25.

CHAPTER IONIZATION 

Necessity of Moisture for Chemical Action. When we consider the conditions under which chemical action takes place, one fact becomes evident: action is initiated and intensified by water. A mixture of undissolved chemicals usually causes little or no action; the addition of water greatly increases the possibility of reaction taking place. What purpose, then, does water serve in causing this action? The answer will be found when we learn what takes place when some chemical compounds dissolve in water. 

We have heretofore considered chemical action as an interaction of atoms or molecules. This theory is not entirely satisfactory, since molecules are present in undissolved compounds as well as in their solutions; chemical action, however, usually takes place readily only under the latter condition. Evidently, a change occurs when some chemical compounds are dissolved. 

Theory of Dissociation. Suppose we dissolve salt in water, and determine the freezing point of the solution. The more salt we add, the lower the freezing point becomes; hence we conclude that the lower the freezing point, the greater is the number of molecules (or particles) of salt in solution. However, if equal numbers of molecules of sugar and salt are dissolved in equal quantities of water, the freezing point of the salt solution will be lowered twice as much as the freezing point of the sugar solution. It was shown by Arrhenius that there is but one lògical explanation of this phenomenon: each salt molecule in solution separates or dissociates into two particles called ions; the sugar molecules do not separate, but remain intact. 

Experiment shows that the ions formed are electrically charged. 

The action in dissolving salt can be expressed: 

NaCl (dissolved) salt molecule

Na+ + Clsodium ion chlorine ion Ions are charged atoms or groups of atoms. The number of charges are determined by the valence of the element or groups 72

IONIZATION 

73 which have gained or lost electrons during the process of dissociation (see page 65).

Electrolytes and Non-Electrolytes. Compounds which dissociate in solution have another property in common: the ability to conduct a current of electricity. This property is lacking in solutions of undissociated compounds, as the following experiment demonstrates. An electric light is connected in series with a source of current and two platinum electrodes. If we trace the current flow according to the diagram, we can see that in order to light the electric bulb the current must flow through the solution. 

BULB LIGHTED 

LIGHTED 

BULB NOT LIGHTED 

ELECTROLYTE STRONG ACID, BASE, 

SALT SOLUTIONS ETC. 

NON 

ELECTROLYTE SUGAR, PURE WATER, GLYCERINE, ALCOHOL, ETC. 

Electrolytes conduct electric current; non-electrolytes do not. 

On testing, it is seen that the bulb will glow brightly with strong acids, bases and salt solutions. With pure water, sugar solution, alcohol and glycerine no illumination takes place. Compounds, the solutions of which conduct electricity, are called electrolytes; those which do not, non-electrolytes. 

Difference between Ion and Atom. One may ask, "why are ions charged?" Let us include some of the facts we learned from our study of the electron theory and trace the history of sodium and chlorine first as separate atoms, then as parts of the undissolved sodium chloride molecule, and finally as sodium and chlorine ions.

Sodium Chloride (DISSOLVED) 

When the compound sodium chloride was formed from sodium and chlorine, sodium loaned one electron to chlorine. Upon dissociation, water made that loan a definite transfer, causing the sodium atom to lose one electron (and having thus lost a negative charge becomes a positive ion) and the chlorine atom to gain one electron (and having gained a negative charge becomes a negative ion). We may state the action as follows: sodium atom (neutral) minus one electron yields sodium ion. Naº e Na+ Chlorine atom (neutral) plus one electron yields chlorine ion. 

C10+ e

Cl- It is a striking fact that the charging of an atom during ion formation changes the properties; the chlorine ion, for example, does not have the color or odor of the element chlorine. The sodium ion does not react with water, as does the sodium atom. 

Free Ions. Free ions, or ions not formed in a solution, are formed by high temperatures. Hot gases, incandescent metals, and fused or molten compounds dissociate and form free ions by ejecting electrons.

IONIZATION IONIZA 

75 Dissociation of Acids and Bases. We have already seen the action of sodium chloride (a typical salt, see page 72) in solution. 

Acids and bases act in similar manner: Acids

HC1 H2SO4 H++ Cl- 2H++ SO4¯¯ Note that the electrical charge is numerically the same as the valence; note also that the hydrogen ions formed from sulphuric acid are single ions.

Bases NaOH Ca(OH)2 Na+ + OH- Ca++ + 2(OH)- Acids are compounds which contain in solution hydrogen ions (H+).

Bases are compounds which contain in solution hydroxyl ions (OH−). 

Acid and Base Activity. Some acids, such as nitric and hydrochloric, are strong acids, while carbonic acid (H2CO3) is weak. 

Similarly, potassium hydroxide is a strong base, while calcium hydroxide is weaker. What causes this difference? Briefly, the activity of acids and bases is dependent on the degree of dissociation in solution. Nitric acid is almost entirely dissociated into H+ and NO3-ions; carbonic acid is ionized to but a slight degree. A similar condition exists in the dissociation of KOH and Ca(OH)2. 

Concentrated sulphuric acid is not chemically active when cold, since the molecules are not ionized in any considerable amountsdue to the insufficient quantity of water present. The activity of this acid may be greatly increased by diluting it with water. 

Concentrated sulphuric acid may be shipped in iron containers, but the more dilute form must be transported in lead-lined barrels. 

Reversible Reactions. A reversible reaction is one that has reached a point of chemical equilibrium, as in the following equation:

KNO3 + NaC1 KC1+ NaNO3 The potassium, sodium, nitrate and chlorine ions are constantly dissociating, forming molecules of NaCl and KNO3, dissociating again and forming molecules of KCl and NaNO3, and so on over again.

76 

Common-Ion Effect. We have just learned that some reactions result in chemical equilibrium. In a saturated solution (see Chapter 

4) of sodium chloride a state of ionic equilibrium exists, which means that the rate at which NaCl molecules dissociate into Na+ and Cl- ions equals the rate at which these ions meet and reform NaCl molecules. NaCl Na+ + Cl- We learned in Chapter 4 that the addition of more NaCl to a saturated solution caused a precipitation of NaCl crystals. This is due to the excess of Na+ and Cl-ions. It is also possible to bring about a precipitation of NaCl crystals by adding another solute, such as HCl, having a common ion. The addition of the common ion Cl- brings about an excess of Cl-ions which combine with Na+ ions, form NaCl molecules and leave the solution as a precipitate. 

Reactions that Go to Completion. A reaction goes to completion when one of the products formed is removed from the field of chemical action. 

1. The non-ionization of some chemical compounds is an important factor in causing reactions to go to an end. When solutions of an acid and a base (HCl and KOH) are mixed, the ions present are:

KOH + HC1 K+ + OH‍ + H+ + Cl− The hydrogen ion, combining with the hydroxyl ion, forms water which does not dissociate (to any appreciable degree). 

For all purposes of reaction, then, the water withdraws from the field of chemical action; the reaction goes to completion through non-ionization of water. A complete ionic equation of this reaction is:

K++ OH + H+ + Cl− → (H2O) + K+ + Clsolution of base acid solution undissociated water dissociated ions 

2. Insoluble Precipitates. The formation of insoluble precipi tates also causes reactions to go to an end through non-ionization. 

When solutions of barium chloride and sulphuric acid are mixed insoluble barium sulphate forms, and settles out as an insoluble precipitate.

Ba+++ 2C1 + 2H+ + SO¯BaSO4 + 2H+ + 2C1-

IONIZATION 

77 3. Volatility. Evolution of a gas as one product of a chemical process causes reaction completion due to volatility. In preparing hydrogen, we allowed dilute sulphuric acid to act on zinc. By lending two electrons to the hydrogen ions of the acid, the zinc itself becomes ionized: Zn⁰ 2e 

Zn++ 

The equation is as follows: 

Zn + 2H++ SO4 H₂ ↑ + Zn+++ SO4¯¯ metallic atoms dissociated acid 2 gaseous hydrogen dissociated salt

The formation of other gases (Cl2, SO2, CO2, etc.) also causes reactions to complete through volatility.
