CHAPTER ACIDS, BASES AND SALTS 

In studying the composition and chemical properties of compounds in general, it is soon noticed that certain of these compounds have properties which classify them into three important groups: acids, bases and salts. These compounds are so closely related that it is advisable to study them together. 

ACIDS 

Nature of Acids. It would be impossible to distinguish acids as a group from other chemical compounds by physical properties alone; acids exist as solids, liquids and gases. In fact, pure acids would have few properties of any kind. It is only in water solution that their action is apparent. 

Properties of Acids 

1. All Acids Contain Hydrogen Ions. If we examine the formulas of common acids (HCl, HNO3, H2SO4) we notice that all contain a common element-hydrogen. The other elements may vary, but hydrogen is common to all. However, it is not merely the presence of hydrogen, but its action, which classifies acids as a group. Each of the above acids, in water solution, ionize or separate into electrically charged groups as follows: HC1 HNO3 H2SO4 H+ + Cl- H+ + NO3- 2H++ SO4¯¯¯ Innumerable other chemical compounds contain hydrogen which does not ionize, as does the hydrogen of acids. The presence of this ionizable hydrogen, then, is one of the distinguishing properties of acids. A strong acid is one which ionizes to a high degree. A weak acid is one which ionizes to a small degree. 

2. They Turn Litmus Paper Red. Acids act on some complex compounds, causing color changes. The most common of these is a 80

ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

81 substance known as litmus. In the presence of acids, blue litmus is changed to red-which is probably the most familiar (although not necessarily positive) test for acids in use. 

3. They Have a Sour Taste. If tested in highly dilute solution, common acids taste sour. The characteristically sour taste of such citrus fruits as lemons, or the sourness of vinegar, is also caused by the presence of acids. 

4. They Yield Hydrogen by Replacement with Metals. When acids are allowed to react with metals, in many cases hydrogen gas is given off-the method used for the laboratory preparation of hydrogen. Most acids which ionize to any degree (i.c., are fairly strong) act similarly. (With nitric acid the hydrogen does not escape as a gas, but is oxidized to water.) This hydrogen-forming action is called replacement, since the metal has taken the place of the hydrogen in the acid (see page 168 for relative displacement of metals): 

Zn + H2SO4 H2 + ZnSO4 5. Reaction with Bases-Neutralization. Acids, when mixed with bases, form water and salts; the properties of both acids and bases are lost. (See neutralization, below)

Preparation of Acids. Three methods are commonly used to prepare acids. These methods are described in detail in the discussion of the individual acids; a brief summary will be given here: 

1. Reaction of Compounds which Supply the Required Positive and Negative Elements or Groups (See Preparation of HCl and 

HNO3). To prepare any given acid, we need: (a) a compound to supply H+ ions, and (b) another compound to supply the negative group (acid radical). Hydrogen ions are obtained from sulphuric acid. The negative group is obtained from any salt containing it. 

In general, then, an acid may be prepared by heating H2SO4 with a salt:

H2SO4 + NaCl NaHSO4 + HC1 

2. The Solution of a Gas in Water. The hydrogen chloride formed in the above reaction, although containing hydrogen, does not exhibit acid properties until given an opportunity to ionize (by solution in water). When so dissolved, it then becomes hydrochloric acid.

3. Reaction of an Acid Anhydride with Water (See Preparation of SO2, SO3). Oxides of some non-metallic elements, when dissolved in water, react to form acids: CO2 + H2O 

H2CO3 

SO2 + H2O - → H2SO3 SO3 + H2O →→ H2SO4 Common Acids. The acids mentioned above are liquid or gaseous; still others exist as solids. Oxalic acid, H2C2O4, used as a bleaching agent, is one of these. In everyday life we continually use or come in contact with acids, some of which are: citric acid (in lemons and grapefruit); lactic acid (in sour milk and cream); and tartaric acid (in grapes).

BASES 

Nature of Bases. If, as we previously did with acids, we examine the formulas of some common bases (NaOH, KOH, Ca(OH)2), we find again, that a common characteristic is apparent; all bases contain the group OH. In addition to the OH group, bases have another common property. The positive element in all bases (ammonia excepted) is a metal. 

Properties of Bases 

1. Dissolved in water, bases ionize:

NaOH 

Na+ + OH- We may consider the presence of the hydroxyl ions (OH) as denoting bases, just as the H+ ions characterized acids. A strong base is one which ionizes to a great degree. A weak base is one which ionizes to a small degree. 

2. When bases act on litmus, the color of the litmus is changed from red to blue. 

3. Mixed with acids, the properties of both the acid and the base are lost; water and salts are formed (neutralization). 

4. A solution of a base, when rubbed between the fingers, feels slippery or soapy.

Preparation of Bases. Bases are most commonly prepared by (1) the action of an active metal with water, (2) the action of a

ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

83 888 basic anhydride with water. In both cases we have the same end in view: to form a compound of a metal and the OH group. (3) 

One base ammonium hydroxide, NH4OH-is formed by the reaction of a gas with water. 

1. The Action of an Active Metal with Water. If active metals, such as sodium and potassium, are allowed to react with water, part of the hydrogen is replaced as follows: 2Na + 2H2O2NaOH + H2 Potassium and calcium react similarly, calcium more slowly, since it is less active. 

2. The Action of a Basic Anhydride with Water. Oxides of certain metals, reacting with water, also produce bases:

CaO+ H2O Ca(OH)2 Sodium peroxide, Na2O2, forms NaOH with water.

2Na2O2 + 2H2O 4NaOH + O2 This action may also be used as a method of preparing oxygen. 

3. Ammonia Gas, NH3, Dissolved in Water, Reacts to Form a Base. 

NH3 + H2O NH4OH

Uses. An outstanding characteristic of bases is their ability to attack organic matter. Fats and grease are readily dissolved by their action, which makes weak solutions of bases valuable as cleansing agents. When fats are treated with bases, soap is formed. Some paint removers contain strong bases. Bases, because of their ability to neutralize acids, are employed medicinally to control excess acidity of the human body. 

SALTS 

Nature of Salts. If, as we did with acids and bases, we examine the formulas of several salts (NaCl, KNO3, CaSO4), we can determine the character of the elements which constitute salts. 

In each case we see that the first part of the compound is a metal; the remainder is an acid group or radical. 

Acid Radical

Metal Na Cl NO3 Ca SO4

A salt, then, is a chemical combination of a metal and an acid radical. 

Formation of Salts 1. Neutralization. If an acid is added to a base until the solution produces no color change on litmus, the properties of both base and acid completely disappear. According to the ionic equation,

Na+ + OH + H+ + Cl− Na++ Cl+ H₂O the H+ and OH- ions unite forming water, the molecules of which do not ionize (at least, not to an appreciable degree). The very ions, then, which gave acids and bases their identity are withdrawn from solution, hence the formation of a neutral mixture. 

The other salts just mentioned are formed in a similar manner: K++ OH + H++ NOз˜¯ →→ K+ + NO3 + H2O Ca+++ 20H- + 2H++ SO4 Ca+++SO4 + 2H2O (a) Heat of Neutralization. All neutralization reactions are accompanied by the liberation of heat. The heat is called the heat of neutralization, which may be accounted for by the formation of water, since the combination of hydrogen and oxygen to form water always releases heat energy. (b) Hydrolysis. Hydrolysis, the reaction of a salt and water to form an acid and a base, is the opposite of neutralization. When salts of strong acids are dissolved, they react with the water to form a strong acid and weak base.

CuSO4 + 2H2O 

Cu(OH)2 + H2SO4

The slight ionization of water yields OH- ions which unite with the Cu++, and H+ ions which unite with the SO4-- H2O H+ + OH- The Cu(OH)2, however, dissociates to a lesser degree than the H2SO4. As a result, there are fewer OH- than H+ ions in the solution and the solution gives an acid reaction. 

When salts of strong bases are dissolved, they react with the water in a similar manner to form a strong base and weak acid. 

H2CO3 + 2NaOH 

Na2CO3 + 2H2O

ACIDS, BASES, AND SALTS 

85 Neutral salts, such as NaCl, do not hydrolyze. In this case it may be assumed that both compounds formed on reaction of the salt with water dissociate to an equal degree; there is no excess of either H+ or OH- ions in solution, and the solution as a result is neutral. 

2. Single Replacement. A metal replaces the hydrogen of an acid, forming a salt:

Zn + H2SO4 → H₂ ↑ + ZnSO4 

3. Interaction of Salt Solutions. An insoluble salt precipitates: 

Na++ Cl+ Ag+ + NO3- AgCl↓ +Na+ + NO3 

4. Direct Combination (synthesis) 

2Na + Cl2 2NaC1

CHAPTER 10  

The halogen ("salt formers") group includes fluorine, chlorine, bromine and iodine. They are the most active non-metals and correspond to the active group of metals. Their salts form a large number of stable compounds. 

Distribution. The halogens are not to be found free, but they have a wide distribution in the form of halogen salts (halides). 

Chlorine is found in deposits of rock salt (halite) and in sea water as sodium chloride.

Bromine occurs in salt deposits and sea water as sodium and magnesium bromide (NaBr, MgBr2).

Iodine is obtained from the ashes of seaweed (kelp) and, in large quantities, from the residues of Chile saltpeter as sodium iodate (NaIO3).

Fluorine compounds are chiefly fluorspar (CaF2) and cryolite (NaзAIF6). 

Preparation of Halogens. All the halogens may be prepared by the following methods: 

1. The electrolysis of their salt solutions. 

2. In the laboratory, chlorine, bromine and iodine are prepared by the oxidation of the halogen acids. 

CHLORINE 

Preparation of Chlorine. Chlorine is prepared in the laboratory by the oxidation of hydrochloric acid by manganese dioxide. 

Manganese dioxide is placed in a flask and concentrated hydrochloric acid is poured through the thistle tube. On warming gently, the oxygen of the manganese dioxide combines with the hydrogen of the acid to form water and half the chlorine is liberated. The gas is collected by upward displacement of air. MnO2 + 4HCl →→→→ MnCl2 + Cl2 ↑ + 2H2O 87

Commercially, chlorine is made by the electrolysis of brine. 

The products are sodium hydroxide and chlorine. The Nelson 

Cell, which is chiefly used, has a special diaphragm of asbestos between the electrodes to keep the chlorine from reacting with the sodium hydroxide (see page 175 for preparation of sodium hydroxide). The complete reaction is as follows: 2NaCl + 2H2O → 2NaOH + H2 ↑ + Cl2 ↑ HCI

MnO2 AUD 

CHLORINE 

MnO2 + 4HCI → MnCl₂+2H₂O+CI₂ 

MANGANESE DIOXIDE AND HYDROCHLORIC ACID YIELDS MANGANESE CHLORIDE-WATER-CHLORINE 

Laboratory Preparation of Chlorine Physical Properties. Chlorine gas has a greenish-yellow color, is quite soluble in water (2:1), and is two and one-half times heavier than air.

Chlorine has a characteristic irritating odor and, if inhaled, attacks the lung passages with the result that a high concentration of the gas will produce fatal results. It was the first poison gas to be used in the World War.

For transportation, chlorine gas is liquefied and shipped in steel cylinders.

Chemical Activity 1. Chlorine Combines Directly with Many Elements. Antimony, iron, zinc and hydrogen will burn in an atmosphere of chlorine to form chlorides: 2Sb + 3C12 2Fe + 3C12 2SbC13 2FeCl3 Zn + Cl2 → ZnCl2 H2 + Cl2 →→ 2HC1 

Note that these are examples of combustion without oxygen. 

Hydrogen burns with a bluish-white flame to form hydrogen chloride, a gas with a sharp, penetrating odor. 

2. Chlorine Will Take Hydrogen from Hydrocarbons. Paraffin is a compound of hydrogen and carbon. When a lighted candle (paraffin) is lowered into a bottle of chlorine, a reddish flame appears. Carbon is deposited on the sides of the bottle and hydrogen chloride, noticeable by the odor, is formed. 

3. Action in the Presence of Moisture. Chlorine, dissolved in water, combines with the hydrogen of the water, first forming hypochlorous acid (HCIO).

H2O + Cl2 HCl + HC10 HClO The hypochlorous acid is then decomposed by the action of sunlight: HCIO HC1 + O It is important, consequently, that chlorine water be kept in a dark place.

If organic matter is present, the hypochlorous acid will decompose and oxidize the organic matter without the need of light. 

Chlorine water then becomes an oxidizing agent. 

Uses of Chlorine. The principal uses of chlorine are for bleaching cotton and paper, disinfecting in the household to destroy bacteria, and purifying water supplies of cities and towns. 

Bleaching. In bleaching cloth, a solution of bleaching powder (CaOCl2) and dilute acid are kept in adjacent tanks so that the cloth runs through an acid solution and then through the bleaching solution. The acid reacts with the calcium hypochlorite to form

hypochlorous acid, which in turn gives up nascent oxygen to destroy the dyes and coloring matter forming colorless compounds. CaOCl2 + H2SO4 CaSO4 + HCIO + HC1 

HCIO + Organic Matter

HC1+0+ (Change in bleached materials) 

The action of bleaching is controlled by treating the bleached goods chemically to nullify the action of the chlorine. 

Disinfecting. Chloride of lime (CaOCl2) is the source of the chlorine used for disinfecting purposes. This bleaching powder, sprinkled about in damp places, such as a cellar, releases chlorine when the carbon dioxide and moisture in the air combine to form a weak acid.

CaOCl2 + H2O + CO2 →→ CaCO3 + Cl2 + H2O The damp chlorine, through oxidation, destroys disease germs and the materials in which they thrive. 

Solutions of sodium hypochlorite, such as the Dakin solution, are used to treat deep wounds. Sodium hypochlorite is made by adding sodium carbonate to a solution of bleaching powder (CaOCl2). 

Purification. The water supplies of many towns and cities are treated with carefully controlled amounts of chlorine to destroy bacteria and prevent epidemics (typhoid fever, for example). 

Other Uses. Chlorine is used to make some of its compounds, such as carbon tetrachloride and chloroform, and is a constituent for many of the poison gases used in warfare. 

BROMINE 

Laboratory Preparation. To prepare bromine, potassium or sodium bromide, concentrated sulphuric acid and MnO2 are mixed in a test tube. This tube is connected by a glass delivery tube with another partly filled with water. On heating, the brown bromine vapor passes off and condenses as a liquid under water. The reaction takes place in two steps: 2KBr+H2SO4 K2SO4 + 2HBr The hydrobromic acid formed is oxidized by the manganese dioxide.

The complete reaction is: 2KBr+MnO2 + 2H2SO4 - K2SO4 + MnSO4 + 2H2O + Br₂ KBr +MnO₂ +H2SO4 BROMINE 

WATER 

BROMINE 2KBr+MnO₂+2H₂SO4•K₂SO4+MnSO4+2H₂O+B₁₂ Laboratory Preparation of Bromine Commercial Preparation. The increased demand for bromine has made it commercially important. It is made commercially by treating brine residues and sea water (which contain magnesium bromide) with chlorine in the presence of steam. The chlorine replaces the bromine in the magnesium bromide: 

MgBr₂+ Cl2 MgCl2 + Br2 Properties of Bromine. The word bromine means stench. 

Bromine vapor is irritating to mucous membranes, resembling chlorine in its effect. It is a dense heavy liquid which vaporizes easily, so that a bottle of liquid bromine is filled with fumes above the liquid.

It shows varying degrees of solubility. It is slightly soluble in water, but it is very soluble in a solution of potassium bromide, carbon disulphide, or chloroform.

Bromine resembles chlorine in chemical behavior, but is not so active. It combines with hydrogen and metals to form bromides. 

The water solution is an oxidizing agent and has bleaching properties.

Uses. Bromine is useful in the making of many dyes and drugs. Bromides are used in medicines and in photography. One recent use of bromine is in the making of tetraethyl lead for antiknock gasolines.

IODINE Preparation. Iodine is given off when a mixture of potassium iodide, manganese dioxide and sulphuric acid is heated in a test tube. The violet vapor condenses directly into a solid on the

Laboratory Preparation of Iodine funnel. The action is similar to that for the preparation of bromine, the manganese dioxide acting as an oxidizing agent on the hydrochloric acid. 2KI + MnO2 + 2H2SO4 — K2SO4 + MnSO4 + 2H2Ỏ + I2 Physical Properties. Iodine is a blackish crystalline solid. It is scarcely soluble in water, quite soluble in a solution of potassium iodide and very soluble in alcohol, forming a brown solution. 

In carbon disulphide or chloroform it gives a purple-colored solution. A small amount will color starch-paste blue, which is a test for either starch or free iodine.

If a crystal of iodine is warmed, it will not melt but will pass directly into a vapor, which will condense again into the crystals. 

This change from a vapor to a solid without going through a liquid stage is called sublimation. The process serves to distil or purify the impure product. 

Chemical Properties. Iodine shows less chemical activity than the other halogens. It will unite directly with yellow phosphorus, furnishing an example of spontaneous combustion: 2P + 312 - 2PI3 Uses. Iodine is used in manufacture of drugs and photographic chemicals. Tincture of iodine, a solution of iodine in alcohol, is a strong antiseptic for treatment of wounds and is useful to reduce swellings.

FLUORINE 

Fluorine, the most active of all the elements under ordinary conditions, is a slightly yellowish gas. It is separated from its compounds by the electrolysis of potassium fluoride and hydrochloric acid. Since it is so active, only materials that are very resistant to acids can be used. Moissan, the French chemist, first used a platinum and iridium container and electrodes. Now graphite is used. Fluorine is too difficult to control to have any commercial uses.

CHARACTERISTICS OF THE HALOGENS 

Halogens as Non-Metals. The halogens form the most marked series of elements with strong non-metallic properties. In the order of chemical activity, they range from fluorine, the most active, through chlorine, bromine and iodine. Their affinity for positive elements is illustrated by the action of each toward water. 

1. Fluorine and water combine with violence. 

2. Chlorine dissolves in water and combines with it. 

3. Bromine dissolves in water and combines with it under sunlight. 

4. Iodine is only slightly soluble in water but does not combine with it.

The more active fluorine and chlorine combine directly with many metals, while bromine and iodine are made to do so with difficulty.

The physical state of these four elements indicates a growing complexity of atomic and molecular structure from the active gases fluorine and chlorine, through liquid bromine, to the solid iodine.

The atomic structure accounts for the close relationship of these elements. A fluorine atom has a nucleus and 2 rings of electrons, chlorine has 3 rings; bromine 4, and iodine 5. In the outer ring of each element there are seven electrons, making one electron lacking. Thus each element needs to take up only one electron to complete its outer ring. Since hydrogen and metals easily give up electrons, it accounts for the halogen's readiness to combine with them.

Their relative chemical activity is explained by the particular ring in which the added electron is placed. In fluorine, the electron finds a place in the second ring while in iodine it is in the fifth ring, much further away from the nucleus; the attraction from the nucleus, consequently, is less. Hence, iodine ions can be replaced with greater ease than chlorine ions, with the result that the stability of iodine compounds is less. 

Since the halogens form a related group, their compounds also have many characteristics which are in common, viz., general methods of preparation, similar chemical properties and relative degrees of stability.

The acids of this group are prepared by treating a salt of the element with sulphuric acid and heating to drive off the vapor of the acid. The water solution of these acids have strong acid properties in the same order of stability as the elements themselves.

Physical Properties. Hydrogen chloride, a colorless gas, has a sharp odor and is very soluble (500:1) in water. It is 1 times heavier than air. The water solution is known as hydrochloric acid or by its common name, muriatic acid. In damp air, a bottle of the gas will "fume." This can be seen by blowing across the mouth of the bottle.

Chemical Properties. The water solution is a strong acid, i.e., it has a sour taste, turns blue litmus red, liberates hydrogen when it reacts with metals and combines with bases to form salts and water.

These marked acid properties are due to the presence of hydrogen ions, since hydrochloric acid is highly dissociated. 

Uses. Hydrochloric acid is used to prepare its salts (chlorides).

It is boiled with starch to change the starch into glucose. It is also used to obtain glue and gelatine from bones and scraps of hide. It will dissolve oxides and film on metals. 

Chlorides. Chlorides are salts of hydrochloric acid. They may be prepared by:

This precipitate is soluble in ammonium hydroxide, but insoluble in dilute nitric acid.

8HI+ H2SO4 H2S + 412 + 4H2O

This is a test for an iodide. 

Hydrofluoric Acid. Hydrogen fluoride is prepared by the action of concentrated sulphuric acid on calcium fluoride. Since the gas attacks glassware, lead dishes are usually used.

CaF2+H2SO4 CaSO4 + 2HF CaSO4+2HF The gas is colorless and has a sharp odor. The water solution forms the stable hydrofluoric acid, which is usually kept in wax bottles, since it will combine with the silica in glass.

CHAPTER 11  

Occurrence of Sulphur. Sulphur is found in widely distributed localities both in free (native) states and in combination with other elements. Volcanic regions, chiefly in Sicily and Japan, furnish a limited amount of sulphur, but the major portion of the sulphur supply at present is obtained from the immense deposits of native sulphur which underlie extensive areas in Texas and Louisiana. 

APPROX. 

500 

FEET 

ESTEAM 

SULPHUR SAND CLAY SULPHUR BEARING ROCK 

STEAM 

SULPHURE AIR- Frasch Process 

Extraction. For many years, deposits of nearly pure sulphur were known to exist in the above-mentioned states, but the presence of quicksand above the sulphur beds made sulphur extraction practically impossible. Eventually, a process devised by the chemist, Frasch, solved the difficulty. The Frasch process employs 102

three concentric pipes extending from ground level to the sulphur beds. Water heated under pressure to high temperature is forced down through the space between the outer and middle pipes, melting the sulphur. The liquid sulphur is then blown into a light, frothy mass by hot compressed air supplied through the center pipe, and is forced to the surface through the middle pipe. The sulphur is allowed to solidify in large wooden bins. After solidification, the bin sides are removed, leaving a block of sulphur weighing hundreds of tons. The purity of the sulphur is so high (over 99%) that for most purposes it requires no further refining before use. 

Allotropic Forms of Sulphur. Sulphur, like oxygen, exists in allotropic forms. In the case of sulphur, there are three forms which differ markedly in appearance and stability. 

1. Rhombic. If sulphur is dissolved in carbon disulphide and the solvent allowed to evaporate, the most stable form of sulphurrhombic-will crystallize out of solution. The crystals of this form are octahedral (8 sides to each crystal) and resemble two pyramids joined at the base. 

2. Prismatic. To prepare this form, sulphur is carefully melted at a temperature just sufficient to liquefy it. As it cools, long, sharply pointed crystals are seen to project from the sides to the center of the mass. The liquid sulphur remaining may be poured off; the sulphur is then seen as a dense mass of crystals of the prismatic form. 

3. Amorphous (Plastic). When sulphur is heated to the boiling point and then poured into cold water, amorphous sulphur is obtained. It differs greatly in appearance from the other forms, collecting in strands and masses of rubber-like elasticity without crystalline form; hence the name amorphous (without form). The color may vary from amber to black.

Stability of Allotropic Forms. Rhombic sulphur is the most common form; it is stable at all ordinary temperatures (up to 96° 

C). Both prismatic and amorphous forms are stable at higher temperatures (prismatic, 96°C-114°C; amorphous, above 114°C), but on standing at room temperature, they revert to the rhombic in crystalline structure. It is believed that this change is due to the loss of the energy which these two forms acquire during their formation.

Properties of Sulphur 1. Physical. Common roll-sulphur or brimstone is a yellow, brittle, crystalline substance, solid at ordinary temperatures, but melting slightly above the boiling point of water. It is readily soluble in carbon disulphide, from which solution the rhombic crystals are obtained.

During the process of melting, sulphur undergoes noticeable changes in color and viscosity. When first changed into liquid state, it is a thin liquid, amber in color; continued heating transforms it into a dark, thick fluid resembling molasses. Just before the boiling point is reached the liquid again thins out, although the color remains dark. (+8) 

OXYGEN (2) 

(+16) 

SULPHUR (-2) Atomic Structure of Oxygen and Sulphur 

2. Chemical. In many respects sulphur, acting as a nonmetal, is quite similar to oxygen, especially in its ability to combine with hydrogen and metals. Considering the atomic structure of sulphur and oxygen (see diagram), this is not surprising; both are non-metals with the same valence. Although sulphur is not as active as oxygen at ordinary temperatures, its activity is increased as the temperature rises. In general, for most metallic oxides we find a corresponding sulphide. 4Fe + 302 → 2Fe2O3 Fe + S 2Zn + O2 

FeS 2ZnO Zn + S ZnS

Sulphur is an amphoteric element, that is it acts both as a nonmetal (valence-2) and as a metal (valence +4, +6). As a metallic element it combines with oxygen to form sulphur dioxide, as we shall see later.

Uses of Sulphur. Sulphur is essential in the manufacture of a great number of commercial products. The rubber industry uses sulphur in the process of vulcanizing, which increases the strength of rubber and its hardness and makes it unaffected by temperature changes. Rubber cement is made with carbon disulphide. When burned with oxygen, sulphur forms sulphur dioxide, which is used for fumigating purposes and as a refrigerant, as well as in the preparation of sulphuric acid. Explosives, dyes, fireworks, matches and lime-sulphur mixtures for spraying trees and plants consume large quantities of sulphur annually. 

HYDROGEN SULPHIDE 

Preparation. Hydrogen sulphide is found, in its natural state, in volcanic regions and in some wells and springs. It may be prepared by the direct combination of hydrogen and sulphur, but this method is not practical. The commonly used method of preparing 

H2S is the action of HCl on the sulphides of some metals, such as FeS. In this action, hydrogen sulphide, H2S, is formed; FeS2HC1 FeCl2 + H2S ↑ Hydrogen sulphide is also formed by the decomposition of protein in the presence of sulphur. 

Properties of Hydrogen Sulphide 1. Physical. H2S is a colorless gas; it is detected by its disagreeable odor, similar to that of rotten eggs (which odor is caused in part by H2S). It dissolves slightly in water. It is poisonous, continued inhalation resulting in sickness. 

2. Chemical. Hydrogen sulphide forms sulphides with metals by (a) direct action on metals, (b) action on solutions of salts of metals. 2Ag + H2S → 

Ag2S + H2 ↑ Ag₂S + 2HNO3 (a) (b) 2AgNO3 + H2S →

The tarnishing of metals, such as silver and copper, is chiefly due to the formation of sulphide coatings. Compare this with the tarnishing which results from oxide coatings. 

Metallic sulphides formed by the reaction of H2S with salt solutions, as in (b) above, are generally insoluble in water, and can be identified by characteristic colors. Examples: copper sulphide (CUS), black; cadmium sulphide (CdS), yellow; antimony sulphide (Sb2S3), orange; zinc sulphide (ZnS), white. Due to the formation of these colors, H2S is important as a reagent in qualitative analysis, making it possible to determine the identity of metals by the color of the sulphides formed.

Water and sulphur dioxide are formed: 2H2S 302 → 2SO2 + 2H2O If the oxygen supply is insufficient for complete combustion, free sulphur and water are formed: 2H2SO₂ 2H2O + 2S Acid Formation. A water solution of hydrogen sulphide is a weak acid, since it turns litmus red and acts on metals, liberating

hydrogen. The slow action of this solution, and its low capacity to conduct an electric current, indicate that the acid ionizes to but a small degree, and consequently is a weak acid. 

Water solutions of H2S occur naturally in some localities. This liquid, taken from wells and springs, has the characteristic odor of hydrogen sulphide and is disagreeable to taste; it is commonly known as "Sulphur Water." H2SO4 Na2SO3

SULPHUR DIOXIDE 

Occurrence. This gas is found in nature in volcanic gases. It is formed during the burning of some fuels, such as coal and coke, which contain small amounts of sulphur, and is given off as a waste product in some industrial processes, such as the roasting of sulphide ores of lead and copper.

Preparation of Sulphur Dioxide 

1. Industrial Method. SO2 is usually prepared by burning sul

phur in oxygen or in a carefully regulated volume of air. The sulphur and oxygen combine directly:

S+ O2 SO2 2. Laboratory Method. SO2 may be conveniently prepared in small quantities by the action of a dilute acid on sodium sulphite. 

The sodium sulphite is placed in a flask, and the acid allowed to drop slowly into the flask through a thistle tube, producing a steady evolution of the gas. Sulphur dioxide is collected by downward displacement of air.

Na2SO3 + H2SO4 Na2SO4 + H2SO3 The H2SO3 then decomposes into H2O and SO2. The complete equation is:

Na2SO3 + H2SO4 Na2SO4 + H2O + SO2 ↑ As an alternative laboratory method for producing SO2, copper is heated with concentrated H2SO4. SO2 is formed by reduction of the acid. The equation is as follows:

Cu + 2H2SO4 Properties CuSO4 + 2H2O + SO2 ↑ 1. Physical. Sulphur dioxide is heavier than air, colorless, and has an acrid, irritating odor. It is readily soluble in water. By the use of pressure, the gas can be liquefied at ordinary temperatures, which makes it a convenient refrigerant for use in household refrigerators. 

2. Chemical. It is an acid former. In water solution, SO2 forms sulphurous acid:

H2O + SO2 → H2SO3 In addition to exhibiting the ordinary acid properties (turning litmus red, neutralizing bases to form salts) this acid is marked by its reducing action, in which it unites with oxygen of other compounds, thus reducing them. With hydrogen peroxide, H2O2, the action is: 

H2SO3 + H2O2 

H2SO4 + H2O

A similar action takes place with many other compounds containing oxygen. We may think of the sulphurous acid as trying to

change into a more stable and active form, sulphuric acid, and to accomplish this action it may take oxygen from any available source. In bleaching (described later) the use of SO2 depends on this property.

When bases are neutralized by H2SO3, salts known as sulphites are formed: 2NaOH+H2SO3 Na2SO3 + 2H2O Calcium bisulphite (Ca(HSO3)2), used extensively in papermaking, is prepared in this manner. 

Uses. Used as a disinfectant and preservative, sulphur dioxide checks the growth of bacteria and molds. When used as a disinfectant in destroying harmful bacteria, the gas is eminently satisfactory; its use in food preservation is open to question, since its action may be injurious to animal as well as plant life. 

SO2 is used for bleaching. Substances which may be harmed by the use of chlorine as a bleaching agent may be safely decolorized by the use of SO2 in the presence of moisture. This bleaching, however, is not of a permanent quality; exposure to light may cause the original color to return.

Sulphur Trioxide (SO3). Sulphur dioxide, when combined with additional oxygen, forms sulphur trioxide, SO3: 2SO2 + O22SO3 SO3 is a colorless liquid which, when dissolved in water, forms sulphuric acid.

SO3 + H2O H2SO4 Since both SO2 and SO3 unite with water to form respectively H2SO3 and H2SO4, they are known as acid anhydrides (anhydride means substance without water). Basic anhydrides also exist; these form bases in an analagous manner. 

SULPHURIC ACID 

Importance. A prominent chemist at one time made the statement, "the civilization of a country is proportionate to the amount of sulphuric acid used by it." Scarcely an important industry exists which does not require the use of this acid in some step of its manufacturing process. Its preparation dates from earliest times.

The Arabians used it, calling it Oil of Vitriol. It was studied and used by medieval alchemists. Today its use runs into millions of tons annually.

Preparation by the Contact Process. The preparation of sul phuric acid requires three main steps: (1) preparing SO2, (2) oxidizing SO2 to SO3, (3) dissolving SO3 in water to form H2SO4. 

BURNING SULPHUR PURIFIER DRIER (CONC.H2SO4) CATALYST PLATINIZED ASBESTOS

Laboratory Preparation of Sulphuric Acid by Contact Process 

(1) Sulphur dioxide is obtained by burning sulphur in air. This gas is thoroughly purified to remove foreign substances. (2) Then, the pure SO2 is mixed with additional oxygen and passed over heated, finely divided platinum, which acts as a catalytic agent. 

In the presence of the platinum, SO2 and O2 combine, forming SO3. (3) SO3 is converted into H2SO4 by dissolving the SO3 in concentrated sulphuric acid to form fuming sulphuric acid, H2SO4. 

SO3, which is then diluted with water to form sulphuric acid of about 98% concentration.

The contact process may therefore be summarized in the following equations: (1) SO₂SO2 (2) 2SO2 + O2 2SO3 (3) SO3 + H2SO4 H2SO4.SO3 (4) H2SO4.SO3 + H2O → 2H2SO4 

Properties of Sulphuric Acid 1. Physical. Sulphuric is the heaviest of common acids, its weight being nearly twice that of water. Its specific gravity is 1.84. 

In the pure state it is colorless, and oily or "syrupy" in appearance. When concentrated sulphuric acid is mixed with water, a great deal of heat is liberated. Care should be taken when mixing with water to pour the sulphuric acid into the water slowly. 

2. Chemical. Sulphuric acid acts on metals in both dilute and concentrated states, the type of action varying with the degree of concentration and the temperature of the acid. 

Dilute acid gives a simple replacement action with metals:

Fe + H2SO4 Zn + H2SO4 FeSO4 + H2 ↑ ZnSO4 + H2 ↑ Cold, concentrated sulphuric acid has little action on metals; when heated, it acts as an oxidizing agent with most metals, being itself reduced during action.

Cu + 2H2SO4 CuSO4 + 2H2O + SO2 ↑ Concentrated sulphuric acid is intensely active as an absorber of water, in fact, so powerful is the action that it will absorb the elements of water from compounds in which they may be present. 

Carbohydrate materials, such as paper, wood, and sugar, are instantly charred when brought in contact with hot, concentrated H2SO4. The hydrogen and oxygen are absorbed by the acid, leaving carbon. Because of this action of removing water from compounds, sulphuric acid is known as a dehydrating agent.

The ability of H2SO4 to dissolve grease and metallic oxides is

used in preparing metal surfaces for plating, galvanizing and covering with tin. The electro-chemical industry employs the acid in plating solutions, and as the electrolyte of lead storage batteries. 

It is used in refining petroleum to remove impurities. As a dehydrating agent it serves to remove moisture from gases, both in the laboratory and industrially. In preparing dyes and explosives, sulphuric acid is used to absorb water, which forms as a by-product in these processes. Other uses, such as the preparation of sulphates, will be described in succeeding chapters.

CHAPTER 12 THE ATMOSPHERE-NITROGEN 

Composition of Air. Oxygen and nitrogen comprise over 98% of the composition of the atmosphere; the remainder consisting of small quantities of carbon dioxide, argon, water vapor and minute amounts of rare, inert gases. Although the percentage of the constituents of the atmosphere varies slightly, the composition by volume is approximately: Nitrogen. Oxygen. Argon...

Percent 78 21 .93 .04 .03 

Carbon Dioxide. 

Helium, Neon, Krypton, Xeon..

Air-Compound or Mixture? The ancients regarded air as an element, and this belief persisted through medieval times. Experiment has proven, however, that the constituents of air are not chemically combined and air is a mixture of the substances composing it. The following proofs may be given to confirm this statement: 

1. The percentage of the constituents of the air varies slightly; in a compound the percentage is fixed and unvarying. 

2. A compound boils at a definite temperature; its elements cannot be separated by evaporation. When air is liquefied, nitrogen boils away before oxygen. 

3. When elements which form a compound are mixed under the proper circumstances, they usually react, producing a noticeable effect of chemical change. This does not happen when oxygen and nitrogen are mixed in the proper proportion to form air. 

4. When air is dissolved in water, oxygen and nitrogen dissolve at different rates. Constituents of a compound would dissolve at the same rate. 117

Minor Constituents of the Atmosphere 

1. Water Vapor. Evaporation of water in oceans, lakes, rivers, etc., and animal respiration continually supply water vapor to the air. The presence of water vapor is detected when its condensation results in rain, clouds, snow, fog or the "sweating" of a glass of ice water in warm air. Depending upon conditions, the amount of water vapor may range from only a trace to over 1%. 

Our comfort is greatly dependent upon the amount of moisture in the air; too little or too much moisture is undesirable. Improper relative humidity is injurious to the health. By relative humidity is meant the amount of water vapor actually present in the air as compared with the maximum amount the air can hold at the given temperature and pressure. For example, a relative humidity of 

50% (the healthiest percentage) means that the water vapor content is 50% of the maximum amount (saturation) that the air can hold under the existing conditions of temperature and pressure. 

The modern system of air conditioning used in buildings and homes keeps the moisture content at the proper degree. 

2. Carbon Dioxide. This gas is always present in atmosphere in small amounts (about .04%). The importance of this compound to plant life and its relation to oxygen is discussed in Chapter 2, Oxygen, and in the next chapter. 

3. Rare Gases. Traces of five gases are found in the atmosphere.

They are called inert because of their total inactivity. There are no known compounds which they form. (Recent experiments, however, seem to indicate that some of these gases may react; this has not been conclusively proven.)

Argon-less than 1% by volume. Used to fill electric light bulbs.

Helium-next to hydrogen, the lightest gas known. It was first discovered by use of the spectroscope (see page 173) in the region of the sun (1868), then detected in air (1895). It is now obtained chiefly from natural gas in Texas. Being inert, it cannot be ignited and is therefore a safer gas than hydrogen for use in inflating balloons. Helium is also used with oxygen to make synthetic air. 

The mixture is used in deep-sea diving and compression chambers to prevent the "bends," which are caused by the expansion and liberation of nitrogen gas in the blood. 

Neon-An electric discharge passed through this gas causes it

THE ATMOSPHERE-NITROGEN 

119 to glow with an intense, bright-red color. Its greatest use at present is in electric signs.

Krypton and Xenon-the remaining inert gases have no practical uses.

Liquid Air. The principle followed in making liquid air is quite similar to that used in artificial refrigeration: cooling through expansion. As with ammonia (see page 127), air is compressed, cooled and allowed to expand through a small opening, the expansion absorbing heat and lowering the temperature. When a temperature of about —189°C. is reached, the air liquefies. Liquid air is a light-blue liquid slightly lighter than water. If kept confined, it is a dangerous explosive (result of expansion). Although used to create low temperatures, its primary industrial use is the preparation of oxygen, nitrogen, and the inert gases, which boil off at different temperatures when the liquid air is allowed to evaporate. As the temperature rises, nitrogen, having the lower boiling point, escapes, leaving nearly pure liquid oxygen. The oxygen in turn is evaporated and compressed in cylinders. Thus, in one operation, it is possible to obtain oxygen for welding and nitrogen for fixation. 

NITROGEN 

Occurrence of Nitrogen. In our study of oxygen and of air, we saw that oxygen constitutes about one-fifth of the atmosphere. 

Nitrogen in a free state comprises by far the greater part of the balance. Nitrogen is also found combined with other elements in chemical compounds, chiefly organic. All proteins contain nitrogen. A few mineral substances-nitrates also contain the element.

Preparation 

1. Nitrogen may readily be obtained from air by allowing the active oxygen to combine with other elements and then removing the oxide formed. (a) Air is passed over heated copper; the oxygen combines with the copper, leaving a gaseous residue which is chiefly nitrogen. 2Cu + O2 2CuO (b) If phosphorus is burned in a volume of air confined over water, the phosphorus oxide dissolves in the water.

The nitrogen obtained by the above methods contains small amounts of other substances (described later) which are present in air. 

2. Pure nitrogen may be obtained by decomposing ammonium nitrite with heat:

NH4NO2 N2 ↑ + 2H2O 3. Industrially, nitrogen is obtained in large quantities by liquefying air, then allowing the nitrogen to boil off from the mixture a process of distillation at low temperature. 

Properties of Nitrogen 1. Physical. Pure nitrogen has no outstanding physical properties by which it may be identified. It is a colorless, odorless, tasteless gas, slightly soluble in water and weighing a little less than air. 

Like other gases, it may be liquefied by compression and cooling. 

2. Chemical. Nitrogen does not support combustion, and burns in oxygen only with difficulty. One may think of this gas as an element with a great tendency to play a lone hand-it is difficult to unite it with other elements, and it is quite ready to break away from compounds which contain it. In fact, it is upon this last tendency that the very existence of compounds called explosives depends; their unstableness—or ability to decompose or explodeis dependent on the inertness of nitrogen. 

Uses of Nitrogen. Since nitrogen does not support combustion, it serves to counterbalance the activity of oxygen in air, preventing oxidation and combustion from proceeding with undesirable rapidity. It is used in electric light bulbs, allowing the filament to be heated to a high temperature without burning. In general, however, the practical uses of free nitrogen are limited. 

Fixed or Combined Nitrogen. Nitrogen compounds, unlike free nitrogen, are exceedingly valuable and widely used substances. 

As has been stated, nitrogen is an essential part of protein; all animal life requires nitrogen compounds in food to replace and build muscular (protein) tissue. The quantity of nitrogen we inhale and exhale daily is useless in this connection: free nitrogen cannot be absorbed by animal life. The nitrogen must be in a fixed form (combined with other elements). 

Plant life also requires nitrogen for growth and formation of protein compounds, and obtains it from the soil together with

THE ATMOSPHERE-NITROGEN 

121 water and mineral matter. Indirectly, then, we obtain the nitrogen needed through the intermediate stage of plant life. 

One may ask, "Is not the nitrogen in soil eventually used up?"

It would be, unless by some process, natural or artificial, the nitrogen in the soil is replaced. In nature, the constant decay of organic matter releases nitrogen compounds which are reabsorbed by the earth. Some plants themselves have the ability to fix the nitrogen of the air. Beans, peas, clover and other plants called legumes have packets or nodules of bacteria attached to their root formations which accomplish this result. The wise farmer rotates his crops, allowing the nitrogen withdrawn by one crop to be replaced by a crop of plants of the above type. Animal wastes, such as manure, also serve as a means of replenishing the nitrogen compounds. 

Some chemicals, such as Chile nitrate (NaNO3), are used as nitrogen-supplying fertilizers (see Chapter 23). 

Fixation of Free Nitrogen. As industrial chemistry developed and the density of population increased, the natural supplies of fixed nitrogen, aptly called "a nation's life-blood", fell short of the demand. For many years, the deposits of sodium nitrate in Chile formed a major part of the world's supply. The nations of the world felt they would be helpless should anything cut off the supply of Chile nitrate. Realizing the dangers of such dependence on one source, scientists sought a method of utilizing the abundant nitrogen available in the air. Just before the outbreak of the World 

War, a process was made practical. This process, the Haber 

Process, enabled Germany to continue the combat in spite of the 

Allied blockade. The several methods now used to fix nitrogen are described in detail in the next chapter but may be briefly mentioned here: 

1. Nitrogen is combined with hydrogen, forming ammoniathe Haber Process. 

2. Nitrogen, reacting with calcium carbide, yields calcium cyanamide. 

3. Nitrogen unites with oxygen, forming nitrogen oxides which, dissolved in water, will form nitric acid. 

The particular substance formed through the fixation of nitrogen is immaterial; once the nitrogen is in combination, other required compounds may readily be prepared.

CHAPTER 13  

In combination with other elements, nitrogen forms a large number of important compounds. We have already seen that salts called nitrates contain nitrogen. This element also unites with oxygen, forming a group of oxides; combines with hydrogen to form ammonia, and is present in nitrous and nitric acids. 

Ammonia, NH3. Ammonia is formed in nature by the decomposition of protein compounds. In the process of decomposition the nitrogen and hydrogen present combine to yield ammonia, together with other products. At one time this served as a commercial source of ammonia; animal horns and hoofs were distilled in closed vessels, and the compound formed was called "spirits of hartshorn," a term still used commercially for ammonia.

Preparation. At present, the supply of ammonia is obtained from several sources, among which are: 

1. Destructive Distillation of Soft Coal. When coke and coal gas are prepared by destructive distillation (see page 150), ammonia is formed as one of the by-products. This constitutes one of the largest sources of ammonia. 

2. Direct Combination. When an electric discharge is passed through a mixture of nitrogen and hydrogen, the gases combine in small amounts:

N2 + 3H2 → 2NH3 ↑ A more efficient process for the direct combination of these elements was devised by the German chemist, Haber, and is known by his name. Under high temperature and pressure, in the presence of a catalytic agent, the direct combination takes place. The temperature and pressure must be carefully controlled or the action may reverse. 

3. Other Methods of Preparation 

(a) Ammonia is also prepared by the use of calcium carbide, 124

CaC2. When this compound is heated, nitrogen from the air combines with it, forming calcium cyanamide: CaC₂+N2 - CaCN2 + C Steam is added to the compound formed, causing the following change:

CaCN2+ 3H2O 2NH3 ↑ + CaCO3 2NH3+ AND

NH4CI 

Ca(OH)2 NH3 2NH4CI+Ca(OH)2 CaCl2+2NH₂+2H₂O 

Laboratory Preparation of Ammonia Gas 

(b) Laboratory Method. When an ammonium salt is heated with a base, ammonia is obtained. 2NH4C1+ Ca(OH)2 - 2NH3 + 2H2O + CaCl2 

Ammonium sulphate may also be used as the ammonium salt; any strong base will serve in place of the calcium hydroxide: (NH4)2SO4 + 2NaOH 2NH3 + 2H2O + Na2SO4 The ammonia formed is collected by downward displacement of air. 

Properties of Ammonia 

1. Physical. Two properties of ammonia are cutstanding: its

penetrating, pungent odor, and its great solubility in water. Under ordinary conditions, one volume of water will dissolve over seven hundred times its volume of the gas, forming ammonium hydroxide.

NH3 + H2O → NH4OH

A diluted solution of ammonium hydroxide (NH4OH) is known as household ammonia, or ammonia water. The gas can be driven from the solution by heating; this serves as a method of obtaining small quantities of the gas.

Ammonia is a colorless gas. It is lighter than air, making its collection by downward displacement of air possible. 

2. Chemical. Pure ammonia has little chemical activity; it is comparatively inert. When dissolved in water (see above) the compound formed, NH4OH, is a strong base, turning litmus blue and neutralizing acids. -> 

NH4OH + HCI NH4Cl + H2O

The formation of ammonium hydroxide is more than a physical solution of the gas. Chemical action takes place, in which the ammonia unites with a hydrogen atom of the water, forming the ammonium radical, which shows up when ammonium hydroxide ionizes: 

NH4OH NH4+ + OH 

Uses of Ammonia 

1. Refrigeration. Ammonia gas may be readily liquefied by pressure and cooling. When allowed to evaporate (expand) to its normal state (gaseous) it absorbs large quantities of heat. Because of this action, ammonia is extensively used as a refrigerant in icemaking and cold storage plants. The process followed involves two steps: (a) compressing and cooling the ammonia, thus liquefying it; (b) allowing the ammonia to expand, as a result of which expansion it absorbs heat and thus cools the surrounding region. 

In the apparatus shown, ammonia is compressed by a pump and cooled by water, whereupon it changes to the liquid state. An expansion valve allows the liquid ammonia to evaporate in pipes placed in the region to be cooled. The ammonia is then recompressed and the cycle repeated. 

The same process is used in mechanical refrigerators for home

use. Other refrigerants which may be more easily liquefied are employed but the processes are all similar. 

2. Cleansing Agent. It has been previously stated that bases possess the ability to emulsify and dissolve grease and fats. Since ammonia in water solution has basic properties, it acts similarly, and is widely used as a cleansing agent in removing grease spots 1

How Ammonia is Used as a Refrigerant from fabrics. It is also used in washing windows and glassware to remove any film of grease which would give a cloudy appearance to the glass surface. Ammonium hydroxide has an added advantage over other bases in that it evaporates after emulsifying the grease, and thus leaves no residue. 

Test for Ammonia. Ammonia in the free state is easily detected by its odor. To determine the presence of ammonia (or rather, the ammonium radical) in a chemical compound, the compound is heated with a base. The odor of ammonia formed proves the presence of the radical.

Nitrogen Oxides. In combination with oxygen, nitrogen forms the following oxides: 

1. Nitrous Oxide (N2O). This is a colorless gas with a somewhat sweet odor, used as an anaesthetic and commonly called "laughing gas." It is prepared by heating ammonium nitrate:

NH4NO3 N2O + 2H2O

Like oxygen, but to a lesser degree, nitrous oxide supports combustion. 

2. Nitric Oxide (NO). This is a colorless gas, insoluble in water, which is formed when nitric acid acts on copper (see below). 

When exposed to air or oxygen, nitric oxide unites with another oxygen atom forming nitrogen peroxide. 

3. Nitrogen Peroxide (NO2). This is a heavy, reddish-brown gas, very soluble in water. NO2 has a pungent, suffocating odor, and is poisonous. The reaction for its formation is as follows: 

2NOO2 - 2NO2 

Of lesser importance are nitrogen trioxide (N2O3), and nitrogen pentoxide (N2O5), which, when reacting with water, form nitrous and nitric acids, respectively. For this reason, they are also called nitrous and nitric anhydrides. 

NITRIC ACID 

This acid, almost indispensable to chemical industry, has been used for hundreds of years. The alchemists studied its properties and named it Aqua Fortis (strong water) because of its great chemical activity.

Commercial Preparation 

1. From Air and Water. If NO2 is dissolved in water, a mixture of nitrous and nitric acids is obtained: 2NO2 + H2O HNO2 + HNO3 The HNO2 is further oxidized to nitric acid. The nitrogen peroxide for this process is prepared by passing an electric discharge through air forming NO:

N2O22NO The nitric oxide (NO) is then oxidized to NO2 by addition of more oxygen. Where electric power is abundant and cheap, this furnishes a practical process for the preparation of nitric acid. 

2. From Ammonia. When a mixture of ammonia and oxygen is blown through a heated platinum screen, the ammonia is oxidized to nitric acid:

NH3+202 HNO3 + H2O

3. From a Nitrate. This commercial process is identical in chemical action with the laboratory method. 

Laboratory Preparation. Following the general method of preparing acids (see page 81), we may prepare nitric acid by using a salt to supply the acid radical, and sulphuric acid to give the hydrogen. A mixture of sodium nitrate and concentrated H2SO4 is heated in a retort and the nitric acid which is formed distills off and is condensed in a cooled vessel. The equation is as follows: NaNO3 + H2SO4 NaHSO4 + HNO3 H₂SO NaNO3 COLD 

WATER 

HNO3 NaNO₂+H₂SO HNO3 + NaHSO4 

Laboratory Preparation of Nitric Acid 

Because of the activity of nitric acid, the apparatus used is entirely of glass construction, since cork or rubber disintegrate when in contact with the acid.

Physical Properties. Pure nitric acid is a colorless liquid, although the commercial product is often yellowish in color, a result of the presence of dissolved nitrogen oxides. It is heavier than water, and in the commercial diluted solution boils slightly above the boiling point of water. 

Chemical Properties 

1. Oxidizing Action. Nitric acid, when reduced, readily liberates oxygen and consequently acts as an energetic oxidizing agent. 

Glowing charcoal and wood fiber burn actively in the acid vapor.

In all common actions of metals with nitric acid, hydrogen is never given off; the hydrogen in solution is oxidized to water. 

2. Intense Activity. Nitric is the most active of acids, due to its high degree of ionization. Since the activity (or "strength") of an acid is dependent on the degree of dissociation, nitric acid may be called the strongest of common acids. 

3. Action on Protein. A common test for protein is the yellow color formed when HNO3 is brought in contact with protein matter. The yellow stain on the fingers when the acid is spilled is characteristic of this change. TEHNO CU 

WATER NITRIC 

OXIDE 

WATER 

3 Cu+8HNO3- 3Cu(NO3)2+4H₂O+2NO 

Laboratory Preparation of Nitric Oxide 

4. Action with Metals. Since nitric acid is reduced when it reacts with metals, the reactions are complex and the compounds formed vary according to the metal used and the concentration of the acid. In general, nitric acid reacting with a metal yields three main products: a salt of the metal, water, and a nitrogen oxide. 

When dilute HNO3 acts on copper, the equation is: 3Cu+8HNO33Cu(NO3)2 + 4H2O + 2NO ↑ 

With concentrated acid, NO2 is formed instead of NO: 

Cu + 4HNO3 Cu(NO3)2 + 2H2O + 2NO2 ↑

With metals of greater activity than copper, the reaction with HNO3 is likely to be violent; other products, such as ammonium compounds, may form.

Gold and platinum are not acted on by nitric acid, but will dissolve in a mixture of one part nitric acid and three parts hydrochloric acid (Aqua Regia). In this mixture, the HCl is oxidized, forming nascent chlorine, which attacks the metal. 

Test for Nitrate Ion. The presence of the nitrate ion (NO3) in solution can be detected by mixing ferrous sulphate with the solution being tested, then slowly adding concentrated sulphuric acid. 

The formation of a dark-brown ring where the heavy layer of H2SO4 meets the remainder of the solution is a test for the nitrate ion-the color coming from nitrogen oxides formed by reduction of the NO3 group. 

Uses of Nitric Acid. Nitric acid finds its greatest use in the chemistry of modern explosives and synthetic dyes. A majority of powerful explosives are prepared by the action of nitric acid on a wide variety of substances. Nitric acid and cellulose form nitrocellulose or gun-cotton; nitric acid and glycerine yield nitroglycerine; nitric acid and toluol form T.N.T., or trinitrotoluol, and many others.

Other uses include the preparation of nitrates by neutralization of bases with nitric acid, separation of gold from silver in refining these metals, and etching of designs on metal surfaces.

CHAPTER 14  CARBON 

Occurrence. Carbon, like many other elements, is found in both free and combined states. All plant and animal life contains carbon in the tissue structure. Many minerals have carbon as part of their chemical composition. Gases, such as carbon dioxide and methane, also contain a large proportion of carbon. In the free state, carbon exists in crystalline form, in which it exhibits definite crystalline structure, and in an amorphous state. Carbon, therefore, is an element (like sulphur and oxygen) which possesses allotropic forms. 

Crystalline Forms 1. Diamond. Pure crystallized carbon, in colors from black to pure white, is known as the diamond, which is valued both as a gem and as an abrasive. Diamonds occur free, sometimes on the surface of the earth, but mostly buried deep under ground. The diamond fields in South Africa are the largest source. It is the hardest substance known.

When cut to form numerous facets on its surface, the diamond disperses light rays into a brilliant play of colors. Its high index of refraction (light-bending power) aids in producing this effect. 

Diamonds of inferior appearance, of little value as gems, find extensive use because of their hardness. They are employed for rock drills, dies for drawing wire, and in the construction of measuring gauges which can withstand wear and remain accurate indefinitely. 

2. Graphite. Unlike the diamond, the crystallized form of carbon called graphite is soft, greasy to touch and less dense than the diamond. Careful examination shows graphite to be composed of tiny plates or scales which slide over one another freely, producing the greasy effect. This property makes graphite a useful lubricating material. When used for this purpose, graphite is commonly mixed with grease or oil, or even held as a suspension in water. 134

Graphite, like the diamond, will burn if the temperature is sufficiently high but it does not melt. Graphite is used in crucibles for melting metals. Metals will melt at a temperature much lower than the ignition point of graphite. 

A lump of graphite drawn across paper will leave a mark. 

This is due to the rubbing off of graphite crystals. Graphite, mixed with clay to secure the proper degree of hardness, is used in the manufacture of pencils. Since graphite conducts electricity and also withstands high temperatures, it is used for electrodes in electric furnaces.

Although graphite is found in free state as a natural mineral, most of the amount used is manufactured artificially by heating anthracite coal or coke in an electric furnace.

Amorphous Forms. Amorphous carbon takes the following important commercial forms: 

1. Coal. Both anthracite (hard) and bituminous (soft) coal contain a large amount of free carbon, anthracite having the greater proportion. The carbon of coal (and, for that matter, of other fuels, such as peat and lignite) is really the residue of vegetable matter which fossilized during the earlier stages of the earth's formation. Great quantities of wood fiber (cellulose, C6H1005) buried under layers of earth and subjected to heat and pressure, released most of the hydrogen and oxygen it contained leaving nearly pure carbon. In anthracite coal the formation process has been more complete; greater quantities of the gases were lost and the percentage of carbon is therefore higher. 

2. Coke. Bituminous coal, strongly heated without access to air, yields coke and a large quantity of gaseous and liquid by-products, such as coal gas (see page 150), coal tar, and ammonia (see page 124). This process is known as destructive distillation. 

Coke is nearly pure carbon, forming as a grayish, porous, hard mass. It is widely used as a fuel, giving intense heat with little smoke or flame. In the extraction of metals from ores, coke is used as a reducing agent, removing the oxygen from the metallic oxides, leaving the free metal (see page 167). 

3. Charcoal. This is prepared by destructively distilling wood, just as coke is obtained from bituminous coal. Wood charcoal is the residue. If bones are treated in a similar manner, a form of amorphous carbon called animal charcoal or boneblack is produced.

This form of charcoal finds use in filtering liquids, since its extreme porosity enables it to collect and hold coloring matter, solids and gases which might cause undesirable odors. 

4. Lampblack. If oil is burned with a limited air supply, or if the carbon of a gas flame is deposited upon a cold surface, lampblack is formed. The soot which collects on the chimney of a smoking lamp or lantern chimney is pure lampblack. It is commonly used as a pigment in paint manufacture, and in making carbon inks.

Useful Carbon Compounds. Carbon, like sulphur, is an amphoteric element, acting both as a metal (valence +4) and as a nonmetal (valence -4). At ordinary temperatures, carbon is practically inert; at moderately high temperatures it will oxidize; but in the intense heat developed by electric furnaces it will unite with many elements, forming compounds of great industrial importance.

Carborundum, silicon carbide (SiC), is made by the union of carbon (acting as a non-metal) and silicon (from coke and sand, SiO2) at high temperature:

SiO2 + 3C SiC + 2CO ↑ 

This is an extremely hard compound, widely used as an abrasive in grinding wheels, sharpening stones and powder. 

When heated with lime, (CaO), carbon (again acting as a nonmetal) forms calcium carbide, CaC2. Calcium carbide reacts with water forming acetylene gas, used for illumination and as a fuel in welding (see page 155).

At high temperatures, carbon (acting as a metal) unites with both sulphur and chlorine to yield (1) carbon disulphide, CS2, an excellent solvent of rubber and gums (rubber cement is rubber dissolved in CS2); (2) carbon tetrachloride, CCl4, used as fire extinguisher and as a safe, non-inflammable solvent for cleaning clothes. 

Summary of Carbon Properties. Although the forms of carbon may vary greatly in appearance and structure, certain properties are common to most forms: 1

1. Insolubility; carbon is unaffected by all ordinary solvents. 

2. Carbon, an amphoteric element, is inert at ordinary temperatures, but active at high temperatures. 

3. It is a strong reducing agent (see later chapters discussing reduction of metal ores to obtain metals, especially the reduction of iron oxide to obtain iron).

CARBON DIOXIDE 

Occurrence. Carbon dioxide, CO2, is formed when substances containing carbon are oxidized, as in the combustion of fuels and the decay of animal and vegetable matter. This gas is present in

Exhaled breath of animals contains CO2, which is formed by the oxidation of carbon in the body tissues. Small amounts of carbon dioxide (.04%) are always present in the atmosphere. 

Preparation of Carbon Dioxide 

1. Product of Combustion. As just stated, the combustion of fuels yields carbon dioxide as one of the products formed. In this process, the oxygen unites directly with carbon at high temperatures:

CO2 CO2 ↑

In ordinary combustion, the carbon dioxide given off is not pure, since other combustion products are also formed. Industrially, large quantities of the gas are obtained by this method; "flue gas" is treated to extract the carbon dioxide, which is then purified. 

2. Decomposition of Carbonates (a) With Acids. This method is most commonly used for the laboratory preparation of carbon dioxide. When a carbonate of one of the more active metals (such as Na, K, Ca) is treated with a dilute acid, carbon dioxide is formed as one of the reaction products. Usually, limestone, CaCO3, is used with HCl or H2SO4. 

The CaCO3 in finely divided form (limestone or marble chips) is placed in a flask, and the dilute acid added through a thistle tube. 

Carbon dioxide is evolved with a rapid bubbling action or effervescence.

CaCO3 + 2HC1 CaCl2 + H2O + CO2 ↑ (b) By Heat. CaCO3 may be decomposed by heating. 

Since intense heat is required for this decomposition, this process is not practical as a source of CO2. CaCO3 →> CaO + CO, Â 1 

Properties of Carbon Dioxide 1. Physical. Carbon dioxide, one of the heaviest of common gases, is nearly one and one-half times heavier than air. It is colorless and, when pure, tasteless and odorless as well. The gas is not very soluble in water, but its solubility increases under pressure, forming carbonated or "soda" water. CO2 liquefies readily under moderately high pressure. 

2. Chemical. Carbon dioxide is quite inert; it does not burn or support combustion. It will, however, react with bases, forming salts. This serves as a test for CO2. When limewater (Ca(OH)2) is the base used and carbon dioxide is passed through limewater, a milky white precipitate is formed.

Ca(OH)2 + CO2 CaCO3 + H2O This test may also be used to indicate the presence of the carbonate radical in a chemical compound. An acid is added to liberate the CO2, which is then passed through the limewater. 

Uses of Carbon Dioxide 

1. Plant Life.

The carbon dioxide formed as a product of

oxidation would, if not disposed of, increase the amount of CO2 in air far above the normal amount. Plants serve to maintain a balance between carbon dioxide and oxygen. In the presence of sunlight, the chlorophyl contained in green plant leaves acts as a catalyst, causing CO2 to combine with moisture, forming starch and liberating oxygen to the air: 6CO2 + 5H2O ISTAKEN 

C6H10O5+ 602 (Process of Photosynthesis) (starch)

Carbon Dioxide-Oxygen Cycle

This natural chemical process has a twofold value to animal life: It supplies oxygen for respiration and forms starch for food. 

2. "Soda Water"—Carbonated Beverages. Carbon dioxide may be dissolved in water under pressure. When the pressure is released, the gas escapes with a frothing or effervescing action, as can be noticed when a bottle of carbonated beverage is opened. In soda fountains, the water is charged with CO2, which is supplied from a cylinder of the liquefied gas. Some beverages, such as champagne or sparkling wines, are also charged by carbon dioxide, but in this case the CO2 was formed as a product of fermentation. 

3. Leavening Agent. În the baking of bread and cake, CO2 is employed to leaven (or lighten) the dough, which otherwise would form a heavy, soggy mass. The gas may be generated either by de-

composition of a carbonate, as in the use of baking powder, or by fermentation of sugars by the action of yeast.

Baking powder consists essentially of sodium bicarbonate with an acid ingredient (cream of tartar, alum, or monocalcium phosphate). The action of cream of tartar may be considered typical. 

In the presence of moisture, the acid salt and bicarbonate react: NaHCO3 + HKC4H406 NaKC4H4O6+ H2O + CO2 ↑ Sodium bicarbonate

Fire Extinguisher 

4. Fire Extinguishing. Since carbon dioxide does not support combustion and is heavier than air, it serves as an important agent in extinguishing fires. CO2 directed at a fire will form, in effect, a heavy blanket of inert gas which excludes oxygen and prevents combustion. In the fire extinguisher of the CO2-acid type, a solution of sodium bicarbonate is acted upon by sulphuric acid when the extinguisher is inverted. A mixture of carbon dioxide and water is forced a considerable distance by the pressure of the gas generated: 2NaHCO3 + H2SO4 2CO2 ↑ + 2H2O + Na2SO4 

---

Naturally, a strong wind or the draft of a hot fire may disperse the CO2 "blanket". A more effective method of using the gas is in the Foamite system, in which the CO2 is entrapped in minute gas bubbles in a non-combustible, frothy mass. This is achieved by the use of aluminum sulphate and a colloidal substance (licorice extract) with NaHCO3. This method is particularly effective in extinguishing oil fires and for use on materials which would be damaged by water. 

5. Refrigeration. At a sufficiently low temperature, carbon dioxide solidifies, forming a white solid at a temperature of —72° C. Under the name of "dry ice" this solid is widely used as a refrigerant, as it not only maintains a far lower temperature than ice but also evaporates without leaving any liquid residue, as does ice. 

6. Resuscitation. When the lungs contain air having a high percentage of carbon dioxide, deep breathing is induced-as shown by the deep breaths one draws after prolonged physical exertion. If a victim of asphyxiation is allowed to breathe air or oxygen rich in CO2, deep respiration is induced and recovery facilitated. Cylinders of oxygen with 7% carbon dioxide are sold for this purpose under the name of "Carbogen".

CARBON MONOXIDE 

Preparation. Carbon monoxide may be prepared by: 

1. Reduction of CO2: Passed over strongly heated carbon, which is a powerful reducing agent, CO2 is reduced to CO: CO2 + C → 2CO ↑ 2. Combustion of Carbon with Insufficient Oxygen Supply. 

When the oxygen supplied to a fire is insufficient in quantity to completely oxidize the carbon, CO is formed. 2C + O2 → 2CO ↑ This action frequently takes place when fresh fuel is added to a fire and the drafts immediately closed. In furnace or stove operation, it represents a fuel waste; the fire should be allowed to burn through the fresh fuel to insure igniting and burning any CO which may form. 

3. Chemical Decomposition (Laboratory Method). Hot, concentrated H2SO4 will react with oxalic acid as follows:

C2H2O4 + H2SO4 CO ↑ + CO2 ↑ + H2O + H2SO4 

Oxalic acid

The sulphuric acid acts merely as a dehydrating agent, removing one molecule of water. The resulting decomposition yields a mixture of CO and CO2; the CO2 may be absorbed by limewater, leaving pure CO.. 

Properties of Carbon Monoxide

Physical. Like CO2, CO is colorless, odorless and tasteless, but differs in all other respects. It is lighter than air and practically insoluble in water.

Chemical. Carbon monoxide has one outstanding chemical property: its ability to unite with oxygen. It is, therefore, combustible, burning with a pale, nearly colorless and hot flame: 2CO 02 → 2CO2 ↑ Carbon monoxide will reduce CO2 (see above) and metallic oxides:

Fe2O3 + 3CO2Fe + 3CO2 ↑ 

Uses of Carbon Monoxide. The combustibility and reducing action just stated comprise the chief uses of CO. Many fuel gases (coal, water and producer gases) contain CO in varying quantities. 

In extracting metals from oxide ores, CO serves as a valuable reducing agent, uniting with the oxygen at a high temperature and freeing the metal.

Poisonous Action. Carbon monoxide, when inhaled, is probably the most dangerous of common gases. Its lack of odor and color make it nearly impossible to detect, and its chemical activity causes it to combine with the red corpuscles of the blood. This reduces the ability of the blood to carry oxygen to the tissues. 

The asphyxiation which results is actually "oxygen-starvation" of the tissues. The compound formed by the action of CO with the blood is extremely stable, hence a very small amount inhaled accumulates until fatal poisoning may be caused; as little as 1 part in 

1000 in air may quickly produce death.

Some of the common sources of CO in everyday life are: exhaust gas of automobiles, which may contain 10-15% CO; gas heaters for hot water tanks, which should have flue connections since there is danger of reducing CO2 to CO in this device; and coal gas, which contains a high percentage of CO.
