CHAPTER 15 LIQUID AND GASEOUS FUELS 

Advantages of Liquid and Gaseous Fuels. Recent years have been marked by a steady trend toward the increased use of fuels in the liquid or gaseous form. Although as a rule more costly than coal and other solid fuels, the advantages of the liquids and gasesease of handling and transportation-go far to offset the added cost. Again, liquid fuels yield more heat per unit volume, requiring smaller storage space. Ships using oil fuel thus have more cargo space or greater cruising radius than coal burners. Finally, labor costs in handling and firing with oil are reduced to a minimum.

Petroleum. Petroleum (or "crude oil") is found in widely distributed locations, the most important sources of supply being the United States, Mexico, Russia, the Balkan States and Asia Minor. 

Subjected to fractional distillation, this natural oil yields most of the common liquid fuels in use. Chemically, petroleum is a mixture of hydrocarbon compounds (page 154) having different boiling points. In the refining of petroleum, this process of fractional distillation separates the crude oil into various products or fractions according to their respective boiling points, or volatility. 

The most important liquid products of petroleum are: 

1. Gasoline, the most volatile part (lowest boiling point). 

2. Kerosene 

3. Benzene 

4. Light 

5. Heavy 

Less volatile lubricating oil Vaseline, paraffin and gas coke are other substances obtained. 

Petroleum refining involves more than merely separating the crude oil into fractions. Undesirable impurities, such as sulphur compounds and organic matter, which would give offensive odors when burned, must be removed by suitable processes. Paraffin is obtained by chilling the lubricating oil fractions, then filtering to 148

LIQUID AND GASEOUS FUELS 

149 separate the congealed solid from the liquid (now) "wax free" oil. 

Oil coke, useful in making electrodes, forms a hard mass in the still. 

Cracking Process. In order to increase the yield of the more volatile fraction, gasoline, a process known as "cracking" is employed. By the use of high temperature and pressure, the larger molecules of the heavier hydrocarbons are broken down into smaller molecules, similar (although not identical) to those composing the natural gasoline. This process makes possible a much greater supply of gasoline than would be available by fractional distillation alone.

Many brands of gasoline at present are treated with chemical compounds, such as tetraethyl lead, (C2H5)4Pb, to reduce the rapidity of fuel combustion in engine cylinders, and prevent knocking.

Kerosene. Kerosene is a mixture of heavier hydrocarbon molecules. It is less volatile than gasoline and safer to use as fuel in stoves. The combustion of kerosene, however, tends to be less complete, and deposits of carbon may be formed so that the flame is likely to be smoky. As an illuminant, kerosene is the most common liquid fuel in use. Some internal combustion engines operate on kerosene; these are usually started and warmed up on gasoline. 

Fuel Oil. Fuel oil is a cheap grade of petroleum by-product.

When atomized with compressed air, it forms a highly combustible vapor, and is used for furnace fuel. Its low cost makes it available for uses where gasoline and kerosene would be too expensive; its low volatility makes it a safe substance to handle and store.

Diesel and "heavy oil" engines are commonly operated on fuel oil. 

Alcohols. Ethyl and methyl alcohol (see next chapter) are useful for fuels on a small scale, but the higher cost makes their use in large quantities prohibitive. They supply a clean, intense heat when used in special burners.

GASES 

Natural Gas. Natural gas is chiefly methane (CH4) and small quantities of other hydrocarbon compounds. Like petroleum, with which it often occurs, natural gas is obtained by drilling wells down to the rock formations in which the gas is confined. It has a

high heating value as a fuel; on combustion, carbon dioxide and water are given off.

CH4 +202 2H2O + CO2 ↑ Coal Gas. When bituminous coal is destructively distilled, coal gas is formed as one of the products. Other valuable substances formed during the process are coal tar (page 135), ammonia, and the carbon residue, coke.

The destructive distillation of coal is carried on in tall narrow iron ovens arranged in groups or batteries. The volatile products of the distillation contain the coal gas, tar and ammonia. Several steps of purification are necessary to remove the latter two substances from the gas. 

1. The vapors from the oven first pass through the hydraulic main (a trough of running water) where some of the tar is extracted. 

2. Large condensers and tar extractors cool the gas and remove the remainder of the tar. 

3. In scrubbers, the gas is agitated or sprayed with water to dissolve the ammonia. 

4. Purifiers containing iron ore or lime remove the sulphur compounds which otherwise would form sulphur dioxide when the gas is burned.

Coal gas is used for fuel and illumination, since it burns with a bright flame. In some regions it is mixed with natural gas to augment the supply of the latter gas.

Water Gas. By heating coal or coke to incandescence, then blowing steam through the hot carbon, water gas is produced: C +H2O → CO 1 +H2 1 As the equation shows, water gas is a mixture chiefly of carbon monoxide and hydrogen, both of which burn with a hot, colorless flame. It is therefore an excellent fuel gas and can be made more cheaply than coal gas: a volume of water gas several times that of coal gas can be obtained from a given weight of carbon. 

Since water gas burns with a colorless flame, it cannot be used for illumination in open burners unless enriched by the addition of oil vapors. In some methods of water gas manufacture, oil is sprayed into the heated gas; the oil vaporizes and mixes with the gas, forming illuminating or enriched water gas.

LIQUID AND GASEOUS FUELS 

151 Producer Gas. Steel plants and other industries make use of producer gas, which can be cheaply prepared from inferior grades of fuel. When air is blown through a heated mass of carbon, carbon dioxide is first formed: this compound is then reduced by the excess of carbon:

CO2 CO2 ↑ CO,+C → 2 CO 1 The producer gas formed is chiefly carbon monoxide and nitrogen. It has a lower heat value than water gas, but the low cost of production makes it a valuable industrial fuel. 

Acetylene. C2H2 (see page 155), when burned with oxygen in the oxy-acetylene torch, gives an intensely hot flame suitable for welding and cutting metals. In a burner designed to admit a sufficient supply of air, acetylene burns with a brilliant flame and is used for illumination. 2C2H2 + 502 2H2O + 4CO2 ↑

CHAPTER 16 ORGANIC CHEMISTRY 

Historical Introduction. In the early days of chemistry, the compounds containing carbon were believed to be derived only from organized forms of life. This belief was upset in 1828 by the 

German chemist, Woehler, who proved it possible to synthesize organic compounds. However, the term "organic" has continued to be used in reference to compounds which contain carbon in combination with other elements. A few carbon compounds (such as the carbonates) are studied in connection with inorganic compounds.

At the present time, it has been determined that there are more carbon or organic compounds than there are compounds of all other elements combined. One fact alone accounts for this: unlike most other elements, carbon atoms have the power of uniting with each other, as well as with other elements. This makes possible the formation of a great number of compounds; over 

200,000 have been identified and studied. These compounds have been classified; some of the more important groupings are hydrocarbons, alcohols, acids, esters, aldehydes, and carbohydrates. 

Structural Formulas. In studying organic compounds, it is desirable to use a formula arrangement which gives more informa- OH OH H2SO4 Structural Formula of H2SO4 tion than does the customary or empirical formula. 

The formula H2SO4, for example, tells only the elements present and the number of atoms of each. 

In structural formulas commonly used to represent organic compounds, the atom arrangement in the molecule (as generally accepted) and also the valence of the elements is shown. Valence is represented by lines or bonds joining the elements: NaOH becomes Na-O-H. Carbon has a valence of 4 and may be represented structurally as - 153

Hydrocarbons. Hydrogen and carbon combine in varying atomic proportions and arrangements to form the very important group of organic compounds called hydrocarbons. Hydrocarbons are divided into different series, named after the simplest compound in the group. Each member of a series has a general structural relationship with the next, as we shall see later. One of the most important of the hydrocarbon groups is the methane series. 

Methane. Methane occurs to some extent in nature as a product of decay of plant life. It is often found in swamps and marshy places, hence its common name "marsh gas." The compound is colorless, odorless and burns readily in air. Natural gas contains over 90% methane. Combined with air, methane forms an explosive gas mixture (fire damp) which constitutes a source of fire hazard in coal mines. 

Structure of the Methane (Paraffin) Series. If hydrogen atoms are united to each valence bond of the carbon atom, the compound formed is H 

C—H or methane.

Since carbon atoms unite di- rectly with each other, two carbon atoms in combination would have six valences available for union with hydrogen: C-C-; H H the compound then is H-C-C-H, C2H6, or ethane.

Simi- H H larly, three carbon atoms form propane, C3H8. As many as 60 or more carbon atoms may be thus joined to form the higher compounds of this series. In general, as the number of carbon atoms in the compound increases, the physical state changes from gas, through the liquid to the solid state. 

Saturated and Unsaturated Compounds. In all compounds of the methane series, the carbon atoms are united by the minimum of one bond. All the other bonds are used to hold hydrogen atoms. 

Compounds of such a series are called saturated. In other groups,

ORGANIC CHEMISTRY 

155 two or even three bonds may join adjacent carbon atoms; these are called unsaturated compounds. Following are two examples of unsaturated series. 

Ethylene Series. Ethylene, (C2H4), the first of this series, is

H H arranged C = C.

Ethylene is a gas which burns with a bright flame, H H and is used as an illuminant. 

Acetylene Series. Acetylene (C2H2), begins another series in which the carbon atoms are united by three bonds: H-C=C-H. 

Pure acetylene burns with a smoky flame and has a disagreeable, nauseating odor. It is prepared by the action of water on calcium carbide:

CaC2 + 2H2O C2H2 + Ca(OH)2 When burned with oxygen in the oxy-acetylene torch, it yields a high temperature and is employed in cutting and welding metals. 

As a gaseous illuminant, acetylene is unsurpassed, giving a brilliant flame when used with a burner designed for complete combustion of the gas.

Homologous Sefies. It will be noted that each compound in the methane series differs from the preceding one by the group CH2. When compounds in a series bear a general structural relationship to each other, and differ by a definite group (such as CH2 in the methane series), the series is called homologous. It is possible to calculate the formula of any compound in a series, if the number of carbon atoms is known. In the series just studied, the general formula for each is:

Series 

1. Methane 

2. Ethylene 

3. Acetylene

General Formula 

CnH2n+2 CnH2n CnH2n-2 (n represents any number of C atoms. Series 2 and 3 cannot have less than two C atoms in a compound.) 

Benzene Series. In numerous organic compounds, the carbon atoms, instead of forming a straight line or chain (as in the paraffin

series) unite in a hexagonal arrangement. These compounds are generally known as "ring" or aromatic compounds, as contrasted with the paraffin or "chain" compounds.

Benzene, (or Benzol) C6H6, is the simplest compound of this group, having the structural arrangement shown in the diagram. H-C HC. C-H Benzol Molecule

Benzene is obtained as a by-product of the destructive distillation of bituminous coal. It is a colorless liquid with a distinctive odor, and volatilizes readily. Mixed with air, it forms a highly explosive mixture; it has been successfully used as motor fuel. Commercially, benzene serves as a basic compound in the manufacture of organic compounds such as explosives and dyestuffs (see below for list of benzene derivatives).

Replacement or Substitution Products. By replacing one or more hydrogen atoms in organic compounds with various chemical elements or groups, a large number of important compounds is obtained. The compounds so prepared are called "substitution products." These compounds are often named from the method of replacement. 

Methane, CH4

H-C-H 

Trichlor-methane, CHCl3

CL-C-CI C1

Chloroform or trichlor-methane, is an oily liquid with a sweetish odor. It is used as a solvent and as an anaesthetic. 

Iodoform, CHI3, is a yellow, crystalline solid with an unpleasant odor. It is useful as an antiseptic.

Carbon tetrachloride or tetrachlor-methane, CCl4, may be considered to be a substitution product of methane in which all four atoms of hydrogen have been replaced by chlorine. The compound is an effective solvent for use in cleaning fabrics, and is safer than gasoline since it is non-explosive. It is used in fire extinguishers of the "pyrene" type.

Alcohols. This class of organic compounds contains one (or more) OH radicals replacing hydrogen atoms in a hydrocarbon. 

An alcohol may therefore be considered an Organic Hydroxide. 

Methyl Alcohol. Methyl alcohol, CH3OH, is a substitution product of methane. Commercially it is produced by the destructive distillation of wood, which has given this compound the common name of "wood alcohol." It is also known as methanol. A recently developed process (combining carbon monoxide and hydrogen) produces synthetic methyl alcohol cheaper than the original method of wood distillation, and is rapidly replacing the former method.

Methanol or wood alcohol is extremely poisonous. When pure, it has a pleasant odor, but the commercial form has a rather acrid odor. The predominant uses of methanol are: solvent, in the preparation of shellac and lacquers; fuel, since it burns with a clean, colorless and hot flame; denaturing ethyl alcohol to prevent the use of the latter substance as a beverage; and as an anti-freeze in automobile radiators. 

Ethyl Alcohol. Ethyl alcohol, C2H5OH, may be considered a substitution product of ethane. It is obtained when sugar is fermented by the action of yeast plants, which secrete an organic

catalyst or enzyme called zymase. Carbon dioxide is given off during the reaction:

C6H12O62 C2H5OH + 2 CO2 ↑ sugar alcohol

The alcohol formed is obtained from the mixture and concentrated by distillation. In the commercial manufacture of ethyl alcohol, substances containing starch (chiefly grain and potatoes) are first changed to sugar by fermentation or hydrolysis (see page 162). The sugar is then fermented. This constitutes a cheap source of alcohol, making possible its extensive commercial uses. 

Properties and Uses of Ethyl Alcohol. Ethyl or "grain" alcohol is a colorless liquid; has a pleasant odor, and burns with a colorless flame, forming water and carbon dioxide:

C2H5OH + 3 023 H2O + 2 CO2 ↑ Taken internally, it is mildly poisonous in its action. Like methyl alcohol, ethyl is used as a solvent and for fuel. 

Since it can be cheaply made, its use as motor fuel is often proposed, offering a means of disposal for surplus crops. 

An important use of ethyl alcohol is in alcoholic beverages. In fermented liquors (beer and wine) the alcohol content may vary from 4 to 18%, as produced by the fermentation process directly. 

Beverages containing higher amounts of alcohol are distilled from the fermented mixture, and may contain 50% or more ethyl alcohol. The term proof in relation to distilled liquors indicates double the alcoholic percentage; that is, 100 proof equals 50% alcohol.

To make ethyl alcohol unfit for beverage purposes (and avoid government tax on this form of alcohol) small quantities of poisonous or unpalatable substances (such as wood alcohol, benzene and others) are added to ethyl alcohol. This alcohol (called denatured alcohol) can then be sold for industrial use without restriction. 

Glycerol. Glycerol, or glycerine, is an alcohol containing three OH radicals: C3H5(OH)3. It is a clear, syrupy liquid having a sweet taste. Large quantities are used in the manufacture of the explosive nitro-glycerine; other uses are for cosmetic lotions, medicines, and leather dressing.

Phenol. Phenol, commonly called carbolic acid, is described in the table on page 157.

ORGANIC CHEMISTRY 

159 Organic Acids. This group of organic compounds is characterized by the elements COOH, the carboxyl group arranged C—O— H, by which one valence of the carbon atom is free to combine with hydrogen or other radicals. Some of the more important organic acids are: 

1. Formic, H.COOH, having one hydrogen atom combined with the carboxyl group. It is the irritant in some insect stings. 

2. Acetic, CH3COOH (sometimes written C2H4O2). The active principle in vinegar, acetic acid is formed by the oxidation of ethyl alcohol in the presence of ferments in "mother of vinegar."

Acetic acid

Commercial acetic acid is obtained by the destructive distillation of wood, and is widely used in the production of organic compounds, such as the cellulose acetates for films and fabrics.

The reducing action of this acid makes it valuable for bleaching, removing metallic stains and cleaning surfaces of copper alloysbrass and bronze. 

4. Citric acid occurs in citrus fruits (lemons, grapefruit and others). 

5. Tartaric acid, C2H4O2. (COOH)2 or H2 . C4H4O6, is obtained from a salt (potassium hydrogen tartrate, HKC4H4O6) which is formed by grape fermentation. The purified salt, "cream of tartar", is important as the acid ingredient in some baking powders.

The "tartness" of some fruits is due to the presence of organic acids.

Esters. If we neutralize KOH with HCl, an inorganic salt, KCl, is formed. Similarly, the reaction of an alcohol with an acid will yield a compound which we may consider an organic salt. 

Such a compound is known as an ester, or ethereal salt. C2H5OH + HNO3 Ethyl alcohol Nitric acid 

C2H5NO3 + H2O Ethyl nitrate

Common Esters. Nitro-glycerine (correctly named glyceryl tri-nitrate) a powerful explosive, is an ester prepared from glycerol and HNO3: C3H5(OH)3 + 3 HNO3 C3H5(NO3)3 + 3 H₂O Glycerol is treated with a mixture of nitric and sulphuric acids; the sulphuric acid absorbs the water formed. (This is a necessary procedure in making all esters.) Nitro-glycerine is dangerous to handle, since slight shocks may cause it to explode. It is usually absorbed in porous clay or sawdust and used in the solid form as dynamite, which is less sensitive. 

Other esters are essences, such as oil of wintergreen and cloves for flavoring, and "banana oil" used in paints and lacquers. Animal and vegetable fats and oils are esters. These contain radicals of organic acids.

Soap Saponification. Soap is chemically a salt; it consists of a metal combined with an organic acid radical of the higher (fatty) type.

In the manufacture of soap, a strong base (NaOH or KOH) is caused to act on an animal or vegetable fat, as for example, when beef fat (glyceryl stearate) is boiled with NaOH. 

Ester (organic salt) base 

Metallic salt 

Organic base

C3H5 (C18H35O2)3 + 3 NaOH 3 NaC18H35O2 + C3H5(OH)3 

Glyceryl stearate 

Sodium stearate Soap Glycerol

Other fats or oils may be used; the reaction is similar in every case. (Sodium hydroxide yields hard soap; potassium hydroxide, soft soap.) When the process is complete, as shown by test, salt is added. The soap, being insoluble in salt brine, separates from the glycerol and salt water. According to the kind of soap desired, various ingredients are then added and the soap is allowed to solidify in molds. 

Soap powders contain borax or Na2CO3-water softeners.

Floating soaps are charged with air bubbles to lessen the weight. 

Scouring soaps contain mild abrasives, such as pumice. 

Insoluble Soaps. Sodium and potassium soaps are marked by their ability to dissolve in water, form lather or "suds" and emulsify grease and adsorb dirt particles, thus exerting a cleansing action. When soap is used in water containing calcium and magnesium salts (hard water) a reaction takes place in which the more

ORGANIC CHEMISTRY 

161 active Na and K replace the less active metals in solution, and an insoluble soap results: 

Sodium stearate + Calcium sulphate (soap) 

Calcium stearate + sodium sulphate (insoluble soap)

This can be seen as kind of a "scum" floating on the surface. 

The hardness of water is tested by determining its "soap-destroying" power.

Insoluble soaps, calcium and magnesium stearates, find use as the base of some pharmaceutical preparations.

Ether. Ether is an organic oxide, (C2H5)2O. It is a colorless, exceedingly volatile and highly inflammable liquid obtained by distilling ethyl alcohol in the presence of sulphuric acid. When one molecule of water is removed from two molecules of alcohol, ether results.

Ether is a valuable solvent for fats and oils. It is best known for its use as an anaesthetic. /H -C=0. 

Aldehydes. Aldehydes are distinguished by the group —Ċ―0. 

Formaldehyde, H.CHO, is a gas with a pungent, rather disagreeable odor. It has germicidal properties, and is used for fumigating. In water solution it is employed as a preservative of animal matter. Chemically it is used in the production of synthetic plastics, such as "Bakelite."

Carbohydrates. There are compounds containing carbon, hydrogen and oxygen; the number of H atoms is double that of oxygen. The hydrogen and oxygen atoms, existing in the same proportion as in water, may be extracted by the use of a strong dehydrating agent-hot concentrated H2SO4-leaving the carbon as a charred mass.

The outstanding compounds in this class are: 

Starch, C6H1005, obtained from potatoes and grain, chiefly corn. The formation of starch has been previously described (see page 138). Most foods contain starch in varying amounts. 

Starch is insoluble in cold water, since the starch grain is composed of starch particles enclosed in an insoluble coating. This coating is destroyed by the action of heat or dilute acids in the preparation of dextrine or of soluble starch. In laundering, starch is mixed with boiling water, forming a colloidal suspension.

Iodine solution added to starch turns the starch a deep blueblack color, acting as a sensitive test for this carbohydrate. 

Cellulose, C6H1005 is plant fiber. Plant stems and wood are chiefly cellulose. Cotton fiber is nearly 100% cellulose. 

Great chemical and other industries are devoted to the manufacture of cellulose products. Paper making, cotton and linen textiles (see page 234) and wood products employ the native cellulose fiber. Upon treatment with chemicals, cellulose is converted into silk substitutes as "rayon," plastics and photographic film base.

Nitric acid acts on cellulose to form cellulose nitrate or guncotton, one of the first of modern high-power explosives. Modern smokeless gunpowder is made by dissolving nitrocellulose in suitable solvents, and reducing the mixture to the solid state. Other nitro-cellulose products include celluloid, pyroxylin, collodion and lacquer.

Sugars. In this class of carbohydrates we are undoubtedly most familiar with sucrose, C12H22O11, and glucose, C6H12O6. Several other sugars will be considered in addition to these more common forms.

Sucrose is best known as cane sugar. Its main sources are sugar cane, sugar beet and maple syrup. Sugar cane at present supplies the greater part of sucrose used, with the sugar beet as a close second. In the production of sucrose, sugar cane is crushed to extract the juice, which is treated to prevent fermentation and to remove albumin and then filtered. Evaporation in vacuum pans (to avoid charring) yields raw sugar which is further refined by redissolving, filtering and crystallization.

Glucose occurs in many fruits; it is commonly called "grape sugar." Large quantities are prepared from starch by hydrolysis: the starch is heated with water in the presence of a small amount of hydrochloric acid.

C6H10O5+ H2O C6H12O6 Glucose is used in the manufacture of many food products, syrups and candies. It is cheaper than sucrose, and has a high food value. As we have seen, fermentation changes glucose to alcohol; in some processes it acts as an intermediate product in the preparation of alcohol from starch.

When boiled with Fehling's solution, glucose yields a dark red precipitate of reduced copper (test for glucose).

ORGANIC CHEMISTRY 

163 Other sugars of importance are lactose, or milk sugar; levulose, or fruit sugar. 

Note. See Chapter 25, for new developments in organic compounds, such as bakelite, celluloid, and new forms of rubber.

CHAPTER 17 THE CHEMISTRY OF METALS 

Metals and Non-Metals. In Chapter 7, the Electron Theory, we learned that elements are classified into metals and non-metals. 

Metals are lenders of electrons and consequently have a positive valence. In Chapter 8, The Ionization Theory, we learned that when metallic atoms dissociate they form positive ions. Some elements always behave as metals and some as non-metals (borrow electrons, negative valence, negative ions), but others, as we know, behave as both, or are amphoteric.

Physical Properties of Metals. Metallic elements as a group exhibit similar properties which help us recognize the group. 

1. Lustre. All metals shine, some more so than others. Examples are nickel, gold, and silver. 

2. Crystallization. All metals are crystalline in structure. 

3. Ductility. Metals can be drawn into thin wires. Copper wire is an example. 

4. Malleability. Metals can be hammered into different shapes.

Beaten copper and silver ware are common examples. 

5. Good Conductors. Metals are good conductors of heat and electricity. A copper wire becomes hot from one end to the other very quickly, even if only one end is placed in a flame. Copper wires are used almost exclusively for electrical hook-ups (telephone, telegraph, lighting, radio, coils, etc.).

Alloys. Mixtures of metals have been known since the ancient races added tin to their native copper to harden their crude axes. 

The alchemist's dream of gold from copper and zinc gave useful brass to the world.

Nature of Alloys. Metallic substances of uniform texture, such as brass, formed by the mixture of two or more molten metals are commonly called alloys. By extension, the term "alloy" also includes similar mixtures of metals and non-metals. Alloys of mercury are called amalgams. The microscope reveals that the complex structure of alloys takes three general forms. 166

THE CHEMISTRY OF METALS 

167 1. The metals may crystallize out in the pure state. Silver and copper alloys show separate crystals of each metal throughout the alloy. 

2. The metals may combine to form compounds with a definite composition and freezing point. Some of these compounds are known as AuAl2, CuзAl, and FeзC. 

3. The metals may crystallize out together in solution to form mixed crystals, as in silver and gold.

Properties of Alloys. Alloys have properties which may not be characteristic of the constituents. Alloys are usually harder than the metals from which they are made. The melting points of alloys are lower than some of the metals composing them. Electric resistance of alloys is higher than that of pure metals. Malleability of the alloy is less than the chief metals of which it is composed. 

Resistance to corrosion may be increased by adding another metal in comparatively small amounts. Attractive colors of alloys can be made by the selection of proper combinations of metals. Brass is harder than copper but is not so easily rolled into sheets. Solder has a lower melting point than tin or lead, its component metals. 

A trace of arsenic in copper makes it unfit for electrical transmission. The colors of gold for jewelry depend on the metals with which it is alloyed. (See table in Appendix for list of alloys and their properties and uses.)

Metallurgy. Civilization is marked by steps of advancement in terms of the stone, bronze, and iron ages. The discovery and smelting of copper and tin offered less difficulty than that of iron, since native metallic copper needed only to be melted. Tin oxide can be easily reduced to tin by charcoal at low temperatures. 

Iron ore, however, was not reduced to iron until a primitive blast furnace was invented to produce high temperatures.

The less active metals, such as copper, gold, and silver, occur in rocks as free or native metals. Most of the common metals are found combined with other elements as oxides, sulphides, and carbonates. Such natural compounds or minerals are called ores of the metals.

The process of extraction of metals from ores is known as metallurgy. In general, the process is to change a sulphide or carbonate ore to an oxide by roasting it in air and then reducing the oxide to the metal with carbon or some other reducing agent.

Since metals are positive elements and have lost electrons when they combined with the other elements in their ores, the reducing agent must restore the electrons which were taken away by the negative elements with which they are combined. Thus the zinc in zinc oxide borrows two electrons from carbon and becomes metallic zinc. The carbon lends two electrons to the oxygen in this chemical action and forms carbon monoxide. ZnOC Zn + CO ↑ In obtaining aluminum, an electric current is used to supply the flow of electrons, since carbon cannot separate the aluminum from the oxide. However, the more active metal, sodium, was formerly used to reduce aluminum from bauxite (aluminum ore). See page 

66 for the electronic definition of oxidation and reduction. 

Electrochemical Series. The electromotive or electrochemical series of metals was named from the manner by which it was established in the laboratory. It is an arrangement to show the relative activity of the metals. In this list, each metal in the series will replace any metal from its salt solution which is below it. Conversely any metal will be replaced by one above it. According to the electron theory this is based on the ease with which the more active metals lose electrons so that they displace the others from their salt solutions.

ELECTROCHEMICAL SERIES OF METALS 

Potassium Sodium

Calcium Magnesium Aluminum Zinc Iron Nickel

Tin Lead Hydrogen Copper Mercury Silver

Platinum Gold

THE CHEMISTRY OF METALS 

169 A study of the series will bring out these facts. Potassium is the most active of the metals and will replace any of the others. 

Gold is the least active and will not replace any of the others. 

Metals above hydrogen will displace it from most acids. The metals beginning with copper do not displace hydrogen from acids. 

The more active metals near the top occur only combined with other elements while those at the bottom are found in the free state. The compounds of sodium, calcium, etc., are very stable and are difficult to decompose while those lower in the list are less stable. 

Chemical Properties of Metals 1. Action with Acids and Bases. The active acids, such as nitric, sulphuric and hydrochloric acids, are highly ionized in water solution, with the hydrogen ion common to all. If the acid is brought in contact with an active metal the metal gives up electrons to the hydrogen ions, which become atoms and molecules, and the metal becomes an ion. Zn° + 2H+ + SO4¯¯ Fe°+2H++ 2C1- + H₂ ↑ 2 Zn+++ SO4 Fe+++ 2C1+ H2 ↑ Attention is again called to the fact that in the replacement series hydrogen is above copper, silver and mercury, and that it is not replaced by these metals. Nitric acid, when it reacts with these metals, generally forms nitric oxide, since it is a strong oxidizing agent. 3Cu+8HNO3 3Ag + 4HNO3 3Cu(NO3)2 + 2NO ↑ + 4H20 3AgNO3 + NO ↑ + 2H2O Dilute acids are often more active on metals than concentrated acids; for example, concentrated sulphuric acid is transported in steel containers but dilute sulphuric acid attacks iron to form ferrous sulphate and hydrogen, and consquently is transported in lead containers.

Fe + H2SO4 FeSO4 + H₂ ↑ H2 Some metals such as aluminum and zinc react with both acids and bases. Aluminum will liberate hydrogen from hydrochloric acid. 2A1 + 6HC1→ 2A1C13 + 3H2 ↑

With strong bases aluminum and zinc react to form compounds in which they become part of a negative radical. 2Na3A1O3 + 3H2 ↑ 2A1 + 6NaOH sodium aluminate

Zn + 2NaOH →→ Na₂ZnO2 + H2 ↑ sodium zincate

These metals are said to be amphoteric, since they appear as both basic and acid in compounds. 

2. Action with Oxygen. Metals react with oxygen to form basic anhydrides (see page 83). 2Ca + O2 2CaO CaO+ H2O Ca(OH)2 3. Action with Water. Metals react with water to form bases or oxides. 3Fe+4H2O-Fe3O4 + 4H2 ↑ 2Na + 2H2O2NaOH + H₂ ↑ H2 4. Action with Non-Metals. Metals react with non-metals to form salts.

Zn + S → ZnS

CHAPTER 18 SODIUM, POTASSIUM, AND THEIR COMPOUNDS METALLIC SODIUM AND POTASSIUM 

Place in Electron Theory. Sodium has the atomic number of 

11, and has only one electron in its outer ring. If we remember our study of the electron theory, we know this indicates that it is a very active element and distinctly metallic. 

Preparation. Sodium, because of its chemical activity is not found free in nature, and was not known as a metal until Davy (1807) prepared it by passing an electric current through fused sodium hydroxide. The commercial method uses the same principle. The sodium hydroxide is melted in an iron tank, which has the carbon cathode (negative) in the middle of the bottom. The anode (positive) is an iron cylinder which surrounds the cathode. 

The sodium and hydrogen ions formed by the dissociation of fused sodium hydroxide collect at the cathode, pick up electrons, and become atoms. Oxygen ions collect at the anode where they discharge electrons and become atoms. Provision is made so the gases do not mingle. The equation is as follows: 2NaOH 2Na + H2 ↑ + O2 ↑ Physical Properties. Metallic sodium is true to its place as a metal. A freshly cut piece shows the bright metallic luster characteristic of other metals. Since it can be pressed into a layer we know it is malleable. Sodium is a good conductor of heat and electricity. In contrast to other metals it is lighter than water and soft enough to be cut with a knife. Sodium melts at 95.6° C. 

Chemical Properties. Sodium is a very active element. It reacts so readily with oxygen and the water in the air that sticks of sodium are kept covered with kerosene oil. When placed on water its action is striking. Characteristically a metal, sodium reacts with the water to liberate half of its hydrogen and to form a base. 

The heat generated melts the sodium so that it forms a globule as it moves on the surface of the water. The sodium hydroxide 172

SODIUM, POTASSIUM, AND THEIR COMPOUNDS 

173 formed dissolves in the water and is recovered as a white solid by evaporation. The equation for the reaction is as follows: 

2Na + 2H2O2NaOH + H2 ↑ Uses of Sodium. Sodium is a very useful reducing agent in the manufacture of complex compounds. It was formerly used to reduce aluminum oxide to prepare aluminum. Sodium peroxide and cyanide are made from the metallic sodium. 

Potassium. Potassium is a companion metal to sodium. It, too, is not found free in nature and was first isolated by Davy. 

It has a silvery luster which dulls quickly when exposed to the air. Potassium is softer than sodium and lighter in weight. It is more active than sodium in its action on water, the heat generated being enough to ignite the hydrogen which is given off. The potassium hydroxide formed is left in solution. 2K+ 2H2O → 2KOH + H2 ↑ Potassium, like sodium, is an example of an extreme metal since it gives up its one electron in the fourth ring readily and has all the marked metallic characteristics of luster, malleability, conductivity of heat and electricity. 

Spectroscope. The spectroscope is basically a prism which breaks up a light beam into the various colors of the spectrum. 

The spectroscope is used in chemistry to detect the presence of elements by noting their characteristic colors. Ordinary sunlight, when passed through a prism, shows all the colors of the spectrum. 

The light formed by the incandescent vapor of an element, when viewed through a spectroscope, shows colors peculiar to that element, which may definitely be identified by its peculiar color or colors.

Flame Tests. Sodium and potassium vapors give characteristic colors in a flame, which serve as tests for the metals and their presence in compounds. A nichrome wire dipped in a solution of a sodium compound will give a bright yellow flame, while potassium will give a violet flame. To detect potassium vapor in the presence of sodium, a thick piece of cobalt-blue glass is used to filter out the color waves of the sodium flame. 

Place in Chemistry. Since sodium and potassium are so active, they form a great number of stable compounds which, on account

of their abundance and value, furnish the basic materials for great industries. Sodium hydroxide (used in soap), sodium carbonate (glass-making) and sodium nitrates (fertilizers and nitric acid) are used in huge quantities. Potassium fertilizers from the mines of 

Alsace-Lorraine have been of immense importance to Germany and France.

Sodium Chloride. Crude rock salt, halite, occurs in widely distributed beds in New York State, Michigan, Texas and in European countries. In solution, it makes up about 4% of sea water and is found in a more highly concentrated solution in Great Salt 

Lake and the Dead Sea.

Methods of Obtaining Salt. Salt may be directly mined (as is coal) where the beds are near the surface. In New York State, wells are driven into the salt layers. The salt then is dissolved with water and the brine pumped to the surface. 

Along the sea coast, salt water in shallow basins is evaporated by the heat of the sun until the salt crystallizes out. In the colder climates the salt may be obtained by first freezing the water, then removing the ice and finally by heating the concentrated brine until the remaining water evaporates. 

Purification of Salt. The modern method of purifying salt is by boiling off the water in vacuum pans, allowing the crystals to form, then redissolving the crystals in water and allowing the pure salt to crystallize.

Washing the impure crystals with pure brine will dissolve other salts which have also crystallized in the concentration. Pure salt may be obtained by adding concentrated hydrochloric acid to a saturated salt solution. The salt precipitates, due to the excess of chlorine ions (see common-ion effect, page 76). From the residue, called bittern, other sodium compounds such as sodium sulphate, bromides and iodates may be obtained. 

Properties and Uses. Pure sodium chloride forms cubical crystals which have the common salty taste. They have no water of crystallization, but when heated the crystals will explode because of the water inclosed in them. This is called decrepitation. Pure salt is not deliquescent. 

Salt is a necessary food. To primitive tribes it was more valuable than gold. It is the source of manufacture of other sodium

SODIUM, POTASSIUM, AND THEIR COMPOUNDS 

175 compounds. Sodium hydroxide, sodium carbonate, and sodium bicarbonate are made from it, as well as chlorine and hydrochloric acid. It is used to preserve fish and meats. Brine is cooled and circulated in pipes to furnish low temperatures. Freezing mixtures of salt and ice keep ice cream in condition for transportation. 

Soap manufacturers use salt to separate the soap from water, and pottery is glazed by fusing salt with clay. Clqas Hqas

Nelson Cell

Sodium Hydroxide. Sodium hydroxide is prepared by electrolysis of a strong solution of brine. The sodium ions pass to the cathode and, after losing their charges and becoming atoms, react with the water to form sodium hydroxide and hydrogen. The chlorine ions pass to the anode and, after the charges are neutralized, pass off as chlorine gas. If the chlorine is not kept apart from the sodium hydroxide it will react with the base to form sodium hypochlorite. The modern Nelson cell is designed to prevent this by having an asbestos diaphragm surrounding the posi-

tive electrode. The cell consists of a steel trough lined with asbestos, which serves as the cathode. The anode is a block of carbon placed in the center. The brine flows into the cell near the anode and the sodium hydroxide is recovered at the bottom. The hydrogen and chlorine gases are led off through separate pipes, the chlorine becoming a valuable by-product. The complete reaction is as follows: 2NaC1 + 2H2O2NaOH + H2 ↑ + Cl2 ↑ Properties and Uses of Sodium Hydroxide. Sodium hydroxide is sold as a white solid known as caustic soda-so-named from its action on the skin. It is very deliquescent and dissolves readily to form a strong base. Its water solution turns red litmus blue, tastes bitter and has a soapy feeling. When added to acids it neutralizes them to form a salt of the acid and water. 

Being a strong base, it is largely used with fats to make a soap (see page 160). The petroleum industry neutralizes acids with it. 

Cotton fiber is treated with it in some processes to make artificial silk. As lye, sodium hydroxide is used to clean sinks and floors. 

Sodium hydroxide dissolves wool and silk fibers. Because of this property it is used as a test for the kind of fiber in a cloth. 

Sodium Carbonate and Sodium Bicarbonate. These two compounds are made in large amounts by the Solvay process, which produces them at a low cost, since the materials used are abundant and the by-products formed are used over again. 

Ammonia gas is passed through a solution of sodium chloride until the solution is saturated. Carbon dioxide is then added. The reactions take place in the following order: (1) NH3 + H2O

NH4OH (2) CO2 + H2O H2CO3 

(3) NH4OH + H2CO3 →→ NH4HCO3 + H2O The ammonium bicarbonate reacts with the salt to form sodium bicarbonate and ammonium chloride:

NH4HCO3 + NaCl → NaHCO3 + NH4C1 The temperature and concentrations are so regulated that the sodium bicarbonate is precipitated, thus avoiding a reversible reaction. The four equations may be combined as follows:

NaCl + H2O + NH3 + CO2 

NaHCO3 + NH4Cl

SODIUM, POTASSIUM, AND THEIR COMPOUNDS 

177 In order to obtain sodium carbonate, the bicarbonate is heated to drive off the carbon dioxide: 2NaHCO3 Na2CO3 + CO2 + H2O ➤ ↑ Plants making these products are generally located near salt and limestone deposits. The salt, of course, furnishes the sodium chloride solution, and limestone the carbon dioxide. The quicklime formed by burning reacts with water to form slaked lime, which is used to recover the ammonia from the ammonium chloride.

Ca(OH)2+2NH4C1→ CaCl2 + 2H2O + 2NH3 ↑ Part of the carbon dioxide is recovered by heating the sodium bicarbonate. Even the waste calcium chloride finds use as a deliquescent substance. 

Properties and Uses of Sodium Carbonate. Sodium carbonate (washing soda) in the crystalline form has the formula Na2CO3.- 

10H2O. When exposed to air, it loses its water of crystallization through efflorescence and becomes a white powder. The anhydrous salt is sold as soda ash. Its water solution turns red litmus blue, since it is formed from a strong base and a weak acid. Large quantities are used in glass-making and refining of petroleum. Its use in washing powders depends on its alkalinity and cheapness. 

Properties and Uses of Sodium Bicarbonate. Sodium bicarbonate, baking soda, takes its common name from its use to furnish carbon dioxide in baking. It is milder in action as an alkali than sodium carbonate. If heated or treated with an acid it will give off carbon dioxide. When it is used to furnish carbon dioxide in baking, a weak acid is present (such as lactic acid in sour milk or an acid salt like cream of tartar in a baking powder).

It is used to furnish carbon dioxide for fire extinguishers, since it furnishes more carbon dioxide in proportion to the amount used than does washing soda. 

2NaHCO3 + H2SO4

Na2CO3 + H2SO4 Na2SO4 + 2CO2 ↑ + 2H2O Na2SO4 + CO2 ↑ + H₂O Sodium Nitrate. NaNO3, or Chile saltpeter, is found principally in the arid region in northern Chile. The salt is dissolved from the earthy matter and the solution evaporated to produce

crude Chile saltpeter. Further crystallization results in a pure salt. Sodium iodate is an important by-product. 

Although the world's largest deposits of nitrate salts are in Chile, the output from this country has declined because of the development of synthetic nitrates on a large scale. However, over a million tons are still exported annually for nitrogen fertilizers and as a source of nitric acid and potassium nitrate. 

Potassium Compounds. Potassium is an element necessary to the growth of plants, hence a source of potassium is essential for artificial fertilizers. The world's principal deposits of chlorides of potassium and magnesium are in Germany and France. The 

United States suffered from a scarcity of potash during the World War, as adequate sources are not present in this country. 

Potassium nitrate, or saltpeter, is made by mixing hot concentrated solutions of potassium chloride and sodium nitrate. KCl + NaNO3 NaCl + KNO3 On evaporation the sodium chloride crystallizes first and then on cooling, the potassium nitrate crystallizes out, being less soluble in cold water than the other salts (This process is known as fractional crystallization.)

Potassium nitrate makes up about 75% of black gunpowder. 

The red color of corned beef is due to potassium nitrate, which is used in this case as a preservative.

CHAPTER 19 CALCIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

Calcium. Calcium could be described by the words used to describe sodium. It is too active a metal to occur free, has all of the metallic properties to almost as great a degree as sodium and potassium, and, since it lends two electrons, has a positive valence of 2. Compounds of the element calcium are abundant and of great commercial importance. 

Calcium Carbonate. CaCO3 occurs chiefly in the form of limestone and marble; to a lesser extent as chalk and calcite, which is a transparent crystallized form of calcium carbonate. The physical properties of CaCO3 vary with the form, ranging from gray, moderately hard limestone to soft white chalk. All forms are insoluble in pure water, and are decomposed by heat. 

Chemical Properties. CaCO3 reacts with acids, forming salts and evolving CO2:

CaCO3 + 2HC1→ CaCl2 + H2O + CO2 ↑ Water containing CO2 (weak carbonic acid) dissolves calcium carbonate, forming soluble calcium bicarbonate: CaCO3 + H2O + CO2 Ca(HCO3)2 Uses of Calcium Carbonate. (a) In building materials. Forms of CaCO3 which have considerable structural strength, such as limestone and marble, are employed in building and ornamental construction. (b) Making quicklime, CaO (see below). (c) The action of acids on CaCO3 is a practical method of generating CO2. (d) Intensely heated with clay, CaCO3 yields Portland cement. (e) Calcium carbonate acts as a flux, or purifying agent, in the reduction of iron ore; it combines with impurities forming slag. (f) Ground limestone is used in agriculture to neutralize certain ground acids which cause "sour" soil. The limestone, by neutralizing the acids, is said to "sweeten" the soil. 181

Limestone Caves. Where extensive deposits of limestone exist, it is common to find caves which have been hollowed out by the solvent action of water and carbon dioxide. The Mammoth Cave of Kentucky, Carlsbad Caverns in New Mexico and many others are of enormous extent. Stalactites ("stone icicles") and stalagmites (mounds and pillars rising from the floor) are noticeable features of these caves; both are forms of precipitated CaCO3 caused by the evaporation of water containing dissolved calcium bicarbonate. Colored by traces of iron and other metallic elements, they present a striking appearance. 

Compounds Derived from CaCO3.

Calcium Oxide. CaO is prepared by heating finely crushed limestone to a temperature sufficiently high to decompose the carbonate and expel the CO2:

CaCO3 →→ CaO + CO, 1 This process is carried out in furnaces (kilns) of either vertical or horizontal (rotating) type. The crushed limestone, moving through the kiln, is heated by long, sweeping flames from powdered coal or oil fuel. Since the CO2 is carried off, a reversal of the reaction is prevented. 

Calcium oxide or "quicklime" is a white solid which reacts with water with the evolution of heat, forming slaked lime: CaO + H2O Ca(OH)2 This process is commonly seen when workmen mix mortar in building construction; as the water is added to the quicklime the bubbling action and evolution of heat indicates the slaking of the lime.

Exposure to air causes "air-slaking" of lime. The moisture in the air is first absorbed, forming calcium hydroxide; this compound then unites with CO2 in the air, forming calcium carbonate. 

Ca(OH)2 + CO2 CaCO3 + H2O Other uses of CaO are for the manufacture of calcium carbide, CaC2, and for making limewater, a solution of Ca(OH)2. 

Calcium Hydroxide. Ca(OH)2 is formed, as we have seen, by the slaking of CaO. This compound is a white solid which dissolves sparingly in water to form limewater. A strong base, cheaply manufactured, limewater is useful in many commercial processes: soften-

CALCIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

183 ing of hard water, removing hair from hides in the process of tanning, making whitewash and bleaching powder.

One of the most important uses of calcium hydroxide is for making mortar, used as a binding material for stone and brick in building construction. The slaked lime is mixed with sand and water to form a pasty mass. Mortar hardens or "sets" as the water slowly evaporates, and CO2 in the air reacts with the calcium hydroxide, forming calcium carbonate. Ordinary lime mortar attains full strength only after a considerable period of time. 

Cement is often added to mortar to increase the strength and to reduce the hardening time. The process of making and hardening mortar may be outlined as follows: Heated limestone (CaCO3) yields quicklime (CaO), which with water yields slaked lime (Ca(OH)2), which with water and sand forms mortar. The mortar reacts with CO2, forming hardened mortar (CaCO3). 

Calcium Sulphate. CaSO4 occurs naturally as Gypsum,

CaSO4.2 H2O. This compound is used to some extent in cementmaking to prevent too rapid a setting of concrete. Its most important use, however, is in the manufacture of Plaster of Paris. 

Gypsum, when heated somewhat above the boiling point of water, loses 3 molecules of water of crystallization from every 2 molecules of gypsum. The resulting compound is Plaster of Paris: 2(CaSO4.2 H20) — 3 H₂O + (CaSO4)2. H2O gypsum 

Plaster of Paris 

This substance formed is a fine white powder which sets when mixed with water, forming a hard mass. Plaster of Paris is used for plaster casts in setting fractured bones. Since the mixture expands slightly on setting, it makes precise reproductions of objects when used as a molding material. It is also used in making plaster, wall board and some forms of tile. 

What is "Hard" Water? Water which contains dissolved salts of calcium and magnesium is said to be hard. For many uses, hard water is undesirable. Ordinary soap does not lather well when used with hard water and does not clean efficiently. When used in boilers, hard water may form mineral deposits which injure the boiler and cause heat waste. It is important, then, to know how this hardness in water may be eliminated and the water made "soft."

Temporary Hard Water. We have learned that calcium carbonate is soluble in water containing carbon dioxide, forming calcium bicarbonate. Water containing this compound in solution is said to be temporary hard water. On a small scale, temporary hard water may be softened by boiling, which decomposes the dissolved compound: Ca(HCO3)2 →→ CaCO3 + CO2 ↑ + H2O The lime deposit often seen in tea kettles has been precipitated by this process.

When water is used in large quantities it would be impractical to boil it before using. The addition of calcium hydroxide (slaked lime)—precipitates CaCO3, leaving the water soft:

Ca (HCO3)2 + Ca(OH)2 2 CaCO3 + 2 H₂O Permanent Hard Water. This is caused chiefly by the presence of calcium and magnesium sulphates. Assuming CaSO4 is present, Na2CO3 may be added:

CaSO4 + Na2CO3 CaCO3 + Na2SO4 Sodium sulphate does not prevent formation of lather when soap is used.

A modern process of water-softening makes use of a substance called permutite, a complex compound which reacts with CaSO4 forming Na2SO4. Permutite has the advantage of being cheaply reactivated after use by a solution of common salt. 

Calcium Phosphate. Ca3(PO4)2 occurs in nature as rock phosphate and in bone structure. When treated with sulphuric acid, it is converted into a soluble form-monocalcium phosphate, Ca(H2PO4)2, used in fertilizers.

Calcium Chloride. CaCl2 is a deliquescent (water-absorbing) solid. It is used to dry gases. When sprinkled on dusty surfaces, such as dirt roads, it absorbs moisture from the air and prevents dust particles from rising. 

Calcium Hypochlorite. CaOCl2 commonly known as chloride of lime or "bleaching powder," is prepared by passing chlorine over slaked lime:

Cl2 + Ca(OH)2 CaOCl2 + H2O

CALCIUM AND ITS COMPOUNDS 

185 This is a white powder similar in appearance to slaked lime, and can be distinguished by the odor of chlorine which is slowly evolved.

Weak acids act on bleaching powder, yielding chlorine. The chief uses of CaOCl2-bleaching and disinfecting are dependent upon this fact (see page 89).

Bleaching. Cotton cloth is usually bleached by immersing it in a thin paste of bleaching powder, then in weak acid. The chlorine formed bleaches the cloth; any excess acid is then neutralized to prevent weakening of the fabric. CaOCl2 + 2 HCl →→→ Cl2 + CaCl2 + H2O Disinfecting. Water and carbon dioxide in air slowly act on bleaching powder: 

CaOCl2 + H2CO3 Cl2 + CaCO3 + H2O Bleaching powder is used as a disinfectant by sprinkling it on substances to be disinfected.

CHAPTER 20  

Occurrence. Since iron is an active metal, it rarely occurs free. 

As a compound, it occurs in the following iron ores: hematite (Fe2O3), magnetite (FeO.Fe2O3), siderite (FeCO3) and limonite (2Fe2O3.3H2O). Hematite, however, is by far the most important iron ore.

The Blast Furnace. Since cast iron is the raw iron from which steel is made, it is produced in enormous amounts. The average annual world production of cast iron is 75 million tons. A single blast furnace can produce 200 tons in 24 hours. The blast furnace used to obtain iron from its ore is a circular structure of steel plates from 80 to 100 feet in height. The inside is lined with fire bricks. Pipes of cold water circulate within the walls to prevent overheating. The charging lock, a chamber with movable top and bottom, is at the top. Near the bottom, air pipes are located which supply the pre-heated air for the blast. The melted cast iron is drawn off at the bottom, and just above it is a hole for the lighter melted slag to be removed. The gases formed pass off near the top. These hot waste gases are used in the stoves in which the blast air is heated and are finally used to supply power to the gas engines which operate the compression air pumps. 

Action in the Blast Furnace. Since iron ore, hematite, contains silica, provision must be made to remove the silica as well as to reduce the oxide. Calcium carbonate or limestone will do this. 

The charge which is put into the air lock, then, consists of iron ore, limestone, and coke. 

1. The coke is first burned to carbon dioxide:

C + O2 CO2 ↑ 2. The carbon dioxide is then reduced to carbon monoxide:

CO2 + C 2CO ↑ 188

3. As a third step, the ferric oxide is reduced by the carbon monoxide:

Fe2O3 + 3CO 2Fe + 3CO2 The melted cast iron sinks down to the bottom of the furnace and is drawn off through the tap hole. The carbon dioxide, how-

CaCO3 →→ CaO + CO2 1 The calcium oxide then unites with the silica of the ore to form the glassy slag, calcium silicate.

CaO + SiO2 → CaSiO3 This melted silicate, lighter than cast iron, forms a layer of liquid above the heavier cast iron, and is tapped off from time to time. Slag is used largely in making Portland cement.

Properties and Uses of Cast Iron. In large steel plants the melted cast iron immediately becomes part of the charge for the steel furnaces. When stored or sold, it is poured into a series of molds to harden. It is then called pig iron. Cast iron melts easily at about 1200° C, and is poured in molds and takes the shape of the interior of the mold, since it expands as it solidifies. When cast iron cools quickly, it forms hard, white cast iron, but when it cools slowly, part of the graphite dissolved in the iron separates and it forms the softer gray iron. Cast iron cannot be bent, hammered or welded.

The crystalline structure of cast iron gives it a rigid form which is desirable for use in bases and frames of heavy machinery, the cylinder blocks of automobile engines, and for steam radiators. 

Refining Cast Iron. Cast iron, the product of the blast furnace, is a crude form of iron containing impurities in the form of carbon (5%), silicon, phosphorus, and sulphur. Experience has taught man how to refine this form of iron into wrought iron and steel, which have properties of toughness or hardness not found in cast iron.

To remove carbon from cast iron, the metallurgist oxidizes it to form carbon monoxide. Basic slags and lining in furnaces tend to absorb the acid anhydrides of silicon, phosphorus, and sulphur. 

Wrought Iron. Wrought iron was produced in ancient times. 

The famous iron pillar of Delhi has withstood the elements from the fourth century A. D. This pure form of iron contains less than .3% of carbon and has a fibrous structure which is due to threads of slag occuring throughout its mass. 

The furnace used to make wrought iron from cast iron is called a reverberatory or air furnace, since an oxidizing flame is directed downward by the sloping roof. The hearth, composed of basic materials, is covered with a layer of iron oxide. The charge of cast iron is placed upon it. The heat of the flame melts the charge. 

Stirring the melted iron brings it in contact with the iron oxide so that carbon in the cast iron is changed to carbon monoxide. Fe2O3 + 3C2Fe + 3CO ↑ Sulphur, phosphorus and silicon are also oxidized. Sulphur passes off as volatile gas with the carbon monoxide. The other two elements combine with the iron oxide and the basic lining of

the furnace to form a slag. As the carbon burns away, the higher melting point of the pure iron causes it to thicken into lumps or masses. The furnace men (called puddlers) gather these masses of iron into balls called blooms. These blooms are placed under steam hammers where liquid slag is hammered or squeezed out. 

Then the iron is further worked by passing it through a rolling mill. The small amount of slag left is drawn out in threads throughout the mass of the iron and gives the iron a toughness and resistance to rusting.

Uses of Wrought Iron. Wrought iron is commonly used in bars and rods called blacksmith iron. Two pieces of this ore can be heated red hot and then pounded together to form a weld. The black oxide which forms upon heating must be first dissolved by a flux such as borax so as to expose the pure iron surface. 

Wrought iron is soft and tough so that it can be bent and worked into many shapes as ornamental iron, chains and wire. 

Brought near a magnet, it will be strongly attracted but can not be made to hold magnetism. STEEL Composition and Properties of Steel. Steel is a refined product of iron containing from 0.1% to 2% of carbon, which gives it desired degrees of hardness. The impurities have also been removed in the process of manufacture so that the amount of phosphorus and sulphur is small. Steel is free from the slag structure of wrought iron and has high tensile strength. It can be forged into various shapes, rolled into sheets and bars, as well as pulled into wire. Steel castings are also made. The composition and properties of steel depend upon the process by which it has been made, the percentage of carbon present and the heat treatment it has received.

Crucible Steel. In early times steel was made by heating wrought iron bars in beds of charcoal so that a slow absorption of carbon took place. In the crucible process two or three hundred pounds of wrought iron and scrap steel are melted in graphite crucibles. If it is desired to increase the carbon content, charcoal is added. 

If it is necessary to decrease the carbon, iron oxide is added to burn out part of the carbon as carbon monoxide.

The advantage of the crucible process lies in the fact that the small amount of charge allows close regulation so that a high quality of steel is produced. Crucible steel is chiefly used for tools and springs.

Bessemer Process. Introduced shortly after our Civil War, this process first produced steel in large amounts and at low cost. The 

Bessemer furnace or converter is an oval-shaped steel shell lined 

AIR BLAST STEEL FLAMES 

SHELL

LINING OF FIRE BRICK MOLTEN IRON 

AXLE 

WIND BOX 

Bessemer Converter with either silica or magnesite brick. The converter is mounted on two axles so that it can be tipped forward. Through one axle, which is hollow, an air duct leads to the bottom. Holes through the bottom permit air to be blown into the furnace.

Melted cast iron from the blast furnaces is poured into the converter, which is tipped to receive the charge. Tipped back into a vertical position, a hot blast of air is forced through the impure iron. Oxidation of the carbon and other impurities takes place. The carbon monoxide burns at the top of the converter. A basic lining helps to free the metal of silicon and phosphorus anhydrides.

After the carbon is burned out, which is shown by a change from a yellow to a blue flame at the mouth of the converter, a weighed amount of the alloy spiegeleisen, rich in carbon and manganese, is added and the blast turned on again. This alloy gives

the right amount of carbon, and the manganese combines with the oxidized steel. After mixing, the steel is poured in a ladle to cool. 

More manganese may be added to complete the deoxidation. The liquid steel is then run into molds to harden and then taken to the rolling mills. In some processes, the molten steel is carried directly to electric furnaces for further refining. 

Uses of Bessemer Steel. Bessemer steel is rolled into structural steel rods for re-enforced concrete and into sheets for stampings. 

Formerly used for railroad rails, its impurities are too high for the requirements of heavy duty traffic.

Open Hearth Steel. This process was developed to remove more of the phosphorus and sulphur present in low grade iron ores than is possible with the Bessemer method. Too much phosphorus, (above .1%) will produce brittle steel when cold. Sulphur (above .1%) makes steel break up when it is rolled hot. 

The open hearth furnace is really a double air furnace, with from 50 to 200 tons capacity. The main bed of the furnace is lined with a basic lining of magnesite brick and is shallow in order to expose a large surface to the flame. On each side of the furnace is a checker work of firebrick divided into two sections. The outer is for a blast of air, and the inner for the producer gas. The flame from one side passes over the charge and through the check work on the other side and thus preheats the bricks. Several times an hour the blast of air and gas is changed from one side of the furnace to the other by a system of valves. A high temperature is thus obtained by this regenerative control. The charge is made of cast iron, scrap steel, iron oxide and lime. The iron oxide furnishes oxygen to burn out the carbon from the cast iron. Fe2O3 + 3C2Fe + 3CO ↑ The phosphorus and sulphur are also oxidized and form sulphates and phosphates with the lime slag. The process is slow (six to twelve hours) when compared with the Bessemer, and is carefully regulated by chemists so that the carbon, silicon, phosphorus, and sulphur content is known before a heat is finished. 

Deoxidizing agents, such as ferro silicon and ferro manganese, are added before the charge is poured to reduce porosity in the metal. 

Uses of Open Hearth Steel. The steel ingots of open hearth steel are sent to the rolling mills when still hot where they are

HOT made into rails, girders and beams for buildings and bridges, and plates for tanks and ships. 

Electric Furnace Steel. The usual type of electric furnace consists of two parts. The lower part or hearth is lined with basic brick and the upper part with fire brick. Through this roof are placed the graphite electrodes. The electric connections are cooled by water jackets to prevent melting. The furnace is mounted so

The reducing atmosphere of the furnace furnishes ideal conditions to control the content and structure of the steel desired. The molten Bessemer steel is poured into the furnace and iron oxide and lime are added. The heat developed by the electric current passing through the slag keeps the metal liquid. The iron oxide causes the phosphorus, sulphur, and carbon to oxidize. The acid GAS

anhydrides combine with the slag while carbon monoxide passes off. 

Then carbon is added in the desired percentage and manganese or other deoxidizers remove excess gases. The product is the highest grade steel which can be produced in large amounts. It is used for automobiles or machines subject to vibration. High carbon electric steel is equal in quality to crucible steel. Many expensive alloy steels are now made by the electric process.

Tempering of Steel. Steel from 0.5 to 2% in carbon is given a special heat treatment to obtain the required degree of hardness. 

When the steel is heated above a certain range (670°C) in temperature, the iron carbide dissolves. Slow cooling permits the iron carbide to crystallize out and a soft steel results. A sudden cooling holds the iron carbide in its dissolved state and a very hard brittle steel is formed. To temper this degree of hardness, the steel is again heated from 225° to 350°C and quenched in water or oil. This reheating causes some of the iron carbide to separate from the steel and hence softens it. Tempering by reheating permits a careful control over the degree of hardness desired.

Metals Related to Steel Manufacture 

Nickel and Cobalt. These are largely found in ores associated with silver. Canadian mines yield the major source of these metals. 

Nickel is a hard, silver-like metal. It may be polished highly and does not tarnish in air. Iron is very often nickel-plated for a protective covering. Many alloys of copper and nickel are known. Nickel steels have high tensile strength-3% of nickel in steel will double its tensile strength. Eighteen per cent nickel makes steel non-magnetic and 36% nickel steel or iron is nonexpansive to heat. 

Cobalt has had fewer uses than nickel but is now finding a place in magnet and tool steels. 

Chromium takes a high silvery polish and is permanent in air. 

Chromium plating is replacing nickel plating because of its hardness and luster. Chromium in steel in small amounts adds to its hardness. Eighteen per cent chromium steel is called stainless steel, since it resists corrosion. 

Tungsten is a very heavy metal with a high melting point (3000° C). For this reason it is used as filaments in incandescent lamps. Steel alloyed with tungsten retains a cutting edge at high temperature. Six per cent tungsten steel is used for magnets. 

Molybdenum is similar in properties and uses to tungsten. In steel it adds hardness, toughness, and tensile strength. 

Manganese occurs as the ore pyrolusite, MnO2. The metal is hard and has a reddish luster. Ferromanganese is used to dioxidize steel. Twelve per cent manganese steel is used for armor plate. 

Vanadium in steel (0.1 to 0.3%) makes steel of high tensile strength and resistant to vibration. It is useful in automobiles. 

Note. See Chapter 25, for recent developments in the chemistry of steel. 

Rust and its Prevention. When exposed to the air, iron corrodes. Dry air does not rust iron, but moisture, oxygen, and carbon dioxide together form an unstable hydroxide with iron which decomposes to become ferric oxide. The ferric oxide crumbles away, so that after the surface is attacked the rusting spreads quickly. Rust stains may be dissolved with oxalic acid and washed away.

Painting is perhaps the most usual way to prevent` rusting of

iron. The surface must be thoroughly cleaned and free from acids. 

The liquid paint dries and hardens to keep out the destructive agents in the air. Rust will develop if the coating is broken. 

Iron is also coated with zinc, tin, nickel, and other metallic coatings. Galvanized iron is made by dipping iron sheets in molten zinc. Zinc makes an excellent coating since its oxide adheres to the metal and protects it. Enameled ware is another method of protecting iron. This has a foundation of pressed or cast iron which is coated with an opaque borax glass. Enameled ware is extensively used for cooking utensils, refrigerators and bathtubs.

It has the disadvantage of chipping when struck by a hard blow. 

Russian iron is iron protected from rust by passing steam over red hot iron. A protective coating of magnetite (Fe3O4) is formed. 

Stainless steel is protected by the presence of alloys in its composition. For the use of electroplating in forming protective coatings, see page 205. 

Iron Oxides. Iron forms three oxides. Ferrous oxide (FeO) is unstable and oxidizes in air to ferric oxide (Fe2O3). This common oxide is the hematite ore used for smelting. Other forms are Venetian red, used for paint and rouge, for polishing metals, and as a color base. Limonite is a hydrated form of ferric oxide yellow in color. Black oxide of iron is found as magnetite. It is a combination of FeO and Fe2O3, or ferro ferric oxide (Fe3O4). 

Iron Chlorides. There are two chlorides of iron: ferrous chloride (FeCl2) and ferric chloride (FeCl3). The ous ending denotes the lesser valence of 2 and the ic ending of 3. Ferrous compounds are less stable and tend to form ferric compounds. (See page 66 for the oxidation and reduction equations of ferrous and ferric chloride according to the electron theory.)

Ferrous Sulphate. Green vitriol (FeSO4.7H2O) is a product of cleaning iron or steel with dilute sulphuric acid. Its light green crystals are known as copperas. Ferrous sulphate is commonly used in black ink and as a mordant in dyeing (see page 237). 

Test for Ferrous and Ferric Ions. If potassium ferricyanide (K3Fe(CN) 6) is added to a solution of ferrous sulphate a deep blue precipitate is formed called ferrous ferricyanide. As a pigment it is called Turnbull's blue. Since ferrous ions must be present for

CHAPTER 21  COPPER 

Refining of Copper. Copper occurs both in the native state and in complex sulphide and carbonate ores. The removal of sulphur and iron by blast furnace and converter methods is complicated. The impure copper is cast into plates for refining. Some carbonate ores are pure enough to be dissolved directly by sulphuric acid, which greatly reduces the cost of production. 

CATHODE - SANODE 

IMPURE COPPER 

PURE

COPPER DISSOLVED DEPOSITED SOLUTION of CuSO4 + H₂SO 

Cu

GE OF GOLD, SILVER,ETC. 

Electrolytic Refining of Impure Copper

Copper is refined by electrolysis of copper sulphate. The impure copper anode supplies the source of copper for the solution, while pure copper is deposited directly on the thin sheet of copper used as a cathode. The gold or silver alloyed with the copper is recovered from the slime of the vat. The value of recovered gold and silver may be enough to pay for the cost of mining and refining the copper. Millions of pounds of copper are refined yearly. 

Uses of Copper. Copper, the red metal, finds its greatest use as a conductor of electricity in transmission lines and in the coils of motors and dynamos. For this purpose, electrolytic copper 204

must be used, as impurities in small amounts greatly reduce the conductivity. Copper sheets are used for roofs, since they become coated with a protective, green, basic carbonate. Copper water pipes have the advantage of not rusting and are easily bent into place. Copper radiators with fins for heat radiation are a recent use. Copper is a very important constituent in brass and bronze alloys. 

Copper Sulphate. Blue vitriol (CuSO4.5H2O), is formed by treating copper with warm concentrated sulphuric acid in the presence of oxygen. The large blue crystals characteristic of copper salts are slightly efflorescent. It has many uses (1) both in copper purification and in plating (2) as a mordant in dyeing (3) for spraying the trees and grape vines to destroy fungus growths. It is mixed with slaked lime to form Bordeaux mixture, used to spray plants and trees. As a water purifier it is added (one part to several million) to reservoirs to destroy minute plants or algae which give a disagreeable taste to drinking water. It is also effective as a germicide.

Copper Plating. Copper plating illustrates the principles of depositing a film of metal by means of an electric current. Refer to page 168 for the position of copper in the electrochemical series. 

You will note from its position that it can be replaced in a compound by iron, zinc, lead, aluminum, etc. The relative difficulty with which copper loses electrons makes it especially useful in electroplating. For example: 

Feo + Cu+++ SO4¯¯ Cuo+ Fe+++ SO4¯¯ The electrolyte used in copper plating is copper sulphate. The anode of pure copper and the object to be plated (the cathode) are placed in the copper sulphate. At the anode the copper atoms lose electrons and enter the solution as Cu++ ions. These ions pass through the solution to the cathode where they recover their electrons to become atoms, which form a coating of copper on the cathode. Proper regulation of the strength of the solution, temperature, and current are necessary to form firm coatings. 

Copper plating is used extensively to make electrotypes or plates used in printing books. Nickel, chromium and silver plating, also widely used, is based on the same principle as copper plating.

ALUMINUM 

Ores of Aluminum. Aluminum is widely and abundantly distributed in common clay as aluminum silicate. No commercial process has been developed to obtain aluminum from this source. 

The ore used is bauxite (Al2O3), an impure oxide. 

Reduction of Aluminum Oxide. Inasmuch as aluminum compounds cannot be directly reduced by carbon, sodium was formerly used to reduce the chloride, since this active metal will give up its electrons more freely than will aluminum. 

AlCl33Na Al + 3NaC1
