MOLTEN ALUMINUM 

Electrolysis of Aluminum Oxide

Aluminum produced by this process was too expensive for general use. Its desirable properties induced a search for a cheaper method, which was invented by a young chemist, Charles M. Hall. 

The electrolytic process invented by Hall uses cryolite, an easily fused fluoride of aluminum, as a solvent for the bauxite. 

An oblong box of cast iron forms the cathode which is lined with carbon. Rows of carbon rods form the anode. An electric current is passed through the cryolite. The heat developed by resistance to the current causes the cryolite to melt. The powdered bauxite is then added.

The aluminum ions move to the cathode to take on electrons and thus become atoms and molecules of metal, which sink to the bottom of the box. The molten metal is then allowed to run off into molds.

The oxygen ions come in contact with the carbon anodes, bebecome atoms, and combine with the carbon to form carbon monoxide. 2 Al2O3 2C + O2 

4 Al + 302 2CO 

Properties and Uses cf Aluminum. The striking property of aluminum is its light weight as compared with other common metals. It has about one-third the weight of iron. It is an excellent conductor of heat and electricity; it is exceeded as a conductor only by silver and copper. Its silvery white luster dulls slightly when exposed to air as a result of the formation of a protective oxide. It can be rolled into sheets and pulled into wire. 

Aluminum ware is well-adapted for cooking since it can be pressed into various utensils, conducts heat easily, does not tarnish, and is not attacked by vegetable acids. 

Electric transmission lines of aluminum are now common since weight for weight it is a better conductor of high tension electricity than copper.

Aluminum foil is used to cover perishable food products and aluminum powder finds use in paints.

Light aluminum alloys are much used in the automotive and airplane industries. Magnalium, containing 5% aluminum, has a high tensile strength. Duraluminum is used extensively in airplanes because of its light weight and great strength. For new uses of aluminum, see Chapter 25.

Thermite. Since aluminum gives up electrons more readily than iron, it will reduce iron oxide. Black or red iron oxide when mixed with aluminum powder is known as thermite. 

When this mixture is ignited by means of a fuse of magnesium ribbon, oxygen combines with aluminum, iron is set free with the liberation of a great amount of heat. Fe2O3 + 2 Al →→ Al2O3 + 2Fe Thermite is very useful for welding. A mold is placed around the parts to be welded and a crucible for the thermite is fixed above

the mold. When the thermite reacts, the intensely heated iron collects at the bottom of the crucible and then is allowed to run into the mold to harden. 

Aluminum Compounds. Aluminum has several important compounds. Pure aluminum oxide is prepared from the bauxite ore for electrolysis. Corundum and emery are hard forms of aluminum oxide used as abrasives. Rubies and sapphires are crystallized aluminum oxide which may now be made synthetically. 

Aluminum hydroxide is a thick gelatinous precipitate formed by adding a base to an aluminum salt. AlCl3 +3NH4OH → A1(OH)3 ↓ + 3NH4C1 Aluminum hydroxide when precipitated in the fibers of a cloth will hold a dye "fast" so the color will not wash out. A substance which will make a dye hold to the fiber of a cloth is called a mordant. The mordant and dye together are called a "lake" in the process of dyeing cloth (see page 237).

Water purification is carried out with aluminum hydroxide. 

Calcium hydroxide and aluminum sulphate are added to the water in regulated amounts. 3Ca(OH)2 + Al2(SO4)3 3CaSO4 + 2A1(OH)3 ↓ In water containing calcium bicarbonate the aluminum sulphate alone may be added. Aluminum carbonate formed immediately hydrolyses to aluminum hydroxide. The bulky precipitate heavier than water settles to the bottom and carries suspended matter and bacteria with it. 

If solutions of aluminum sulphate and potassium sulphate are mixed and then evaporated crystals of a new compound form, K2SO4. Al2(SO4)3.24H2O. This double salt is called an alum. To form other alums, sodium or the ammonium radical may be substituted for potassium and iron or chromium in place of the aluminum.

OTHER METALS 

Metallurgy of Zinc. There are three principal ores of zinc, the oxide, sulphide, and carbonate. The last two ores need to be changed into oxides before they can be reduced to zinc. Zinc sulphide and carbonate are heated (roasted) on grates over which air is passed. In this process, the sulphur is oxidized to sulphur

dioxide and the zinc becomes an oxide. Zinc carbonate is decomposed into zinc oxide and carbon dioxide. 2ZnS+3022ZnO +2SO2 ↑ ZnCO3 ZnO + CO2 ↑ Carbon is the reducing agent used to change zinc oxide to metallic zinc. Powdered mixtures of zinc oxide and coke are placed in earthenware retorts. These retorts are set in an inclined position on each side of the furnace with their necks dipping downward. 

The action is shown by the equation: ZnO + C →→→ Zn + CO ↑ Since the heat of the furnace is above the boiling point of zinc, the metallic vapor passes off and is condensed in iron receivers. 

Forms and Uses of Zinc. Zinc is usually cast into heavy bars called spelter. These have a coarse crystalline structure of a bluishwhite color. Sheet zinc has to be rolled at 150° C, as the metal is brittle at the usual temperatures. At higher temperatures it again loses its malleability.

If zinc is melted and poured slowly into water, the drops of zinc cool into the irregular shapes of granulated zinc. The large surface of this form makes it useful for acids and alkalis to react with the metal. Zinc dust, another laboratory form, is produced when the metal vaporizes and condenses quickly into a solid form (as on the cold sides of the receiver when the zinc furnace is first put in operation).

Cast zinc plates were formerly used as the negative plates of electric cells. The outer cases of dry cells are made of zinc. Sheet zinc may be used to cover roofs and as lining for tanks. The less expensive galvanized iron has a wider application. 

Zinc is mixed with many other metals to form useful alloys, such as brass, bronze, and German silver. 

Zinc Oxide and Zinc Sulphate. Zinc oxide (ZnO) is known as zinc white. It is formed when zinc dust is burned in air. The white powder is an important paint base. Zinc sulphate, ZnSO4.7H2O, is called white vitriol, which is used in electroplating with zinc. 

Silver. Silver, one of the precious metals, occurs in the native state and as a sulphide. The refining processes usually separate silver from lead, with which it is alloyed. Silver can be separated

from gold by dissolving the silver in nitric acid. Silver, a white metal, is very ductile and malleable. Silver is the best conductor of heat and electricity of all the metals. It blackens in air because of the presence of sulphur gases.

Silver is too soft for most uses so that it is alloyed with copper for coins or jewelry. Sterling silver is composed of 92.5 parts silver and 7.5 parts copper. The tarnish on silver may be removed with a fine powder, such as face powder. Many silver polishes also contain solvents, such as ammonia or sodium cyanide. The latter is a deadly poison and should not be used in the home. Tarnished silver may be easily cleaned by placing it in an enameled dish containing a solution of baking soda and a plate of aluminum. 

The solution is heated and an electric cell is set up between the aluminum and silver. Bubbles of hydrogen are given off on the surface of the silver to combine with the sulphide. Silver-plated ware is made by placing the metal to be plated as a cathode in a bath of silver cyanide. Pure silver is used as the anode. A low voltage current is used. The positive silver ions on reaching the cathode take electrons from it, become atoms, and are deposited on it.

Silver nitrate (lunar caustic, AgNO3) is formed by the action of silver with dilute nitric acid. It is used to make other silver compounds, especially the important ones used in photography. 

As a caustic, silver nitrate is used to cauterize wounds. 

Gold. Gold occurs free to a large extent. It is dissolved from the powdered ores by means of sodium cyanide and then recovered by replacement with zinc. It is a soft, heavy, yellow metal, being the most ductile and malleable of all the metals. Gold is principally used for jewelry and for coinage. Gold leaf may be hammered out so thin that it will transmit light. Gold is alloyed with many other metals to form red (copper), white (silver and nickel), and green (copper and silver) gold. Pure gold is known as 24 carat (a carat is the weight of 3.2 grains Troy weight). 

Mercury. Mercury is found combined with sulphur as cinnabar. On roasting, the sulphide is oxidized and the mercury passes off as a vapor.

HgSO2 →→→ Hg + SO2 

Mercury is a silvery liquid which can be frozen with dry ice.

It boils at 357° C to form a poisonous vapor. As a heavy, non-

volatile liquid it is used in barometers and thermometers. Mercury will dissolve free gold and silver. The gold and silver can be recovered by boiling off the mercury. Mercury vapor lamps are used in photographic studios. Mercury arc rectifiers are useful to change alternating to direct current. 

Platinum. Platinum is found alloyed with other metals, especially with iridium. It is a white metal harder than gold and very heavy. Its high melting point is its chief characteristic. Platinum is permanent in air and unaffected by the common acids. Platinum is used for crucibles and electrodes because of its high melting point and inertness. Platinum was once used for coinage and is now in favor for jewelry. Spongy platinum is the catalytic agent used in the contact process for sulphuric acid.

Lead. Galena, a sulphide, is the chief ore of lead. The roasting of the ore under proper conditions forms lead and sulphur dioxide. 

Lead is a soft, heavy, bluish white metal. Lead pipe is made by squeezing the metal through a die. Since lead has a low melting point and is permanent in air, electric cables are covered with it. 

Lead pipes are extensively used for drainage pipes. Storage battery plates are spongy lead. Lead enters into the composition of many alloys. Common solder contains 50% lead and 50% tin. Since it has a low melting point and flows easily, it is used to join sheet metals. A flux is used to clean chemically the parts to be joined. 

Type metal, composed of lead and antimony, expands when it is cast, so that it makes a sharp impression for the type. 

Tin. Tin ore is chiefly an oxide, cassiterite. This oxide is easily reduced with carbon.

SnO2 + 2C → Sn + 2CO Tin a white, lustrous metal. It has a crystalline structure and melts easily. It does not corrode in air and is not affected by weak acids. Tin has been used from ancient times in bronze alloys. Tin plate, which is sheet iron covered with tin, is made into cans for preservation of foods. Tin foil is wrapped around perishable products to retain their moisture content. Fusible metals containing tin, lead and bismuth have very low melting points. When used as plugs in automatic sprinkler systems, the fusible alloys melt when dangerous temperatures are reached and release water, which is held under pressure.

Arsenic. Occurs in arsenical pyrites (FeSAs) and as realgar (As2S2). A steel-gray, dully-metallic and crystalline element, with amphoteric properties, it burns in air and unites directly with the halogens, sulphur and with many metals. It is used for hardening lead for shot. All arsenic compounds are poisonous.

Antimony. Occurs free and as stibnite (Sb2S3). White, brittle, crystalline metal. Its alloys expand on solidification and it is therefore used in type metal.

Bismuth. Occurs free and as bismuth trioxide and trisulphide. 

Exceedingly brittle and lustrous metal. It is used for making fusible alloys in sprinkler systems (Wood's metal, for example, has a melting point of only 60° C.)

Magnesium. Occurs as magnesite (Mg CO3), dolomite (Mg 

CO3. Ca CO3), carnallite (Mg Cl2.KCl. 6 H2O) and is prepared by the electrolysis of carnallite. It is a silver-white metal, ductile when hot. It tarnishes in air and reacts slowly with water, rapidly with steam. It burns in air (forming MgO), emitting a very bright light especially valuable in photography. Magnesium is used in flashlight flares, Magnesium sulphate (Mg SO4. 7H2O) is known as epsom salts. Its oxides, carbonates, and citrate are used in medicines (citrate of magnesia). Magnesium is used extensively in alloys with aluminum.

Radium. Occurs in pitchblende and other uranium minerals. 

Radium was first isolated by Dr. and Madame Curie. In all of its compounds radium has the power of emitting electronic radiations. 

These radiations can pass through matter that is opaque to light, render air a conductor, and affect a photographic plate. This activity is called radioactivity and differs from chemical activity in that it concerns the internal structure of atoms, whereas chemical activity is concerned with the interaction of whole atoms. Radium emits three types of rays, ranging in penetrating force from alpha to beta to gamma rays. Gamma rays travel with the speed of light (186,000 miles per second) and can penetrate a piece of steel twelve inches thick. One gram of radium in any of its compounds gives off about 100 calories of heat per hour. A minute quantity of radium in a compound will provide a bright glow (this property is made use of in radium dials on watches). Although it was at first believed that radium possessed radioactive powers without

itself being affected, it is now known that radium slowly disintegrates into other substances and finally is transmuted into lead, where radioactivity ceases. The radioactive properties of radium were very valuable in explaining many of the phenomená in the electron theory of the construction of the atom (see Chapter 7, Electron Theory, and Chapter 25, Today's Chemistry).

Radium is the most poisonous substance known. Its rays act destructively on living tissues and on bacteria. It is used extensively as a limited means of curbing cancer and tumor growths. 

Its use, however, should be restricted to only thoroughly experienced doctors, since inexperienced handling may cause serious bodily harm (radium burns).

CHAPTER 22 COLLOIDS 

What are Colloids? Colloidal chemistry deals with a type of mixture in which the size of the particles distributed throughout a medium is midway between those of a true solution and those of a suspension (see page 22). Colloids are associated with all conditions and changes in matter organic and inorganic so that they are an integral part of chemical processes.

As in a solution, a colloid has a dispersing medium or sol and a dispersed substance. The sol corresponds to the solvent in a solution and the dispersed substance to the solute. Based on the states of matter, there are eight conditions of dispersion of one substance in another. Examples of these are found in the table. Sol 

Dispersed Substances 

Liquid Solid muddy water

Liquid Gas milk foamite starch paste mayonnaise froth Solid alloys gelatin pumice glass jellies gas in charcoal Gas dust, smoke fog fine snow no colloidal particles Preparation of Colloids. Coarse materials may be reduced to a colloidal state by grinding, spraying or beating. Molecules may be made to adhere to form larger colloidal particles. 

Examples: Water sprayed into the air by an atomizer forms a mist. Purple colloidal gold will form under water between gold electrodes. The particles are torn off by the electric current. Olive oil may be beaten into an emulsion in the presence of white of egg.

Properties of Colloids. Colloids possess characteristic properties which serve to identify them. 217

1. Cloudiness. Colloids show a cloudiness when held up to the light, indicating insoluble particles. 

2. Tyndall Effect. A beam of light passing through a colloid can be traced by the light reflected from the particles. This light reflection is called the Tyndall effect. A true solution remains dark when light passes through it. 

3. Brownian Movement. Under an ultra-microscope the particles of a colloid appear to move back and forth in irregular paths. 

This irregular movement is known as the Brownian movement. 

The Brownian movement is caused by the impacts of molecules of the dispersing medium upon the colloidal particles. 

4. Catalytic Action. The fine particles of colloids taken collectively have large surfaces. When condensation of gas or vapor occurs on these surfaces it is called adsorption. Powdered charcoal is used in gas masks to adsorb harmful gases. Sand in water filters and adsorbs bacteria from the water. Colloids tend to adsorb particles of unlike charge with an increasing action for ions of higher valence. Chemical action is always accelerated with an increase in the surface area of the reacting substances. Hence, colloidal conditions increase the activity of elements. Elements adsorbed on the surface of a colloid react readily, so that the colloid is a promotor of chemical action or a catalytic agent. 

Colloidal platinum is used in the contact process for sulphuric acid. 

Finely divided nickel is the catalyst for hydrogenation of oils. 

5. Emulsions. Emulsions are suspensions of one liquid in another. To keep the drops of one liquid separated, it is often necessary to add another substance to surround them and this keeps them apart. Oil shaken in water will come to the top on standing, due to the higher surface tension of the water, which pulls the water together. When soap is added to oil and water it keeps the oil finely divided inasmuch as the surface tension of the water is lessened. The soap is an emulsifying agent. 

6. Adsorption of Water. Some colloids will adsorb water and swell up as a sponge. Gelatins may contain 90% of water. 

7. Coagulation. Hardening or coagulation is a property of other colloids. White of egg hardens when heated. 

8. Electrically Charged. The particles of a colloid are all either charged negatively or positively. Because of the large surfaces

COLLOIDS 

219 exposed where electrical charges reside, colloids are highly charged electrically. Colloids may be precipitated from liquids by adding an electrolyte with an opposite charge. The neutralized colloidal particles then tend to collect in larger masses. Ferric hydroxide is positively charged. If sodium hydroxide is added, negative (OH)~ ions are introduced. The ferric hydroxide is neutralized and precipitated. Aluminum sulphate Al2 (SO4)3 will form aluminum hydroxide in water if a base is added. The gelatinous precipitate settles out taking impurities with it. Soil particles suspended in the muddy water of rivers are precipitated at the mouths of rivers to form deltas. The electrolytes in the salty sea water neutralize the charges on the fine particles to precipitate them. 

In the Cottrell System smoke and dust are taken out of furnace gases by passing them through chambers containing highly charged plates to precipitate the particles on the walls. 

9. Dialysis. The particles of colloids are too large to pass freely through parchment paper. On the other hand, the molecules of crystals in solution pass through membranes quite readily. This provides us with a method of separating true solutions from colloids which is called dialysis. 

Everyday Uses of Colloids

Foods. Foods furnish us with many examples of colloidal processes. Roasting meat coagulates albumin. The lactic acid found in sour milk curdles the casein. Gels are formed when water is added to powdered gelatine. Digestion is a chemical process promoted by various colloidal enzymes. 

Clothing and Leather. Clothing and leather are included in colloidal chemistry. Fibres adsorb dyes to hold attractive colors. 

Mordants are colloids precipitated on fibres of cloth to hold dyes fast. Rayon is made by precipitating a viscous liquid cellulose in an acid bath after it is drawn into fine threads. Leather results from preserving hides of animals by soaking them in water and then coagulating the gelatine with tannic acid or chrome alum. 

Cement and Glass. The setting of mortar and Portland cement is first a colloidal process to form gels. Later chemical action and crystallization may complete the hardening. Common glass is a colloidal mixture of sodium and calcium silicates. 

Metallurgy. Mining and metallurgical industries have many applications of colloidal properties. In the flotation process to

concentrate ores of lead, silver, zinc, etc., a froth of oil and ore is produced that will float on the surface of water while the rocky material will sink to the bottom of the tank. 

Fuel. Powdered coal is mixed with oil and is fed into furnaces as a liquid fuel. Such a mixture is cheaper and safer to use than oil alone. Vegetable matter in soils acts as a protective colloid to retain fertilizing materials.

CHAPTER 23  

In this chapter there will be discussed the chemistry of several classes of products which have not been mentioned in detail elsewhere in the text. These products and processes are of basic importance in everyday life and include fertilizers, cements, pottery, paints, glass, photographs, and inks.

Fertilizers. Plants chiefly draw potassium, calcium, sulphur, phosphorus, and nitrogen from the soil. These elements are naturally provided through the weathering of minerals and the decay of vegetation. When lands are cultivated, these supplies become exhausted and gradually the soil loses its productivity. Artificial fertilizers are now used to supply these necessary elements and thus keep up the fertility. Soils rich in one substance may be deficient in another so that one type of fertilizer may be added to supply the deficiency. The three types are commonly named from the element which is predominant in the analysis. 

Potassium Fertilizers. These have their chief source in carnallite, which is obtained from the Stassfurt mines of Germany. Potash is used as a fertilizer in growing cotton, potatoes, fruits, and garden truck. In the World War the chief source of potassium was cut off, so that potash sources in the Western part of the United States were developed, but the cost of production is too expensive in normal times to compete with the European product. 

Phosphorus Fertilizers. The United States has large deposits of phosphate rock in South Carolina and Florida. This rock phosphate is largely derived from the skeletons of ancient marine animals. The insoluble rock is treated with sulphuric acid to change it to soluble acid phosphate and calcium sulphate.

Ca3 (PO4)2 + 2H2SO4 - Ca (H2PO4)2 + 2CaSO4 The mixture of both products, called superphosphate, supply both phosphorus and sulphur. Yellow phosphorus is prepared in an electric furnace from a mixture of rock phosphate, sand, and coke. 222

Phosphoric acid (H3 PO4) and disodium phosphate (Na2HPO4) used in medicine, are also prepared from insoluble calcium phosphate (Caз (PO4)2). 

Nitrogen Fertilizers. Nitrogen is needed by plants to form protein matter. As most plants cannot take it directly from the air it must be absorbed through the roots in ammonia or nitrate compounds. Nitrogen may be supplied from organic sources, natural nitrates, or from artificial compounds. Waste animal matter from slaughter houses and fish canning plants supply products containing about 10% nitrogen. Crude Chile saltpeter, NaNO3, furnishes the major part of combined nitrogen. As the supply in these beds is limited, fixation of nitrogen from the air has been developed. 

Natural fixation of nitrogen is caused by bacteria on the roots of some leguminous plants as peas, beans, alfalfa. The tubercles on the roots of these plants have the ability to combine the nitrogen of the air with other elements. Rotation of crops which includes nitrogen fixing plants will keep up the nitrogen content of the soil. 

Artificial fixation of nitrogen is carried out commercially by several processes. (See Chapters 12 and 13.)

Building Materials. The use of modern building materials includes cement, brick, tiles, and glass. The raw products from which they are made are chiefly clay, limestone and sand. Clay is an impure aluminum silicate formed by the weathering of feldspar. 

Limestone is chiefly calcium carbonate. Sand, silicon dioxide, occurs in deposits of weathered rocks which has been sorted and sifted by the action of water. 

Portland cement is made by grinding a mixture of clay and limestone together in a definite proportion. This mixture is burned in a long rotary kiln to drive off moisture and carbon dioxide and finally to form anhydrous silicates of aluminum and calcium. After cooling, the clinker is ground to a fine powder. Portland cement is our most useful artificial stone. It is mixed with water and sand to form a mortar. Cement, water, sand and crushed rock are poured into molds to harden as concrete. Reinforced concrete has iron rods or wire throughout the mass to give strength. On standing, the plastic mass hardens into an artificial stone, even under water. Today our roads, bridges, dams, and buildings are examples of what modern engineers can do with this building material. 

Cement sets first as a gel of calcium hydroxide and silica acid.

This process takes only a few hours. The hardening of cement is a slow process of several months with a reaction between the water and aluminates and silicates to form crystals bound together in a fine mass. Recently, by a process of double firing, a quick-hardening cement has been produced so that emergency repairs to concrete structures may be completed in a few hours. 

Clay was one of man's first building materials. After molding and drying in air, clay bricks are fired in kilns. Loss of water occurs and a crystallized aluminum silicate forms to harden the mass. The reddish color of bricks, tiles and pottery is due to the iron oxide in the clay. Stoneware, porcelain and china are made from white clays. The hard dense structure of porcelain and china is due to the mixing of feldspar and quartz with the kaolin. Table wares are molded or formed on the potter's wheel, then fired in kilns. After the first firing, the unglazed ware is dipped or sprayed with a mixture of feldspar and quartz and fired again at a temperature which fuses the glaze. Decorations may be made before or after the glaze is formed.

Glass. If sand, SiO2, is fused with sodium carbonate, sodium silicate is formed.

Na2CO3 + SiO2 Na2SiO3 + CO2 ↑ This silicate hardens to a clear substance like glass. With water it forms a thick solution, which is called water glass. This soluble glass is used in fireproofing preparations. Eggs keep well in a solution of water glass, as it tends to keep out the air. Since it does not crystallize, it is a colloidal gel. 

Common glass is a mixture of sodium and calcium silicates. In glass-making, sodium carbonate, calcium carbonate and sand are melted in tank furnaces fired by gas or oil.

Since glass is non-crystalline, it has a plastic state in which it is worked into different shapes. Bottles are made by blowing a bubble of the glass to fit a mold. Window glass is pulled out of the furnaces in one long sheet, passed through rollers and then annealed. Plate glass is first made in sheets by pouring molten glass on a table or run through rollers to make a sheet. The surfaces of plate glass are then ground and polished to form an even surface. Safety glass in automobiles consists of two sheets of plate glass with a layer of celluloid between them.

Bottle or window glass is called crown glass. It is a sodium calcium silicate. In flint glass, used for cut-glass and lenses, potassium and lead are combined in a silicate. This glass is soft, easily ground, and has a high refractive index. Glass containing boron, such as Pyrex, may be used for cooking utensils, since it withstands heat well. Colored glass is produced by the presence of colloidal metals. Iron produces green glass; copper, red; cobalt, blue.

Paints. Paints consist of an oil in which is suspended a metallic salt or oxide. The oil, or vehicle, has the property of hardening as it dries and causes the suspended part or base to adhere to a surface. Driers are added to paints to hasten the hardening of the oil while thinners or solvents make it spread freely. 

Vehicles. Oils suitable for paints must have the property of thickening to form a colloidal gel with the base. Linseed oil is the usual drying oil used as a vehicle in paints. When exposed to air, it absorbs oxygen to form a new compound of a resinous nature. 

Boiled linseed oil has a faster drying rate than raw oil as oxidizing agents are added to it. Turpentine is one of the best solvents or thinners for paints. It is distilled from the resins of pine trees. 

Paint Bases. The suspended part of a paint is the metallic base which gives the color and covering power. White lead is one of the most important. It is a basic carbonate (Pb(OH)2.2PbCO3). In the Old Dutch process, thin perforated pieces of lead are exposed to fumes of acetic acid. A basic lead acetate is gradually formed which in turn becomes a basic carbonate in the presence of decaying tan bark. White lead has excellent covering power but has a tendency to chalk and blackens when hydrogen sulphide is present in the air.

Zinc white (ZnO) forms when zinc powder is burned in air. It is whiter than white lead but does not cover so well. It does not blacken in air but tends to peel off.

To reduce the cost of paints, adulterants are often added, such as barium sulphate, kaolin or chalk. 

Pigments. Pigments are added to color paints, such as lampblack, black; hematite, venetian red; lead chromate, orange; chromium oxide, green; ferric ferrocyanide, Prussian blue.

Photography. Photography means literally to write by the action of light. The power of light to decompose chemicals has

been noticed in the case of hydrogen peroxide. Silver nitrate blackens under light and the silver halides are especially sensitive to it. 

Daguerre first made pictures by exposing metal plates coated with silver iodide to light in a camera. The picture was brought out by placing the exposed plate in mercury vapor, which combined with the silver of the decomposed silver iodide. Today, photography is a highly developed process with the use of fast-lens cameras and supersensitive films. The steps in producing a picture by an ordinary camera are as follows: 

1. Exposure. The roll of film is made of cellulose acetate, coated with a gelatine layer in which is suspended a precipitate of silver bromide. The film is placed in the camera and exposed to light as the lens is focused so that a real image is made on the film. 

Silver bromide is decomposed. 2AgBr 2Ag + Br2 2. Development. When the exposed film is viewed under red light in a dark room there is no apparent image. When developing the film, a reducing agent is used. This developer reduces the exposed parts of the film quickly, the unexposed very slowly. The effect is to bring out the image of the picture in the black of finely divided silver. 2AgBr+H22Ag + 2HBr Sodium carbonate is present in the developer to neutralize the acid. To control the amount of development, the film is washed and then fixed to dissolve the unexposed silver bromide. The fixer is sodium hyposulphite, Na2S2O3. The exposed black silver is left on the film wherever the light and developer have acted on it. 

When held up to the light, this film will be seen to be a negative, as the lightest portions of the picture are the darkest on the film. 

3. Printing. To print a positive picture, the negative is placed in contact with paper coated with gelatine and silver bromide or chloride. On exposure to light the silver salt is affected in the clear parts of film so that a reversed image is produced when the paper is developed and fixed. Prints are sometimes given a toning process to make more pleasing contrasts. The print is immersed in a solution of gold or platinum to replace the silver. Many prepared photographic papers are now self-toning. 

Blue prints are used to reproduce drawings and plans which are

made on tracing cloth or paper. The paper is coated with a mixture of potassium ferrocyanide and ferric ammonium citrate without exposure to light. The negative is the tracing with black lines which is placed over the sensitized paper. The light tends to decompose the ferric ammonium citrate. The reducing power of the citrate radical changes ferric ions to ferrous. When the print is washed, the potassium ferrocyanide reacts with the ferrous salt to form the deep color of Turnbull's blue; as this precipitate is insoluble it remains in the paper. In the parts where no reduction took place, the soluble salts are washed away and the tracing is shown in white lines.

Writing Inks. A very simple writing ink can be made by using a vegetable or organic dye, such as nigrosin, adding gum arabic to hold it in colloidal suspension and a few drops of carbolic acid as a preservative against mold.

The so-called blue-black or iron inks are the most familiar. 

The iron compound used is ferrous tannate, which is colorless until it changes on oxidation to black ferric tannate. A blue dye is added to give color at the time the ink is used, since the gradual blackening does not occur for a few hours. Stains from dye inks may be washed out with water but in the case of iron inks a different procedure is used. The insoluble ferric tannate may be reduced to a soluble ferrous compound with oxalic or citric acid, and then it may be washed out with water. In case the dye stain persists, Javelle water may be used to bleach out the stain on linen and cotton goods, if a trial shows that the original color of the cloth is not thus harmed. 

Special Inks. Printer's ink consists of lampblack suspended in a linseed oil varnish, thus making it insoluble in water. Many other colored pigments are used in printer's inks, such as orange lead, yellow lead chromate, Prussian blue, and chrome green.

Copying inks consist of an aniline dye, such as soluble blue, dissolved in water to which glycerine is added to prevent drying out too rapidly when the original copy is prepared. 

Invisible inks received unusual attention during the World War, when spy systems were active. Such inks need a developing process to bring out the writing. A dilute solution of cobalt chloride will be invisible when used as an ink, but will turn pale blue when exposed to heat.

Fugitive inks prevent tampering with bank checks, inasmuch as they wash out or blue easily. Cobalt sulphocyanate is one color so used.

Laundry inks contain silver nitrate mixed with ammonia, which darkens under the action of light to form a black coloring which does not wash out. Potassium cyanide will remove a stain of a silver ink.

CHAPTER 24  FOODS 

Foods in the Body. Foods which are taken into the body are changed chemically and absorbed to become a part of its structure or are burned by uniting with oxygen in the tissues to furnish heat and energy.,

An understanding of the composition of foods, the uses which the body has for them, and the proportion of each necessary to maintain health are some of the factors in the science of nutrition. 

A food is any substance which in the body builds tissues or helps to furnish heat or energy.

Classes of Foods. According to their general composition, food nutrients may be divided into carbohydrates, fats and oils, proteins, and mineral matter, and vitamins. Water is also vitally essential to the body. 

1. Carbohydrates are compounds of carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen in which the atoms of the last two elements are in the ratio of two to one, or the same as in water. The starches of potatoes and grains, cane and other sugars are the best examples of carbohydrate foods. These are mainly energy foods. 

2. Fats and oils also contain carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen. 

However, the amounts of carbon and hydrogen are relatively greater than in the carbohydrates. Fats and oils are found in butter, meat fats, and vegetable oils. They possess high heat and energy values, form protective tissues, and supply reserve foods when needed. 

3. Proteins are sometimes called nitrogenous foods. Their molecular structure is exceedingly complicated and contains carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, and small amounts of sulphur and phosphorus. Well known sources of proteins are lean meats, fish, white of egg, casein of milk, gluten of wheat and peas and beans. The body uses proteins to rebuild its tissues and in some circumstances to furnish energy. 230

4. The minerals of the body make up about 4% of its weight and are supplied through the various foods. Calcium, magnesium, and phosphorus furnish mineral matter for building the teeth, bones, and nerve tissues. Iron is found in the red corpuscles of the blood, where it acts as a carrier of oxygen. Iron is principally derived from green vegetables. Eggs and dairy products are rich in other minerals. Whole wheat bread is richer in phosphates than white bread, since the outer part of the grain contains the mineral matter.

Water, although it does not give any energy to the body, is essential to the body processes of digestion, absorption, and assimilation. 

Chemical Tests for Foods. The presence of starch in foods is shown by the blue-black color found when an iodine solution is added to cooked starch. This test is very sensitive but heating will destroy the color.

The reducing sugars glucose and fructose form a brick-red precipitate when heated with Fehling's solution. Cane sugar will not respond to this test.

When concentrated nitric acid is added to a protein (such as white of egg) a yellow color is formed. If the acid is poured off, the food washed, and then ammonia added, the yellow color deepens. 

Fats in foods can be detected by extracting them with ether, or by the grease spot that is formed when they are warmed on glazed paper.

Mineral matter is left if a food substance is heated to ignition in a crucible.

Heat Values of Foods. The value of a food is generally measured in the number of heat units given off when it is completely burned. 

This unit is the large calorie, which is the amount of heat required to raise 1000 grams of water one degree centigrade.

The heat value of a food is determined by placing it in a bomb calorimeter, which is a strong metal case surrounded by water. 

The food within the case is burned and the heat given off is absorbed by the water surrounding the case. The calories gained by the water is then accurately measured and equals the calorie content of the food. The food oxidized in a human body is found by a metabolism test, which essentially measures the carbon dioxide produced under varying conditions of activity or rest.

Balanced Diets. It is evident that a diet which fully meets the requirements of the body must supply the proper number of heat units and also furnish materials for growth and repair. The energy requirements of a human being may vary from 2000 to 4000 calories a day. An average diet for an adult should contain about 300 calories of protein, and the rest of fat and carbohydrates in a ratio of one to eight respectively. Of course, any diet must also contain the proper amount and distribution of minerals and vitamins (see below) to maintain health.

Milk approaches the perfect food since it contains carbohydrates, fats, protein, minerals and vitamins, not only in proper proportions, but also in forms which are easily assimilated. 

COMPOSITION OF MILK

Vitamins. In 1906, Eijkman was able to show that a certain disease (similar to beri beri in humans) was produced in fowls by feeding them polished rice. This disease was quickly overcome when rice having the outer coverings was used. Scientists slowly began to prove that proteins, fats, carbohydrates, and mineral matter alone did not produce an ideal diet. The missing "something" necessary for health and growth in foods were called vitamins. As the exact chemical nature of vitamins was at first unknown, they were given letters of the alphabet to distinguish them in the effects produced.

White rats were fed various diets while the effects produced were carefully studied through several generations. A pure synthetic diet stunted growth. If codliver oil, containing vitamin A, was added, growth was rapid. Hence the presence of vitamin A promoted growth. Other vitamins were discovered and their effects carefully recorded. Now, the number of vitamins has been increased to nine or ten and their relations to each other have been found to be very intricate. Vitamins are complex organic compounds.

Digestive Enzymes. The digestive system is composed of a series of organs (the mouth, stomach, small and large intestines) in which the food is prepared by chemical changes for ultimate absorption into the blood. Each of these parts of the alimentary canal has a set of glands which secretes a digestive fluid having the power to dissolve or change a certain type of food. This power to break down foods into simpler substances depends on the presence of certain organic catalysts called enzymes. In the mouth, the salivary glands secrete the enzyme, ptyalin, which acts to change starch to grape sugar. In the stomach, the pepsin of the gastric juice attacks proteins to form simpler amino acids. The pancreatic juice of the small intestine furnishes three enzymes for starches, proteins, and fats. From the digestive tract the blood carries the food to the liver, where part is stored and the rest is oxidized for heat and energy or used to rebuild worn-out tissues. 

Hormones. The secretions of many other glands of the body and their chemical effects have been carefully studied. These secretions contain organic substances, known as hormones, which act catalytically in such a way as to affect the growth, health, mental activity, and the whole well-being of our body. 

Many of these hormones have been separated as crystalline chemicals and represent triumphs in the attempt of physiological chemistry to solve the mysteries of body processes. Some of the more important hormones are:

TEXTILES 

Introduction. The dried skins of animals served to protect primitive peoples from the chilling blasts of winter, while in tropical climates grasses and leaves were used to ward off the torrid rays of the sun. In time, the invention of the hand loom produced woven fibers which came from animals and plants, and textile fabrics became common. As man learned to color or dye cloth, our dress became enriched by ornamental effects and played a distinctive part in our life.

SOURCES OF TEXTILES 

Textile Cotton

Linen Silk Wool Rayon Asbestos 

Camel hair

Mohair Sheep Alpaca

Felt Source 

Plants Plants

Cellulose (artificial) 

Minerals (artificial)

Camel Angora goat Llama Rabbits

Cotton. Cotton is one of the world's chief agricultural crops. 

The United States furnishes over one-half of the total yearly supply of some thirty million bales.

Cotton fibers, seen under the microscope, are flat, twisted cells from a half inch to two and a half inches in length. Cotton is com-

posed of practically pure cellulose (C6H1005). Cotton may be washed with little care, since it is unaffected by the alkali in soaps. 

Mercerized cotton has a smooth luster and looks like silk. It is made by stretching cotton fibers in a solution of cold concentrated sodium hydroxide. 

Linen. Linen fibers come from flax stalks. (Linseed oil is made from the flaxseeds, just as cottonseed oil is made from cotton seeds.) The stalks are cut and allowed to soften in a damp place. SILK 

WOOL COTTON FIBERS 

Then the linen fibers are separated from the pulp of the stalks by drawing them through a comb of steel teeth. Linen fibers are coarse and stronger than cotton. Linen is a good conductor of heat and hence is suitable for hot weather clothing. Because of its high cost, linen is now supplanted by cotton and rayon for many uses. Since it launders well, keeps white, and has a luster, linen finds use for table cloths, napkins, and towels. 

Chemically, linen is a pure cellulose. Since linen absorbs water more readily than cotton, a water spot will spread more quickly

through linen cloth than cotton. A mixture of the two fibers is difficult to detect except by microscopic examination. 

Wool. Animal fibers from sheep are called wool. Australia is the chief wool-producing country, although the wool produced by sections of our western states devoted to sheep raising accounts for about 10% of the world supply. 

After shearing the sheep, the fleece is cleaned and treated with a solvent to remove the natural grease in it. The wool fibers are then spun into yarns and finally woven into cloth. The structure of wool fiber resembles a series of overlapping scales. The fiber curls up like a spring after stretching. Chemically, wool is a protein containing sulphur. When burned, it gives off an unpleasant odor characteristic of hair or feathers. Cotton and linen burn with a flame, but wool tends to char. Wool fibers are soluble in warm sodium hydroxide, but cotton and linen are not. 

Wool clothes are warmer than cotton ones because. the wool fibers intermesh better to hold a layer of air as an insulator. Wool also will hold a large amount of moisture without perceptible dampness.

The amount of wool in a cotton-wool mixture may be determined by first weighing the cloth and soaking it in a 5% sodium hydroxide solution, which dissolves the wool. The cloth is washed, dried, and weighed again. The loss represents the wool originally present in the goods. 

Artificial Wool from Milk. Fibers made from the casein of milk are now being used as a substitute for wool in several European countries.

The casein from milk is coagulated by an acid, washed, and dried. It is then dissolved by an alkali into a thick syrup and forced through minute openings into threads which are hardened as in the rayon process (see below). These new fibers, called lanital, are not as strong as wool, and absorb water more readily than the natural product.

Silk. Silk fiber is the outer structure of the cocoon of the silk worm. Silk is produced largely in China, Japan, France and Italy (where the silk worm feeds on the leaves of the mulberry tree). 

The United States is their largest customer for raw silk. The thread of silk is the hardened gelatinous liquid which is forced through small openings in the body of the worm as it spins its

cocoon. The cocoon is then unwound and silk threads, ready for the finishing touches, are produced.

The silk fiber is smooth, round, and very strong. Chemically, silk is a protein, but it does not contain sulphur. It burns with an odor that is similar to wool. Silk is soluble in strong hydrochloric acid (in contrast to rayon or cotton fibers). Chlorides adversely affect the strength of silk so that care should be taken not to allow silk clothing to come in contact with sea water or perspiration. 

Rayon. The high price of silk induced chemists to find cheaper substitutes. The rayon, or artificial silk, industry is now many times larger than the natural silk industry. Nearly all artificial silks are made from cellulose and thus resemble cotton in composition. In the most important process, the viscose process, the wood or cotton fibers are treated with a 17% solution of sodium hydroxide, dried, and then dissolved in carbon disulphide. This viscose solution, a thick syrupy mass, is forced through minute openings called spinnerets. As the fine threads emerge, they are set or hardened in a bath of sulphuric acid and sodium sulphate. The threads are treated to remove the chemicals, and then dried and made into yarn.

In the cellulose nitrate or acetate processes, the cotton fibers are first made into gels, squeezed through fine holes and coagulated into thread. Celanese is a cellulose acetate silk. 

Under the microscope, rayon fibers are smooth and solid. 

Rayon resembles cotton in its chemical properties. When wet, it is not as strong or elastic as natural silk, but because of its low cost it has hundreds of uses in clothing and ornamental goods. 

Dyes. Textiles are made more attractive by coloring them with dyes. Up to the middle of the nineteenth century natural dyes (animal, vegetable and mineral) were used exclusively for coloring. 

For example, the housewife of a century ago may have used log wood chips to color the rags for a rug black, ferrous sulphate to give the shades of yellow and cochineal (the bodies of insects) to give reds.

In 1856, William Perkins revolutionized the dyeing industry by discovering that aniline (C6H5(NH2), which is made from benzine, a coal tar distillate) could be used as a base for artificial dyes. 

Hundreds of new dyes have been built up from this basic chemical. 

Artificial indigo has replaced the vegetable dye of the same name.

Synthetic alizarin is now used for turkey red instead of the natural extract of the madder plant. 

Methods of Dyeing. To dye textile fibers so that the color will be even, of a given density and to be fast when exposed to light or when the material is washed, depends upon the properties of the dye and the chemical nature of the fibers. Natural silk and wool have the greatest affinity for dyes and absorb color better than cotton or artificial silk. 

There are three general methods of fixing colors in fibers: (1)

Direct dyes remain fixed in the fibers and do not run when washed. 

Picric acid is a direct dye for wool and congo red is one of the few direct dyes for cotton. (2) Insoluble dyeing is brought about by filling the fibers of a textile with a soluble dye and then, through oxidation, producing an insoluble compound. Indigo blue is produced in cotton cloth in this way. (3) Mordant dyeing is necessary when fibers have little absorbing properties, as is usually the case in cotton goods. The mordant, when precipitated in the fibers of a cloth, combines with the dye to form an insoluble compound or lake. Aluminum hydroxide, with alizarin dye, forms Turkey red; a tin mordant gives a pink color with the same dye.

CHAPTER 25 TODAY'S CHEMISTRY 

The Chemical Era. This, the final chapter in our study of chemistry, will discuss briefly the present place of chemistry in our industrial and scientific advancement. In contrast to the past years of steam and electrical power, the present years may be called the Chemical Era because of the ever-increasing importance of the chemical industry. Through years of economic depression this industry has steadily grown in the extended use of its products and financial earnings. The United States consumes nearly half the world's output of chemicals and more than three times that of Germany, its nearest competitor. Since the World War, research laboratories have been established in charge of skilled men who are foremost in their field. The latest processes of making dyes, of fixation of nitrogen and of synthetic substitutes are well established in America. 

Progress in Metals.

Steel. The automobile industry, with its increasing use of steel in car bodies, has developed a steel so completely deoxidized and purified that it can be rolled in sheets seventy-two inches wide. 

This steel is then stamped in huge presses and welded into a single unit. Structural and tool steels containing chromium and molybdenum are now produced with superior qualities at comparative low costs. 

Welding. Electric and arc welding are making advances, since the steels now in use are not changed in hardness or strength under the heat of the process. The atomic hydrogen arc may be mentioned here to show improvements in such processes. Hydrogen gas passes through an electric arc having tungsten electrodes. The heat of the air breaks down some of the hydrogen molecules to hydrogen atoms. The hydrogen atoms reunite to form molecules to burn with the oxygen at the tip. As the atoms recombine, the energy absorbed in the arc is liberated and this heat added to that of the burning molecules at the tip produces a temperature of 241

4000° C. This tiny flame is useful in cutting and welding metals. 

It has the advantage of high temperature and a reducing atmosphere to prevent oxidation of the metals welded. 

Aluminum. Since aluminum was produced by Hall fifty years ago its field of usefulness has been increasing. Aluminum girders are now rolled for structural work. External ornaments for building are made of this permanent, light metal. Airplanes have their framework and covering made of new magnesium-aluminum alloys, such as Dow metal, having high tensile strength. Colin G.

Fink, who perfected chromium plating, has recently coated sheet iron with aluminum. The surface of the iron is first treated with hot hydrogen to prevent the formation of an oxide of aluminum. 

Aluminum-plated iron may largely take the place of the more expensive tinned iron. A new anode process produces a film of oxide on aluminum which is resistant to weather and is absorbent to dyes and colors. The surface may be then sealed with a varnish. 

Automobile engine pistons treated with this process wear longer and have a better surface for lubrication. 

Plastic Substances. These have the property of being molded or pressed into desired shapes. Then, by means of heat or pressure in the presence of a catalyst, the plastic hardens to retain the form required. Molded products can be produced cheaply with extreme accuracy and through suitable color can be made attractive. Celluloid combs, rubber hot water bottles and bakelite electric switches are familiar objects in every home.

Celluloid. Celluloid is a mixture of nitro cellulose (C6H10O5(NO3)) and camphor dissolved in certain alcohols.

Celluloid may be colored and can be rolled, cut or pressed into any desired shape. Celluloid dissolved in butyl alcohol forms a lacquer for automobiles.

Rubber. Vulcanized rubber has been known for nearly a century, when this gum from a tropical tree was hardened by the addition of sulphur. The life of rubber goods has recently been increased by the addition of reducers to overcome oxidation and hardening. One new use of porous rubber is in cushions in furniture and auto buses. Liquid latex, mixed with sulphur, is first whipped to form minute bubbles. This foamy mass is poured into molds and hardened by a surrounding jacket of hot water. The air in the bubbles expands to form a light resilient cushion. Synthetic rubber

TODAY'S CHEMISTRY 

243 is now a reality. Natural rubber is a complex hydrocarbon with a formula similar to isoprene (C5Hs). Chemists have started with acetylene and have built up a multiple molecule of isoprene which has some distinct advantages over natural rubber. Germany recently announced that automobile tires made from synthetic rubber were in use in that country. 

Bakelite. Bakelite is a synthetic resin which has found many uses. Phenol (C6H5OH), or carbonic acid, is mixed with formaldehyde in the presence of an alkali. A gummy precipitate results which may be dissolved as a lacquer or molded by heating. 

Bakelite is resistant to chemicals, is an excellent electrical insulator and has good tensile strength. We are familiar with it in phonograph records, radio panels, pipe stems, molded cases and frames of ornamental articles, distributor heads on automobile engines, etc. Lacquers made from phenol resin now coat the inside of cans for many preserved foods. 

New Uses for Glass. Glass is continually finding new and varied uses. The abundant silica and lime from which it is made suggest glass as a building material. Translucent glass bricks are now produced for walls of buildings. Light is transmitted through the walls, making windows unnecessary. Tempered glass is a tough resistant glass made by cooling the surface quickly so that the unequal contraction places the surface under a tension. A sheet of this glass will bend and support considerable weight before breaking. When broken it crumbles in fine bits rather than forming dangerous pieces with cutting edges.

New Ways for Old. Practically all chemical products have undergone changes in the way in which they are made, as better and cheaper methods of producing them are discovered. At one time soaps were made from potassium carbonate or wood ashes. 

When the process of making soda ash from salt was perfected, hard sodium soaps replaced the semi-liquid potassium soaps. 

Synthetic indigo dye destroyed an occupation of many people and cut the price of the dye in half.

The abnormal demands of the World War stimulated manufacturers to develop synthetic processes for chemicals for which a scarcity existed. The Haber process produced synthetic ammonia for explosives after the stored stocks of Chile saltpeter were exhausted. Since that time several synthetic processes of great im-

portance have been introduced. Methanol (wood alcohol) and acetic acid are now synthesized. Synthetic fuels are rapidly gaining a place of great importance in our industrial world. 

Attack Upon the Atom. Recent investigations in the field of pure science have made progress comparable to, if not in advance of, those in industrial processes. Problems which have existed in scientific fields for years are approaching solution; theories have been rapidly modified or abandoned in the light of new discoveries. 

New Particles of Matter. In addition to electrons and protons, long accepted as the basic building stones of matter, the existence of new particles has been determined. The neutron, discovered in 

1932, is composed of an electron and a proton in close combination. 

Since the charges are neutralized, the particle is electrically neutral. 

It cannot be repelled by either positive or negative charges, and so has great power of penetration. The neutron serves as a valuable agent in bombarding the atomic nucleus. The positron is a particle equal in mass to the electron, but having a positive charge. Evidence at present seems to indicate the existence of a particle tentatively called the neutrino, or "little neutron", having the properties of the neutron but a lesser mass. This has not as yet been isolated. 

Transmutation and Radioactivity. Our study of the atom has shown that the nucleus is the heart of atomic structure; it is therefore logical to conclude that any change in the nucleus will result in the atomic structure itself changing. By bombarding the nucleus with particles driven at enormous velocities, efforts have been made to learn more about atomic structure and to bring about transmutations of matter. The particles used are neutrons or hydrogen ions; they are caused to move at high velocities by high voltage electrical discharges, or are accelerated by huge electromagnets, such as the "Cyclatron". Radioactive substances (see 

Radium, Chapter 21) will also attack the nucleus. By dislodging one proton from the aluminum nucleus, and the consequent release of one electron, magnesium has been formed. Similarly, nitrogen has been converted into carbon. In these changes the long-sought goal of the alchemists has been realized; not, indeed, in changing of common metals into gold, but in changing one element into another. 

Energy of Atoms. Over thirty years ago, Einstein stated that matter and energy may be converted one into the other, as proven

TODAY'S CHEMISTRY 

245 by the evolution of radiant energy (heat) during the combustion of fuel. One might then ask, "Would it be possible to convert the entire atom (by disintegration) into energy? If so, what quantity of energy could be obtained?" As yet, complete conversion of the atom into energy is not possible, although it may be taking place in the extremely high temperature of star interiors. Dr. Bazzoni, in Energy and Matter, has estimated that the energy derived from a piece of coal the size of a pea would be more than sufficient to drive an ocean liner across the Atlantic.

ACHESON, EDWARD (1856- ). American. Aided in the development of the electric furnace. Invented processes for the manufacture of artificial graphite and graphited lubricants.

ARRHENIUS, SVANTE (1859-1927). Swedish. Stated the theory of dissociation of electrolytes.

AVOGADRO, AMADEO (1776-1856). Italian. Stated the hypothesis of the relation of gas volumes and numbers of molecules contained in the volumes. 

BAEKELAND, LEO (1863- ). American (of Belgian birth). Prominent in the development of molded resinous plastics (Bakelite).

BERZELIUS, JOHANN (1779-1848). Swedish. Formulated the present system of element symbols. 247

BLACK, JOSEPH (1728-1799). Scottish. Proved the presence of carbon dioxide in air, also its evolution from heated carbonates. BOHR, NILS (1885about a nucleus.

). Danish. Stated the theory of electrons in orbits 

BUNSEN, ROBERT (1811-1899). German. Invented the burner named for him. 

Prominent in the field of spectroscopic analysis. 

CASTNER, HAMILTON (1857-1899). American. Inventor of the Castner cell for the production of sodium hydroxide by electrolysis of sodium chloride solution.

CAVENDISH, HENRY (1731-1810). English. Studied the chemical composition of water. Established the status of hydrogen as an element.

CURIE, MARIE (1867-1934). Polish. Discovered and isolated the element radium (with her husband Pierre as co-worker). Distinguished for researches in the field of radioactive substances. 

DALTON, JOHN (1766-1844). English. Stated the modern concept of the atomic theory, and the laws of definite and multiple proportions.

DAVY, SIR HUMPHRY (1778-1829). English. Prepared sodium and potassium by electrolysis. Studied the properties of nitrous oxide. Invented the miner's safety lamp.

FARADAY, MICHAEL (1791-1868). English. Discovered the process of lique fying gases by pressure.

Stated the laws of electro-chemical deposition of metals. 

FRASCH, HERMAN (1852-1914). American. Inventor of the Frasch process for extraction of sulphur. Also noted for development of processes for refining of petroleum.

HABER, FRITZ (1868-1934). German. Developed the process for the fixation of atmospheric nitrogen. 

HALL, CHARLES (1863-1914). American. Invented the electrolytic process of extraction of aluminum from a bauxite solution in fused cryolite. 

KEKULE, FRIEDRICH (1829-1896). German. Noted for research work with coal tar compounds. Originated the structural representation of benzene and other organic compounds.

LANGMUIR, IRVING (1881- 

). American. Coöperated in the development of the electron theory. Invented process of atomic hydrogen welding. 

Nobel prize-winner in chemistry-1932.

LAVOISIER, ANTOINE (1743-1794). French. Explained correctly the process of combustion, which disproved the phlogiston theory.

MENDELEJEFF, DIMITRI (1834-1907). Russian. Formulated the Periodic Table of arrangement of the elements, making possible the prediction of elements then unknown.

MOISSAN, HENRI (1852-1907). French. Developed the electric arc furnace, and carried out researches on reactions at high temperature. Isolated the element fluorine.

MOSELEY, HENRY (1887-1915). English. Established the table of atomic num bers of the elements, from the action of X-rays on the element.

RICHARDS, THEODORE (1868-1928). American. Noted for accurate work in determination of atomic weights.

NOBEL PRIZE WINNERS 

The Swedish scientist, Alfred B. Nobel, whose fortune was made by the invention of dynamite, established a fund of $9,000,000. The income from this fund is used as yearly awards to those who have contributed to the welfare of mankind. The prizes are worth more than $40,000 each. The physics and chemistry awards are made by the Swedish Academy of Science. Other fields for prizes are medicine, literature and peace. Langmuir, Urey and Richards are the Americans who have won the chemistry prize. Year

IMPORTANT DEFINITIONS AND CHEMICAL TERMS 

Abrasive. An element or compound of extreme hardness used for cutting or grinding less hard substances. Carborundum, diamond and emery are typical abrasives.

Acid. A chemical compound composed of positive hydrogen and a negative radical, yielding hydrogen ions in water solution. 

Adsorption. The condensation of a gas or vapor on the surface of a colloid. 

Amorphous lampblack adsorbs colors from raw sugar solution.

Alchemists. Chemical experimenters of medieval times who sought the 

Philosophers' Stone.

Alkali. A common term for a base or a basic salt. 

Allotropism. Property of elements to occur in two or more forms. Carbon occurs as graphite, diamond and lampblack.

Alloys. An alloy is a solid formed by mixing two or more metals when they are melted or liquefied. (For example, bronze is composed of copper and tin.)

Amalgams. Alloys of mercury and other metals. Silver will dissolve in liquid mercury to form silver amalgam. 

Amphoteric. The ability of an element to act either positive or negative with equal activity in compound formation. Examples are carbon and silicon.

Anhydride. A compound which unites with water to form an acid or base. 

SO is an acid anhydride; CaO, a basic anhydride. 

Anhydrous. A crystal from which the water has been removed. Heating crystals of copper sulphate removes the water of crystallization to form the anhydrous form.

APPENDIX 

251 Atom. The smallest unit of matter which enters into a chemical change. Atoms of sulphur and oxygen combine to form sulphur dioxide. 

Atomic number. A classification number for elements equal to the number of planetary electrons. Obtained from the X-ray examination of the element. 

Avogadro's Hypothesis. Equal volumes of all gases under the same conditions of temperature and pressure contain the same number of molecules. 

Base. A compound composed of a positive metal and negative hydroxyl group, containing OH ions in water solution. 

Boyle's Law. The volume of a gas varies inversely as the pressure upon it, temperature remaining constant. Doubling the pressure on a gas makes the volume one-half. 

Carbohydrates. A compound containing the elements carbon, hydrogen and oxygen, with the latter two in the ratio of 2:1. Starch (C6H10O5) and cane sugar, or sucrose (C12H22O11) are common carbohydrates. 

Catalyst. A substance which promotes a chemical process but which is unchanged in the reaction. Manganese dioxide is the catalyst used in the decomposition of potassium chlorate at lower temperature. 

Charles' Law. The volume of a gas, pressure remaining constant, varies directly as its absolute temperature. A gas expands 1/273 of its volume at 0° C for every degree Centigrade heated.

Chemical Change. Changes in the composition of substances. 

Colloids. Mixtures in which the particles distributed in a medium are smaller than those of a suspension but larger than those of a solution. Example: cooked starch in water.

Compound. A substance made up of two or more elements in definite proportion by weight. Water is formed of hydrogen and oxygen in the ratio of 

1:8 by weight. "Cracking". A process for increasing the yield of gasoline during the refining of petroleum. It involves the breaking down or "cracking" of larger oil molecules to form smaller molecules of gasoline. 

Crystals. Regular geometrical solids formed by cooling from liquid state or by evaporation from solutions. Common salt forms four-sided crystals from solutions. 

Definite Proportions (Law of). When elements combine to form compounds, they do so in a fixed ratio by weight. Hydrogen and oxygen combine in the ratio of 1:8 to form water.

Deliquescence. The absorbing of water vapor from the air by crystals until they become wet. Sodium hydroxide sticks will absorb water from the air until they dissolve. 

Destructive distillation. Heating of complex compounds without access to air, breaking down their molecular structure and forming other compounds. 

Soft coal, destructively distilled, yields coal gas, ammonia and coal tar. 

Developer. In photography-a reducing agent (such as hydroquinone) to bring out the exposed image.

Dialysis. The process of separating true solutions from colloids by a porous membrane. Test to distinguish solution from colloid.

Dissociation. The separating of an electrolyte, in water solution, into positively and negatively charged particles. Common salt dissociates into 

Nat and Cl.

Distillation. Evaporating a liquid and then condensing its vapor to remove impurities. Distilling water removes mineral matter.

Efflorescence. Loss of water of crystallization on exposure to air. Sodium carbonate crystals become an amorphous powder on standing in air. 

Electrolyte. A substance whose solution will conduct the electric current. 

Solutions of acids, bases and salts are usually electrolytes. 

Electromotive series. Order of activity of the metals; that is, iron is placed above copper, since iron ions will replace copper in solution. 

Electron. A negative electrical charge; one of the units of composition of the atom.

Electroplating. Coating a substance with a metallic coating from a solution by means of an electric current. Example: iron is nickel-plated to prevent rusting.

Element. A substance which cannot be broken down into simpler substances by chemical methods.

Enzymes. Organic catalytic agents which promote chemical processes in the body. The ptyalin of the saliva changes starch to soluble sugar. 

Ester. A compound formed (other than water) when an organic base reacts with an acid-may be classified chemically as a salt. Many foods, essential oils and flavoring materials are esters. 

Equation. A chemical statement to show a limited chemical change. 

Equivalent weight. Weight in grams of an element which will replace one gram of hydrogen in a reaction.

Fixation of Nitrogen. A chemical process of combining gaseous nitrogen with other elements, forming useful compounds, such as ammonia and nitric acid.

Fixer. In photography—a substance used to dissolve unexposed silver bromide in order to fix the image.

Flux. Part of a furnace charge (for example, limestone) added to combine with impurities in an ore.

Formula. A symbol or combination of symbols to represent a molecule. 

Hormones. Secretions of certain glands of the body which affect growth, health and mental activity. Thyroxin, secreted by the thyroid gland, controls the rate of metabolism.

Hydrocarbon. An organic compound composed of hydrogen and caroon, as methane (CH4) acetylene (C2H2).

Inert. Elements which exhibit no chemical activity, or do not combine with other elements. This state indicates a complete outer ring of electrons. 

Ionization. The separating of an electrolyte into charged particles, or ions. In some cases ionization may take place outside water solutions, as in fused hydroxides and salts and by passage of an electric spark through a gas.

APPENDIX 

253 Isotopes. Forms of an element which differ in atomic weight, although having similar properties. Nucleus different but same number of planetary electrons. 

Metallurgy. The process by which a metal is recovered from its ore and refined into usable forms. Iron ore is reduced to cast iron, then changed to steel. 

Metals. Elements (such as sodium) which give up electrons easily, have a luster, have positive valence and are good conductors of heat and electricity.

Mineral. An inorganic compound (such as silicon dioxide) which occurs in nature.

Mixture. Any substance whose components are not chemically combined. 

Air is a mixture of oxygen, nitrogen and other substances. 

Molecules. The smallest unit of matter which has the physical and chemical properties of the substance. A molecule of zinc or zinc oxide. 

Multiple Proportions (Law of). When elements form more than one compound, if the weight of the first is considered constant, the weights of the second are in the ratio of small whole numbers to each other. In water, H: 0=1:8; in H2O2, H: 0=1:16.

Non-Metals. Elements (such as the four halogen elements) having the tendency to take electrons from positive elements, as metals. Negative valence. 

Nucleus. The central mass of an atom, composed of electrons and protons. 

Ore. The natural compound or native element in which a metal occurs. 

Organic Chemistry. As commonly used, the chemistry of compounds of the element carbon.

Oxidizing agent. A substance which gives up oxygen in reaction. Manganese dioxide is used to oxidize hydrochloric acid. 

Paint base. The suspended particles of the paint to give it a body. White lead is a paint base.

Periodic classification of elements. An arrangement of the elements according to increasing atomic weights. Arranged in this order, the properties of the elements are periodically repeated. 

Physical change. Change in the physical properties of a substance but not in its composition.

Proton. A positively charged particle of electricity which, with the electron, is a unit of matter structure. 

Reducing agent. A substance which takes oxygen away from another substance in a reaction. Hydrogen will remove oxygen from copper oxide. 

Salt. A compound of a metal and an acid radical, forming one class of chemical compounds. Common salt, NaCl, is typical. 

Saturation. The state of a solution in which it will dissolve no more solute. 

If more solute is added and it does not dissolve the solution is saturated. 

Solute. A substance dissolved in another medium, as carbon dissolved in molten iron.

Solution. Uniform mixture of a liquid and solid which has no definite composition. Salt dissolved in water.

Solvent. A liquid which is used to dissolve another substance, as water is a solvent for ammonia gas.

Specific gravity. Weight of a liter of gas compared with the weight of an equal volume of air. Specific gravity of oxygen is 1.1.

Structural formula. An arrangement in which the atoms in the compound are shown in their relation to each other; valence is also indicated. Water, H-O-H, Sodium hydroxide, Na-O-H. 

Substitution products. Compounds formed by the actual or theoretical replacement of elements in a compound. Methane, CH4, has carbon tetrachloride (CCl4) as a substitution product.

Sublimation. The changing of a vapor to a solid but not passing through the liquid stage. Iodine vapor solidifies directly into crystals. 

Supersaturation. Unstable state of solution in which crystallization will take place until saturation is reached. A hot, saturated solution of potassium chlorate becomes supersaturated on cooling. 

Symbol. One or more letters, the first a capital, to represent an atom of an element. Cu-copper.

Thermite. A mixture of iron oxide and aluminum powder producing a high temperature through reduction of the oxide. Used in welding rails. 

Valence. The combining power of an atom or group of atoms (radical) as compared with an atom of hydrogen. Cu, valence 2, replaces two atoms of hydrogen in H2SO4.

Vapor density. The weight of a liter of a gas as compared with the weight of a liter of hydrogen. Vapor density of oxygen-16. 

Vehicle. Oil or liquid part of paint which has the property of hardeningusually linseed oil.

Vitamins. Organic chemical substances in foods necessary for health and growth. Vitamin C prevents scurvy.

H2SO4 

White precipitate, insoluble in acids 

H2S formed has odor of rotten eggs; lead solution turns black; cadmium, yellow 

Sharp, pungent odor of SO2 is noted; permanganate is bleached Metals Metals may be detected and identified by several methods: (1) color formed in Borax beads, (2) color when heated with cobalt nitrate, (3) color formed by reaction of metallic salt solutions with testing reagents, (4) color imparted to the Bunsen flame when compounds of metals are heated. 

1. Borax Bead Tests. Form a borax bead on a loop of platinum wire or at the end of a glass rod by first heating it and then touching it to borax, and again heating until a clear, glassy bead is formed. Dip the bead into the material to be tested, and heat strongly.

Color in Bead 

Metal Tested

Chromium Cobalt Manganese Nickel Iron

Dark-green Blue Purple-violet Brown Yellow 

2. Cobalt Nitrate Tests. Heat strongly (by means of a blowpipe) a compound of the metal on a charcoal or plaster block. Add a drop of cobalt nitrate solution, and again heat. 

Metal Tested

Magnesium Aluminum Zinc 

Color Appearance 

Pink

Blue Green 

3. Salt Solutions and Reagent. Hydrogen sulphide is a valuable testing agent for the determination of metals by the color of precipitates formed. 

Iron compounds-ferrous and ferric—are identified by cyanides. 

If H2S gas (or water solution) is added to metallic salt solutions, the following results are obtained. 

Salt Solution of Antimony Copper Cadmium

Lead Zinc 

Color of Precipitate Formed 

Orange Yellow 

Black-with dark-brown tinge

Black White 

Ferrous salt: Potassium ferricyanide forms a dark-blue precipitate (Turnbull's Blue).

Ferric salt: Potassium ferrocyanide forms a dark-blue precipitate (Prussian Blue). 

4. Flame Tests. Solutions of metallic salts when heated in the Bunsen flame impart to the flame a characteristic color by which the metal may be identified. A loop of platinum wire is thoroughly cleaned by dipping it in dilute HNO3 and heating it until the Bunsen flame appears colorless. The wire is then dipped into the solution to be tested, and held in the flame. 

Metallic Salt Solution

Bright red Yellow 

Deep red

Violet

APPENDIX 

265 If the potassium compound used is not perfectly pure, the yellow color from traces of sodium will obscure the violet color of the potassium. The student should view the flame through a cobalt glass; the yellow color will be absorbed, allowing the violet to be seen.

GENERAL RULES OF SOLUBILITY 

1. All nitrates, chlorates and acetates are soluble in water. 

2. All chlorides are soluble in water except those of silver, lead and mercury (mercurous). 

3.

All sulphates are soluble except those of barium, strontium and lead (CaSO4 only slightly soluble). 

4. All sodium, potassium and ammonium compounds are soluble in water. 

5. All hydroxides are insoluble except those of sodium, potassium, ammonium, calcium, and barium (the last two slightly soluble).

6. All sulfides, carbonates, phosphates and silicates are insoluble except those of sodium, potassium and ammonium. 

7. All oxides, which do not react with water are insoluble. (Sodium, potassium and calcium oxides react with water to form hydroxides.)
